\title{Critical Jumps for Rough Polynomial Carleson Operators\\
       with Angular Hardy Data and Finite Radial Variation}
\author{Tseng}
\date{October 23, 2026}
\begin{document}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
We prove critical-jump estimates for sharply truncated rough singular
integrals with polynomial phases. For mean-zero angular data in
$H^1(\mathbb S^{n-1})$, every $1<p<\infty$, and every fixed radial
amplitude of finite $\rho$-variation, $1\le\rho<\infty$, the polynomial
modulation supremum and the jump-amplitude supremum are both controlled
pointwise before taking the $L^p$ norm. The bound is linear in the
angular Hardy norm and the radial variation norm. For each fixed
polynomial phase, mean-zero $L\log L$ angular data also give a
coefficient-uniform weak type $(1,1)$ estimate for the pointwise
critical-jump functional.

The strong estimate combines a pointwise dyadic martingale inequality,
rough oscillatory correlations, and polynomial tile geometry. A uniform
conical estimate retains the elliptic cutoff and radial amplitude
created by affine normalization of spherical atoms. The weak endpoint
uses phase-adapted cancellation and packet estimates that remain uniform
under concentration of the bad data.

The estimates concern complete truncation trajectories and imply maximal
truncation, $r$-variation for every $r>2$, and principal-value convergence.
The angular Hardy hypothesis strictly extends the mean-zero $L\log L$
class in dimensions at least two. Counterexamples exclude unrestricted
bounded radial amplitudes and true quadratic output variation.
\end{abstract}
\medskip\noindent\textit{AI-use disclosure.}

the mathematical arguments and derivations were produced by those systems.

\section{Introduction and main results}\label{sec:introduction}

A maximal-truncation estimate controls only the largest excursion of an
operator trajectory.  An $r$-variation estimate with $r>2$ also quantifies
convergence, but the endpoint $r=2$ is generally inaccessible.  The
critical replacement is the pointwise $2$-jump functional
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-pointwise-jump}
 \mathbf J(F)(x)
 :=\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda N_\lambda(F(x))^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
The spatial norm is taken only after this pointwise supremum; in particular,
the endpoint quantity is
\[
 \norm{\mathbf J(F)}_{L^{1,\infty}}
 =\left\|\sup_{\lambda>0}
 \lambda N_\lambda(F)^{1/2}\right\|_{L^{1,\infty}}.
\]
The functional $\mathbf J(F)$ measures disjoint oscillations at every
amplitude and controls $r$-variation for every $r>2$. Its weak spatial
norm is stronger than the conventional mixed quasi-seminorm
$\sup_{\lambda>0}\|\lambda N_\lambda(F)^{1/2}\|_{L^{1,\infty}}$;
Proposition~\ref{prop:mixed-norm-counterexample} proves strictness using
finite jump paths.  We prove two complementary forms of this estimate: a strong
$L^p$ theorem with a pointwise supremum over all bounded-degree polynomial
phases, and a coefficient-uniform weak $(1,1)$ theorem for each fixed
phase. Both concern the complete continuous sharp-truncation trajectory.
The strong modulation theorem and the fixed-phase weak theorem both
admit every fixed finite radial variation exponent.
No nondegeneracy, smallness, or genericity condition is imposed on the
polynomial phase. In particular, the results include convolution phases,
genuinely nonconvolution phases, and mixed phases that vanish on the
diagonal.

The strong modulation theorem assumes mean-zero angular data in
$H^1(\mathbb S^{n-1})$. The fixed-phase weak endpoint retains the
mean-zero $L\log L$ assumption. The pointwise phase supremum includes linear phases and general even
angular components. Its proof combines polynomial tile geometry,
selector-dependent rough estimates, and a conical atomic reduction.
The complementary weak endpoint at $P=0$ strengthens the formulation
posed by Jones--Seeger--Wright \cite{JSW}; phase-adapted cancellation
extends it to every fixed polynomial phase.

\subsection{Notation and truncation trajectories}\label{intro:notation}
Let $d\sigma$ be the usual surface measure on $\Sph^{n-1}$.  Put
\[
K_\Omega(z)=\frac{\Omega(z/|z|)}{|z|^n},\qquad z\ne0.
\]
For $A=\norm\Omega_1>0$ define the homogeneous gauge
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauge}
 \mathfrak L(\Omega)
 =A+\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}|\Omega(\theta)|
 \log^+\!\left(\frac{|\Omega(\theta)|}{A}\right)d\sigma(\theta),
\end{equation}
and set $\mathfrak L(0)=0$.  This is equivalent, with dimensional constants,
to the homogeneous Luxemburg $L\log L$ norm.  Its homogeneity and the
boundedness of the odd and even projections
\[
 \Omega_o(\theta)=\frac{\Omega(\theta)-\Omega(-\theta)}2,
 \qquad
 \Omega_e(\theta)=\frac{\Omega(\theta)+\Omega(-\theta)}2
\]
give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauge-parity}
 \mathfrak L(\Omega_o)+\mathfrak L(\Omega_e)
 \le C\mathfrak L(\Omega).
\end{equation}
The elementary normalization details are recorded in
Lemma~\ref{lem:gauge-normalization}.

On bounded compactly supported inputs define
\[
 T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f(x)
 =\int_{|x-y|>\varepsilon}e^{iP(x,y)}K_\Omega(x-y)f(y)\,dy.
\]
For $L^p$ data with $1<p<\infty$, the simultaneous construction is given
in Sections~\ref{mod:atom:sec:tails}--\ref{mod:atom:sec:mainproof}.
The $L^1$ endpoint construction appears in Section~\ref{sec:limits}.
Both constructions are characterized by the finite-annulus identity and
convergence to zero as $\varepsilon\to\infty$.

Let $E$ be a linearly ordered set and let $F=(F_t)_{t\in E}$ be a scalar
path.  For $\lambda>0$, its paired strict jump number is
\[
N_{\lambda,E}(F):=
 \sup\bigl(\{0\}\cup\mathcal W_{\lambda,E}(F)\bigr)
 \in\mathbb N_0\cup\{\infty\},
\]
where $\mathcal W_{\lambda,E}(F)$ is the set of all $N\in\mathbb N$ for
which there are $s_i,t_i\in E$, $1\le i\le N$, satisfying
\[
 s_1<t_1\le s_2<t_2\le\cdots\le s_N<t_N,
 \qquad |F_{t_i}-F_{s_i}|>\lambda\quad(1\le i\le N).
\]
Thus a path with no strict $\lambda$-jump has jump number zero, while a path
with arbitrarily large finite witnesses has jump number infinity.
For $r>0$, its homogeneous $r$-variation is
\[
V_E^r(F):=\sup\Biggl(\{0\}\cup
 \Biggl\{\left(\sum_{i=1}^N|F_{t_i}-F_{t_{i-1}}|^r\right)^{1/r}:
 N\in\mathbb N,\quad t_0<\cdots<t_N,\quad t_i\in E\Biggr\}\Biggr).
\]
When the index set is displayed or unambiguous, we omit the subscript $E$;
thus the main positive-radius paths use $N_\lambda(F)$ and
$V^r(F_t:t>0)$.  For a restriction to an ordered subset $D\subset E$, put
\[
 \mathbf J_D(F):=\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda
 N_{\lambda,D}(F|_D)^{1/2},
\]
and omit $D$ when it is the full parameter set.
For a measurable function $H$, set
$\norm H_{L^{1,\infty}}=\sup_{\tau>0}\tau|\{x:|H(x)|>\tau\}|$.
Call a path coordinatewise measurable if every $F_t$ is measurable in the
spatial variable.  For such a path define $\mathbf J(F)$ pointwise by
\eqref{eq:intro-pointwise-jump}, initially as an extended-valued pointwise
functional without a separate measurability assertion.  We call the path
jump-separable if there are a countable parameter set $D\subset E$ and one common
full-measure spatial set on which, for every $\lambda>0$, its full and
$D$-restricted jump numbers agree.
Whenever $\mathbf J(F)$ is measurable---in particular, for every
jump-separable coordinatewise measurable path---and $1<p<\infty$, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:inside-jump-norms}
 \Jop{p}(F)=\norm{\mathbf J(F)}_p
 =\left\|\sup_{\lambda>0}
 \lambda N_\lambda(F)^{1/2}\right\|_p,
 \qquad
 \Jop{1,\infty}(F)=\norm{\mathbf J(F)}_{L^{1,\infty}}.
\end{equation}
Because all jumps are strict, the supremum in $\lambda$ may be restricted
to $\mathbb Q_+$ without changing $\mathbf J(F)$.  More precisely, if
$D\subset E$ is countable and the coordinates $F_t$ are measurable
for $t\in D$, then, for every $N\ge1$,
\[
 \{N_\lambda(F_t:t\in D)\ge N\}
 =\bigcup_{(s_i,t_i)_{i=1}^N\in\mathcal O_N(D)}
   \bigcap_{i=1}^N\{|F_{t_i}-F_{s_i}|>\lambda\},
\]
where $\mathcal O_N(D)$ is the countable set of ordered paired
$2N$-tuples in $D$.  Thus the jump count on $D$ is measurable, and every
jump-separable coordinatewise measurable path has a measurable pointwise
jump functional.  If the
path is locally continuous in the truncation parameter, its jump counts on
a dense $D$ agree with those on all positive parameters by
Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-witness}.  These observations and the rational
$\lambda$-supremum prove measurability of $\mathbf J(F)$.

We use the following common-representative convention throughout.  Whenever
one of the continuum-parameter operator trajectories constructed below is
asserted pointwise on the full parameter interval, its construction is
carried out on one spatial set $X_0$ of full measure, independent of the
parameter, on which the parameter path is locally continuous (or locally
absolutely continuous when that stronger property is used) and all stated
finite-annulus identities hold simultaneously.  All coordinates are set
equal to zero on $X_0^c$.  Thus a countable dense parameter set determines
all jumps and variations by Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-witness}, and the preceding
countable-union argument proves their measurability.  This convention does
not impose continuity on the abstract scalar paths used in the jump lemmas.
We do not pass from coordinatewise almost-everywhere identities at
uncountably many parameters to a common operator representative without an
explicit finite-witness or anchored uniform-Cauchy argument.  Joint
measurability in the parameter is stated separately whenever Fubini or a
parameter integral requires it.

Throughout, \(C\) and \(c\) may change from line to line.  Every constant
in a global theorem or corollary depends only on the parameters displayed
there; in particular, it is independent of the support and location of the
input and of every approximation used to construct the operator.  Constants
inside proofs may depend on fixed smooth cutoffs and normalized coordinate
charts chosen once and for all.  Only an explicitly localized statement may
depend on support or chart data named in that statement.  No constant depends
on the coefficients of \(P\), on truncation or dyadic scales, or on auxiliary
finite cutoffs.  Every coefficient norm is a fixed Euclidean norm on the
relevant finite-dimensional polynomial space; changing that norm changes
only the permitted structural constants.

\paragraph{The angular Hardy space.}
For $n\ge2$, $H^1(\mathbb S^{n-1})$ denotes the real atomic Hardy
space on the sphere, with complex-valued functions permitted. Write
$m_n=\sigma(\mathbb S^{n-1})$. Its atoms are the constant atom
$a_*=m_n^{-1}$ and the cancellative atoms $a$ satisfying
\[
 \operatorname{supp}a\subset B,\qquad
 \int a\,d\sigma=0,\qquad
 \|a\|_\infty\le\sigma(B)^{-1},
\]
where $B$ is a geodesic ball or the whole sphere. The norm is
\[
 \|\Omega\|_{H^1(\mathbb S^{n-1})}
 =\inf\left\{\sum_\nu|\lambda_\nu|:
       \Omega=\sum_\nu\lambda_\nu a_\nu\text{ in }L^1\right\}.
\]
The series are absolutely summable in $L^1$. This is the usual compact
real Hardy space; the atomic formulation and the small-cap formulation
used below are related in Section~\ref{mod:atom:sec:atoms}.
We write $H^1_0=\{\Omega\in H^1:\int\Omega\,d\sigma=0\}$.
For $n=1$, the sphere consists of two points; we use its finite-dimensional
$L^1$ norm as the Hardy norm and impose the same mean-zero condition.
The variable in this Hardy space is the angular variable. Spatial
inputs in the main strong theorem belong to $L^p(\mathbb R^n)$.

For $1\le\rho<\infty$ and a pointwise bounded radial function
$b:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb C$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:radial-variation-norm}
 \|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}
 :=\|b\|_\infty+V^\rho(b;(0,\infty)).
\end{equation}
A function of finite $\rho$-variation is regulated.  In
\eqref{eq:radial-variation-norm}, $\|b\|_\infty$ denotes the pointwise
supremum of the chosen representative, consistently with the pointwise
definition of $V^\rho$.  Whenever point values enter a Stieltjes formula,
we use the representative
\[
 b^+(t):=\lim_{s\downarrow t}b(s),\qquad t>0.
\]
Indeed, simultaneous approximation of the points of any finite partition
from the right gives
$V^\rho(b^+;(0,\infty))\le V^\rho(b;(0,\infty))$, and the same
right-limit argument gives $\|b^+\|_\infty\le\|b\|_\infty$.  To quantify
the exceptional point values, fix $\delta>0$ and distinct
$t_1<\cdots<t_N$ such that $|b(t_i)-b^+(t_i)|>\delta$.  Choose, successively
and sufficiently close to the right of each $t_i$, points
$s_i\in(t_i,t_{i+1})$ (with the evident convention for $i=N$) such that
$|b(s_i)-b^+(t_i)|<\delta/2$.  The ordered partition containing every
$t_i,s_i$ then gives
\[
 N(\delta/2)^\rho\le V^\rho(b;(0,\infty))^\rho.
\]
Thus there are only finitely many such points for each $\delta$, and
$b=b^+$ off a countable set.  Polar
coordinates then show that $b(|z|)=b^+(|z|)$ for almost every
$z\in\mathbb R^n$, so replacing $b$ by $b^+$ changes none of the
Lebesgue integral operators below and does not increase
$\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}$.
Initially for bounded compactly supported inputs define
\[
 T^{P,b}_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f(x)
 =\int_{|x-y|>\varepsilon}e^{iP(x,y)}b(|x-y|)
 K_\Omega(x-y)f(y)\,dy.
\]

\subsection{Principal results}\label{intro:results}
\begin{theorem}[Angular Hardy data and maximally modulated finite radial variation]
\label{mod:main}
Let $n\ge1$, $d\ge0$, $1<p<\infty$, and $1\le\rho<\infty$.
Let $\Omega\in H^1_0(\mathbb S^{n-1})$, and fix a radial function
$b:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb C$ with $\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}<\infty$.
For every $f\in L^p(\mathbb R^n)$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:target}
 \left\|\sup_{\deg P\le d}\sup_{\lambda>0}
  \lambda N_\lambda(T^{P,b}_{\Omega,t}f:t>0)^{1/2}\right\|_p
 \le C_{n,d,p,\rho}\|\Omega\|_{H^1(\mathbb S^{n-1})}
       \|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}\|f\|_p.
\end{equation}
Here $P$ ranges over all real polynomials on
$\mathbb R^n\times\mathbb R^n$ of total degree at most $d$.
For each selected trajectory, the same polynomial is used at every
truncation parameter. Both suprema in \eqref{mod:target} are pointwise
suprema inside the spatial norm. The amplitude $b$ is fixed; the
constant is uniform over $b$ subject to its displayed norm.

There is one full-measure set, independent of $P$ and $t>0$, on which
all positive truncations are absolutely convergent and depend jointly
continuously on $t$ and the polynomial coefficients. Their absolute
tails tend to zero as $t\to\infty$, uniformly over $P$.
\end{theorem}

Theorem~\ref{mod:main} uses the standard angular Hardy space defined
above. Its $L\log L$ predecessor follows from the continuous inclusion
$L\log L(\mathbb S^{n-1})\subset H^1(\mathbb S^{n-1})$, proved below.
For $n\ge2$ this inclusion is strict, including on the mean-zero
subspaces. The phase supremum is taken pointwise before the spatial norm. It includes the maximally truncated polynomial
Carleson operator: every finite-annulus integral is a single increment
of the trajectory. The resulting maximal-truncation, $r>2$ variation, and simultaneous
principal-value conclusions are collected in
Section~\ref{mod:consequences}.

The next theorem gives the complementary spatial endpoint. Its strong
estimate follows from Theorem~\ref{mod:main} with $b=1$ and the
$L\log L$ inclusion; Part~\ref{part:fixed}
also provides an independent fixed-phase proof, whose localized
estimates enter the weak-type and radial-amplitude arguments.

\begin{theorem}[Fixed-phase critical jumps and the weak endpoint]
\label{thm:main}
Let $n\in\mathbb N$ and $d\in\mathbb N_0$.  Let $P$ be a real polynomial on
$\R^n\times\R^n$ of total degree at most $d$, and let
$\Omega\in L\log L(\Sph^{n-1})$ satisfy $\int\Omega\,d\sigma=0$.
Then the pointwise critical-jump functional of the sharp-truncation
trajectory satisfies the coefficient-uniform estimates
\begin{align}
 \Jop{p}(T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:\varepsilon>0)
 &\le C_{n,d,p}\mathfrak L(\Omega)\norm f_p,
 &&1<p<\infty,\label{eq:main-strong-jump}\\
 \Jop{1,\infty}(T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:\varepsilon>0)
 &\le C_{n,d}\mathfrak L(\Omega)\norm f_1.
 \label{eq:main-weak-jump}
\end{align}
Estimate \eqref{eq:main-strong-jump} holds for every $1<p<\infty$ and
$f\in L^p(\R^n)$; estimate \eqref{eq:main-weak-jump} holds for every
$f\in L^1(\R^n)$.  The constants are independent of every coefficient of
$P$.

For each fixed $\Omega$ as above and each fixed $f$ in the data class of
the asserted estimate, Proposition~\ref{prop:strong-polynomial-jump} in the
strong case and Section~\ref{sec:limits} in the weak case construct the
entire trajectory on one full-measure set, on which all of its finite-annulus
identities hold and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:anchor-main}
 T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f(x)\longrightarrow0
 \qquad\text{as }\varepsilon\to\infty
\end{equation}
holds.  These two properties, rather than the estimates alone, determine
the trajectory uniquely: the finite-annulus identities make the difference
of two such representatives independent of $\varepsilon$ on that common
full-measure set, and the zero anchor makes this difference vanish.  When
$1<p<\infty$, the convergence in \eqref{eq:anchor-main} also holds in
$L^p(\mathbb R^n)$.
\end{theorem}

Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kappa-r}
 \kappa_r=\left(\sum_{k=1}^\infty k^{-r/2}\right)^{1/r}
 \le \left(\frac r{r-2}\right)^{1/r},\qquad r>2.
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[Variation and convergence consequences]
\label{cor:unweighted-consequences}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:main}, for every $r>2$,
\begin{align}
 \norm{V^r(T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:\varepsilon>0)}_p
 &\le C_{n,d,p}\kappa_r\mathfrak L(\Omega)\norm f_p,
 &&1<p<\infty,\label{eq:main-strong-var}\\
 \norm{V^r(T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:\varepsilon>0)}_{L^{1,\infty}}
 &\le C_{n,d}\kappa_r\mathfrak L(\Omega)\norm f_1.
 \label{eq:main-weak-var}
\end{align}
Here \eqref{eq:main-strong-var} holds for every $1<p<\infty$ and
$f\in L^p(\R^n)$, and \eqref{eq:main-weak-var} holds for every
$f\in L^1(\R^n)$.  The factor $\kappa_r$ is needed only for the
$V^r$ conclusions.  The zero anchor and
\eqref{eq:anchor-controls-maximal} give directly
\begin{align*}
 \left\|\sup_{\varepsilon>0}|T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f|\right\|_p
 &\le C_{n,d,p}\mathfrak L(\Omega)\norm f_p,
 &&1<p<\infty,\\
 \left\|\sup_{\varepsilon>0}|T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f|\right\|_{L^{1,\infty}}
 &\le C_{n,d}\mathfrak L(\Omega)\norm f_1,
\end{align*}
with no $\kappa_r$ loss.  The finiteness of $V^r$ implies that
$\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f$ exists almost
everywhere in the respective ranges.  Indeed, if a scalar path $F_\varepsilon$
with finite $V^r$ were not Cauchy as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, there would be
$\delta>0$ and, recursively, pairs
\[
 0<s_{k+1}<t_{k+1}<s_k<t_k,
 \qquad |F_{t_k}-F_{s_k}|>\delta .
\]
For every $N$, inserting the endpoints of the first $N$ pairs in increasing
order into one variation partition would give
$V^r(F)^r\ge N\delta^r$, a contradiction.  Completeness of $\mathbb C$
therefore gives the asserted endpoint limit.  This argument uses only finite
variation; the logically separate zero anchor at infinity is what gives the
maximal estimate above.
\end{corollary}

Consequently, the principal-value limit
$T^P_\Omega f:=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f$
defines an operator from $L^1$ to $L^{1,\infty}$ with bound
$C_{n,d}\mathfrak L(\Omega)$.  For general $P$, this answers, in the
mean-zero $L\log L$ class, the rough-kernel weak type $(1,1)$ problem posed
by Ding and Lai \cite[\S6.1]{DingLai}; the maximal-truncation and
critical-jump bounds are stronger.

\begin{theorem}[Coefficient-uniform bounds for every finite radial variation exponent]
\label{thm:finite-radial}
Let the hypotheses on $n,d,P$, and $\Omega$ be those of
Theorem~\ref{thm:main}.  Fix $1\le\rho<\infty$, and let
$b:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb C$ be pointwise bounded with
$\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}<\infty$.  Then
\begin{align}
 \|\mathbf J(T^{P,b}_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:\varepsilon>0)\|_p
 &\le C_{n,d,p,\rho}\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}
       \mathfrak L(\Omega)\|f\|_p,
 &&1<p<\infty,\label{eq:finite-radial-strong}\\
 \|\mathbf J(T^{P,b}_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:\varepsilon>0)\|_{L^{1,\infty}}
 &\le C_{n,d,\rho}\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}
       \mathfrak L(\Omega)\|f\|_1.
 \label{eq:finite-radial-weak}
\end{align}
Estimate \eqref{eq:finite-radial-strong} holds for every
$f\in L^p(\mathbb R^n)$, and \eqref{eq:finite-radial-weak} holds for every
$f\in L^1(\mathbb R^n)$.  The constants are independent of every
coefficient of $P$ and of the location of the radial variation of $b$.
The trajectory is uniquely determined by its finite-annulus increments
and the zero-at-infinity anchor.  For every output exponent $r>2$, the
corresponding strong and weak $r$-variation conclusions hold with the
additional factor $\kappa_r$.  The maximal-truncation estimates follow
instead from the zero anchor and \eqref{eq:anchor-controls-maximal}, with
the same right-hand sides as
\eqref{eq:finite-radial-strong}--\eqref{eq:finite-radial-weak} and no
$\kappa_r$ loss.  Almost-everywhere convergence as
$\varepsilon\downarrow0$ follows from the finiteness of $V^r$.
\end{theorem}

The dependence on the fixed finite exponent $\rho$ is not asserted to be
uniform.  Proposition~\ref{prop:radial-infinity-counterexample} shows that
the conclusion is false at $\rho=\infty$.  The range $1\le\rho<2$ also
admits the direct pathwise proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:young-radial-transfer}; the general proof, including
the critical and supercritical radial ranges, is given in
Section~\ref{sec:finite-radial}.

For a bounded function $b$ of finite total variation, write
\[
 \norm b_{\BV}=\norm b_\infty+\Var_{(0,\infty)}(b).
\]
The case $\rho=1$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-radial} is complemented by the
following direct Stieltjes transfer, whose constant is obtained without the
operator-specific radial argument.

\begin{corollary}[Radial $\BV$ transfer]\label{cor:bv-extension}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:main}, let
$b:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb C$ be bounded and of finite total variation.
All conclusions of Theorem~\ref{thm:main} and
Corollary~\ref{cor:unweighted-consequences} remain valid with
$T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}$ replaced by $T^{P,b}_{\Omega,\varepsilon}$
and with each right-hand side multiplied by
\[
 \norm b_{\BV}=\norm b_\infty+\Var_{(0,\infty)}(b).
\]
The constants are independent of where the variation of $b$ is
concentrated.
\end{corollary}

The principal estimates have the scopes summarized in
Table~\ref{tab:scope}. The strong theorem combines angular $H^1$ data,
every fixed finite radial variation exponent, and the pointwise polynomial
phase supremum. The weak endpoint remains a coefficient-uniform
fixed-phase result under angular $L\log L$. No supremum over radial
amplitudes is included in these statements.
Proposition~\ref{mod:no-weak-phase-sup} gives a one-dimensional obstruction
to retaining weak type $(1,1)$ under the phase supremum when $d\ge2$.

\subsection{Relation to earlier work}\label{intro:literature}

\paragraph{Static rough polynomial theory.}
For $P=0$, the strong and weak theory of rough homogeneous singular
integrals at the $L\log L$ angular scale was developed in
\cite{CZ56,DRdF86,CRdF88,Seeger96}.  For arbitrary $P(x,y)$,
Jiang--Lu \cite{JiangLu} studied the static principal-value operator with
$\Omega\in L\log L$ and a radial factor in $\BV$, including an $L^p$
boundedness criterion and weighted estimates.  Ojanen \cite{Ojanen}
proved weighted strong estimates for the same polynomial-phase,
angular-$L\log L$, radial-$\BV$ class under the geometric hypotheses
formulated there.  Al-Salman and Grafakos \cite{AlSalmanGrafakos} more
recently obtained static $L^p$ bounds for arbitrary $P(x,y)$, angular
$L\log L$ data, and a rough radial factor satisfying an
$L^\gamma$-type condition.  The corresponding rough-kernel weak type
$(1,1)$ endpoint for the principal-value operator was explicitly listed
as an open problem by Ding and Lai \cite[\S6.1]{DingLai}.
Theorem~\ref{thm:main} and
Corollary~\ref{cor:unweighted-consequences} answer it in the mean-zero
$L\log L$ class, with the stronger critical-jump control of the complete
sharp-truncation trajectory.

\paragraph{Smooth coefficient-uniform weak theory.}
Ricci and Stein \cite{RicciStein} established coefficient-uniform strong
$L^p$ estimates for smooth kernels.  Chanillo and Christ
\cite{ChanilloChrist} proved coefficient-uniform weak type $(1,1)$ for
arbitrary polynomial phases and smooth Calder\'on--Zygmund convolution
kernels.  Their argument removes pure phases, normalizes a leading
coefficient by dilation, and uses a phase-adapted double induction; their
final remarks also yield $L^p$ bounds, $1<p<\infty$, for the maximal
truncation.  Rough $L\log L$ kernels fall outside that theory.  The global
phase flag introduced below is designed to retain coefficient uniformity
while accommodating rough annular decay and phases that degenerate on the
diagonal.

\paragraph{Maximal rough polynomial theory.}
Representative rough polynomial estimates include
\cite{LuZhang,AlSalman}; polynomial maximal-truncation estimates for
smooth Calder\'on--Zygmund kernels include \cite{KrauseLacey}.

Ding and Liu \cite[Theorem~1.1]{DingLiu2011} proved $L^p$ bounds,
$1<p<\infty$, for the pointwise coefficient supremum of principal-value
operators with mean-zero angular $H^1(\mathbb S^{n-1})$ kernels,
$n\ge2$, and radial phases
$Q_\lambda(|z|)=\sum_{k=2}^d\lambda_k|z|^k$.
On the common subclass of even-power radial phases,
Theorem~\ref{mod:main} strengthens this principal-value estimate to
critical-jump control of the complete sharp-truncation trajectory,
with every fixed finite radial variation exponent. Their odd radial
powers lie outside the polynomial phase class considered here.

Choudhary, Shrivastava, and Shuin \cite{CSS} state coefficient-uniform
sparse and strong maximal estimates for arbitrary $P$ when
$\Omega\in L^\infty$.  For $P=0$, Lai \cite[Theorem~1.1]{Lai} proves the
weak $(1,1)$ maximal estimate at the $L\log L$ angular endpoint.  In the
present paper, maximal truncation is obtained as a consequence of the
stronger critical-jump theorem.

Chen and Ji \cite[Theorems~1.1--1.2]{ChenJi} establish coefficient-uniform
weak $(1,1)$ bounds for the principal-value operator and its full maximal
truncation in the same mean-zero $L\log L$ angular class. For $b\equiv1$,
our fixed-phase critical-jump estimate strictly strengthens both
conclusions. Indeed, the zero-at-infinity anchor gives
\[
 T_P^*f(x)\le\mathbf J(T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:\varepsilon>0),
\]
and finite jumps also yield the principal-value limit. The additional
fluctuation control is strictly stronger than maximal control: a finite
path alternating between zero and one can have arbitrarily large jump
functional while its maximum remains one. The related weak endpoint
was publicly stated by Tseng-math on August~31, 2026 \cite{TsengPublic}.

\paragraph{Fluctuation theory.}
For sharply truncated Hilbert transforms,
Campbell, Jones, Reinhold, and Wierdl
\cite[Theorems~1.2--1.4 and Conjecture~7.2]{CJRW} established strong and
weak $r$-variation estimates for $r>2$, the associated noncritical jump
bounds, and short $2$-variation estimates, and isolated the critical jump
exponent.  Their higher-dimensional work \cite{CJRW03} developed
singular-integral variation theory at the rough $L\log L$ scale.
Jones--Seeger--Wright \cite[Theorem~1.6]{JSW} proved the conventional
strong critical-jump inequality for the unphased rough trajectory under
$\Omega\in L^a$, $a>1$, and posed the corresponding weak-type $(1,1)$
estimate for rough homogeneous kernels \cite{JSW}.  Their formulation
places the supremum in the jump amplitude outside the spatial norm.  The
$P=0$ case of \eqref{eq:main-weak-jump} places that supremum pointwise
inside $L^{1,\infty}$, and hence strictly strengthens the problem they
posed.

Chen, Ding, Hong, and Liu \cite{ChenDingHongLiu} state weighted
jump estimates with the amplitude supremum inside the spatial norm.
The proof of their smooth-averaging proposition invokes a martingale
estimate from \cite{KrauseZorinKranich}; the stated estimate in that
source places the amplitude supremum outside the spatial norm. Section~\ref{mod:sec-martingale-pointwise} proves the required
unweighted pointwise martingale inequality directly;
Lemma~\ref{radialmod:smooth-jump} gives its smooth-averaging form, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:one-dimensional-inside-jump} derives the directional
singular-integral and one-sided-average estimates.
  Ding, Hong, and Liu
\cite[Theorem~1.2(ii)]{DHL} state, with the angular size tacitly
normalized, the unphased strong critical-jump theorem at the $L\log L$
endpoint.  Bhojak and Shrivastava
\cite[Theorem~1.1]{BhojakShrivastava} subsequently state the unphased weak
critical-jump and weak $r$-variation endpoints.
Remark~\ref{rem:proof-status} explains why neither of the latter two
arguments is used here.

\paragraph{Polynomial modulation and rough kernels.}
Becker, van Doorn, Jamneshan, Srivastava, and Thiele \cite{BDJST}
construct a polynomial tile geometry and prove Carleson estimates for
one-sided H\"older kernels under a non-tangential maximal $L^2$ hypothesis.
We use the spatial and phase-cell geometry, the combinatorial
decomposition, and the geometric stopping lemmas from that work. The
analytic estimates for the rough kernels considered here are established
in Part~\ref{part:modulation}; they are not consequences of the
H\"older-kernel theorem alone.

Becker, Jamneshan, and Thiele
\cite[Theorem~1.3 and Corollary~1.4]{BJT} prove strong $L^p$
$r$-variation estimates, $1<p<\infty$ and $r>2$, for sharp truncations of
H\"older Calder\'on--Zygmund kernels satisfying exact two-sided annular
cancellation. Their estimate takes the supremum over bounded-degree
polynomial input modulations pointwise inside the $L^p$ norm and holds
more generally on homogeneous Lie groups. In Euclidean space, this
formulation already includes two-variable polynomial phases: for each
fixed output point $x$, write
\[
 Q_x(y)=P(x,y)-P(x,0),\qquad
 T^P_{\Omega,t}f(x)=e^{iP(x,0)}T^{Q_x}_{\Omega,t}f(x).
\]
The factor $e^{iP(x,0)}$ is independent of the truncation parameter.
Consequently, the smooth-kernel result is not confined to phases that
are independent of the output variable.

Combining their one-dimensional theorem with the method of rotations
also gives the fixed-phase, coefficient-uniform strong $r>2$ estimates
for odd angular kernels in $L^1$, including the entire mean-zero angular
class in dimension one. That argument does not treat the general even
rough component at the $L\log L$ scale, the critical $2$-jump estimate,
or the spatial weak endpoint. Theorem~\ref{mod:main} treats the
mean-zero angular $H^1$ class at the critical jump scale, with a
pointwise phase supremum and every fixed finite radial variation exponent. Corollary~\ref{mod:odd-L1} records the corresponding
$L^1$ angular improvement for odd kernels.

\paragraph{Contributions and complementary endpoints.}
Theorem~\ref{mod:main} gives the strong phase-supremum estimate under
angular $H^1$ and finite radial variation. Its bounded-angular stage is
uniform for conically supported elliptic truncations; cancellative
atomic summation then gives the Hardy norm directly. The radial factor
and the sharp cutoff undergo the same affine transformation.
Finite-$\rho$ oscillatory decay and the actual paired-witness structure
give the estimates needed for phase selection, including $\rho\ge2$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:main} and Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-radial} retain the
complementary fixed-phase weak endpoint under angular $L\log L$.
This weak conclusion is not inferred from the strong Hardy theorem.

\subsection{Sharpness and limiting cases}\label{intro:sharpness}

The strong modulation theorem reaches the critical jump exponent $2$;
the fixed-phase theorem additionally reaches spatial weak type $(1,1)$.
For $n\ge2$, the inherited obstruction at the $L\log L$ threshold
concerns the Orlicz scale, rather than optimality among all angular
function spaces.  Al-Qassem, Cheng, and Pan
\cite[Theorem~1.3(b)]{ACP} construct mean-zero angular functions in
$L(\log L)^{1-\varepsilon}$, for every $\varepsilon>0$, whose associated
scalar rough oscillatory integrals fail to be uniformly bounded over
polynomial phases.
This does not conflict with the strict inclusion $L\log L\subset H^1$:
the Orlicz classes $L(\log L)^{1-\varepsilon}$ are not thereby
asserted to lie in $H^1$. No optimality statement for the angular Hardy
class is made. Proposition~\ref{mod:spatial-hardy-obstruction} also
shows why the angular statement cannot be read as a spatial
$H^1(\mathbb R^n)\to L^1$ theorem.

The radial and fluctuation endpoints are settled separately in
Section~\ref{sec:sharpness-obstructions}.  Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-radial}
holds for each fixed $1\le\rho<\infty$, but a one-dimensional Hilbert
multiplier example excludes $\rho=\infty$.  An abstract scalar-path example
shows that the direct Young--Stieltjes transfer necessarily stops before
$\rho=2$, even though operator-specific orthogonality recovers every finite
$\rho$.  Finally, an exact Mellin representation reduces Poisson
$2$-variation to sharp Hilbert truncations and, together with the
finite Gaussian obstruction proved below, proves that the pointwise critical jump cannot
be strengthened to true output $V^2$.  The precise optimal growth of the
$r>2$ constants as $r\downarrow2$ is not addressed.

The radial theory also contains a distinct shellwise refinement for the
Hilbert transform. If
\[
 R_2(b)=\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}
 V^2(b;(2^j,2^{j+1}))^2\right)^{1/2}<\infty,
\]
then Proposition~\ref{prop:hilbert-radial-v2} gives
\[
 \|\mathbf J(H^b_\varepsilon f:\varepsilon>0)\|_2
 \le C\bigl(\|b\|_\infty+R_2(b)\bigr)\|f\|_2.
\]
The condition is genuinely shellwise: a bounded function that is constant
on each open dyadic shell has $R_2(b)=0$, even when its values oscillate
between successive shells and its global finite-exponent variation is
infinite. This refinement is independent of the global finite-variation
hypothesis in Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-radial}.

\subsection{Proof strategy and organization}\label{intro:strategy}

\paragraph{Angular concentration and sharp truncation geometry.}
A small mean-zero cap atom can be stretched to a uniformly bounded
angular function in a fixed cone. The transformation changes Euclidean
sharp truncations into elliptic ones. The estimate in
Theorem~\ref{mod:cone} is uniform as the transverse singular values tend
to zero, provided the angular support stays in the fixed nondegenerate
cone. The exact cutoff is retained throughout. This permits atomic
summation before any conjugate-exponent extrapolation over density
levels.

\paragraph{Collective phase selection.}
A normalized jump witness has equal-amplitude interval coefficients.
Single-shell Fourier decay therefore controls the high-frequency part
after a phase-local smoothing. Near phase blocks are estimated by this
decay; far blocks are estimated by the rough equal-shell correlation.
The resulting collective remainder is summed before absolute values are
taken over the large tiles. This order avoids a spatial-overlap loss in the antichain estimate. For forests, the separated-tree estimate
retains norms on the intersection of the two top cubes, with the kernel
regularity constant separated from the fixed tile geometry.

The weak endpoint requires a different organization of the oscillatory
analysis. Three features of the fixed-phase argument are particularly
important.

\paragraph{Phase degeneracy.}
Neither a displayed monomial coefficient nor the diagonal restriction of
the phase provides a stable normalization.  Simultaneous translation mixes
individual coefficients, and the alternating bilinear phase
$x_1y_2-x_2y_1$ vanishes on the diagonal.  Section~\ref{sec:flag}
constructs a finite global flag on polynomial phases modulo pure input and
output phases.  The transverse rows are placed in reduced row-echelon
form, and the final nonterminal rows consist of convolution phases and
alternating bilinear forms.  Dilation normalizes the first nonzero flag
coordinate, while simultaneous translation preserves that coordinate and
creates only later ones.  This triangular structure yields
coefficient-uniform annular decay and closes the local analysis by a finite
backward induction over the flag.

\paragraph{Modulation-sensitive cancellation.}
The input modulations generated by a nonconvolution phase change the
cancellation condition on each bad cube. The phase-adapted
Calder\'on--Zygmund decomposition supplies
\[
 \int_Q e^{iP(c_Q,y)}b_Q(y)\,dy=0,
\]
and anchors the operator phase at the same parent center. Low phase
coordinates are Fourier-expanded with coefficients fixed before the
output point is evaluated; large coordinates give scale-covariant
oscillatory decay. Proposition~\ref{prop:anchoredpacket} presents this
interaction in a bounded-density model. The concentration-stable packet
estimates in Section~\ref{sec:degree-packets} implement it under the
cancellation and average mass bounds of the actual bad functions.

\paragraph{Output-dependent selectors.}
A jump linearization chooses parameter intervals as functions of the
output point.  The endpoint argument first imposes finite cube, shell, and
parameter cutoffs and assigns every relevant increment a fixed rank.  A
variational Rademacher--Menshov estimate then controls every interval sum
through that ranking, without introducing an output-dependent sign choice.
The phase-adapted bad functions enter directly through cancellation and
the average density bound
$\norm{h_Q}_1\lesssim\alpha|Q|$.  On each shell, a descending
homogeneous-degree packet induction combines Fourier expansion of low
blocks with a concentration-stable high-block estimate.  Across high
shells, an exact oriented two-ray calculation retains a coefficient of
size $R^m$ after all projective cancellations and lower-degree
perturbations.  A one-sided polynomial incidence estimate then sums the
small-cube mesh errors geometrically while the larger atom remains fixed.
The resulting fixed-sign all-shell estimate is precisely the input needed
by the rank argument, and it requires no $L^\infty$ decomposition of the
bad data.

The pointwise martingale, smooth-averaging, and one-dimensional
jump inputs are proved in this paper. The martingale proof uses a
greedy-exit potential and two good-lambda inequalities on finite binary
trees; the square-function and maximal estimates are proved there as
well. A separate Brownian argument gives a stopped continuous-path
estimate. The smooth square-function comparisons use classical
Littlewood--Paley estimates and the
Fefferman--Stein vector-valued maximal inequality. The remaining
specialized inputs include the long--short mechanisms of
Jones--Seeger--Wright, the Hilbert short-variation theorem of
Campbell--Jones--Reinhold--Wierdl, and Seeger's separated-packet estimates.
The precise geometric input from Becker--van Doorn--Jamneshan--Srivastava--Thiele
is stated in Section~\ref{mod:sec-geometry}. The sharpness argument uses
a finite Gaussian obstruction and de Leeuw's restriction principle;
Jones--Wang's Poisson counterexample \cite{JonesWang2004} provides background only. Path normability is proved directly in
Lemma~\ref{mod:pathlemma}; no interchange of amplitude supremum and
spatial norm is used.

\paragraph{Organization and dependencies.}
Part~\ref{part:modulation} proves Theorem~\ref{mod:main};
Part~\ref{part:fixed} develops the independent fixed-phase endpoint,
radial-amplitude, and sharpness theory. A section-by-section guide
opens each part. The correlation inputs for Part~\ref{part:modulation}
are Lemma~\ref{lem:rough-correlation},
Lemma~\ref{lem:allq-rational-operator}, and
Proposition~\ref{prop:allq-rough-correlation}. Their proofs in
Part~\ref{part:fixed} do not use Theorem~\ref{mod:main}.
The explicit path norm in Lemma~\ref{mod:pathlemma} is an independent
algebraic statement used in both parts.



\part{Maximal polynomial modulation}\label{part:modulation}
\noindent
Section~\ref{mod:sec-reduction} reduces the phase-supremum problem to a
finite-path estimate for conically supported elliptic truncations.
Sections~\ref{mod:sec-analysis}--\ref{mod:sec-geometry} establish the
annular analysis and specify the polynomial tile geometry.
Section~\ref{mod:sec-antichains} proves the collective antichain estimate;
Sections~\ref{mod:tree:mod:sec-trees}--\ref{mod:sec-assembly} develop the
localized tree estimates and sum the forests in the full spatial range.
The spherical atomic decomposition, affine normalization, and trajectory
completion occupy
Sections~\ref{mod:atom:sec:atoms}--\ref{mod:atom:sec:mainproof}.
Section~\ref{mod:consequences} collects the maximal, variation, convergence,
and odd-kernel consequences, together with the obstruction to a
phase-supremum weak endpoint.


\section{Maximal modulation and the finite-path reduction}\label{mod:sec-reduction}
The proof of Theorem~\ref{mod:main} begins with a finite-path
linearization. At each output point, one polynomial is used for every
increment in the selected jump family; the polynomial may vary between
output points. Both selections are made before the spatial norm is taken.

The principal analytic reduction is a uniform estimate for conically
supported elliptic truncations. After this estimate has been established,
a cancellative spherical atomic decomposition yields the angular endpoint.
The polynomial geometry is taken from \cite{BDJST}; the required
rough-kernel operator estimates are proved in the following sections.

\subsection{A norm for paired jump paths}
For an ordered parameter set $D$ put
\begin{equation}\label{mod:pathnorm}
 \NN_D(F)=\sup_{N\ge1}\sup_{\substack{s_1<t_1\le\cdots\le s_N<t_N\\s_i,t_i\in D}}
 N^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^N|F_{t_i}-F_{s_i}|.
\end{equation}
The empty supremum is zero.
\begin{lemma}\label{mod:pathlemma}
For every scalar path,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:pathequiv}
 \mathbf J_D(F)\le\NN_D(F)\le2\mathbf J_D(F).
\end{equation}
For finite $D$, $\NN_D$ is a norm modulo constant paths. If $F^z$ is
coordinatewise integrable against the total variation of a complex
measure $\mu$, then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:pathintegral}
 \NN_D\left(\int F^z\,d\mu(z)\right)
 \le\int\NN_D(F^z)\,d|\mu|(z).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A strict $\lambda$-jump witness with $N$ pairs contributes more than
$\lambda\sqrt N$ to \eqref{mod:pathnorm}. Conversely, fix a paired family
and arrange the moduli of its increments in decreasing order $c_k^*$.
The largest $k$ increments, restored to temporal order, remain admissible.
Thus $c_k^*\sqrt{k}\le\mathbf J_D(F)$. Consequently
$N^{-1/2}\sum_{k\le N}c_k^*\le2\mathbf J_D(F)$. This proves
\eqref{mod:pathequiv}. Subadditivity follows by applying the ordinary
triangle inequality to each paired family. A one-pair test detects every
coordinate difference. Finally apply the integral triangle inequality to
each finite paired family and take the supremum.
\end{proof}

The explicit norm $\NN_D$ supplies the finite-path normability used
in Lemma~\ref{lem:jumpnorm}. The proof above concerns the pointwise
functional itself. The mixed-norm interpolation formulation in
\cite{MSZK} is a separate statement and is not needed for this step.

Let $\PP_d$ denote the real polynomials on $\R^n$ of degree at most $d$,
modulo constants. For fixed $x$,
$Q_{P,x}(y)=P(x,y)-P(x,0)$ belongs to this space, and
\[
 T^{P,b}_{\Omega,t}f(x)=e^{iP(x,0)}T^{Q_{P,x},b}_{\Omega,t}f(x).
\]
Conversely, every $Q(y)$ occurs as $P(x,y)=Q(y)$. The two pointwise phase
suprema are therefore equal. It suffices to consider input phases $Q$.

For finite phase and truncation sets, all selections below can be made
measurably, with a deterministic rule for ties. Linearizing
$\max_Q\NN_D(T^{Q,b}f)$ gives a measurable $Q_x$ of finite range and
\begin{equation}\label{mod:witness}
 h_x(r)=N(x)^{-1/2}\sum_{\nu=1}^{N(x)}
 \zeta_\nu(x)\mathbf1_{(a_\nu(x),b_\nu(x)]}(r),\quad |\zeta_\nu|\le1,
\end{equation}
where $a_1<b_1\le\cdots\le a_N<b_N$. The zero operator is used if there
is no nonempty witness. We have
\begin{equation}\label{mod:witnessbounds}
 \|h_x\|_\infty\le N(x)^{-1/2},\qquad V^2(h_x;\R)\le C.
\end{equation}
Indeed, after zero extension the step function has at most $2N+2$
changes, each of modulus at most $2N^{-1/2}$; deletion of repeated
coordinates gives the same bound for every variation partition.
An intersection with one radial interval retains the form
\eqref{mod:witness}, up to a common coefficient of modulus at most one.
This special form is retained whenever a pathwise estimate is used.
An arbitrary bounded-$V^2$ radial multiplier is not substituted for it.

Fix $1\le\rho<\infty$ and the radial amplitude $b$ from
Theorem~\ref{mod:main}. Set $q_*:=\max\{2,\rho\}$. The product
variation inequality gives
\begin{equation}\label{mod:weighted-profile}
 \|b h_x\|_\infty+V^{q_*}(b h_x)
 \le C\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}.
\end{equation}
Indeed $V^{q_*}(b)\le V^\rho(b)$ and
$V^{q_*}(h_x)\le V^2(h_x)$, and
$V^{q_*}(uv)\le\|u\|_\infty V^{q_*}(v)+
\|v\|_\infty V^{q_*}(u)$. Multiplication by an interval indicator
adds at most two endpoint jumps, so the same estimate holds on each
shell after zero extension. This finite-variation fact is used only
in oscillatory correlation estimates. In unphased path estimates,
$b$ remains part of the fixed kernel and $h_x$ retains its actual
witness form \eqref{mod:witness}.

\subsection{The conical analytic theorem}
Fix $c_0\in(0,1)$. Let $A$ be a real matrix with $\|A\|\le1$ and put
$\rho_A(z)=|Az|$. Suppose
\begin{equation}\label{mod:cone-data}
 \int\Theta\,d\sigma=0,\qquad \Theta\in L^\infty(\Sph^{n-1}),\qquad
 \operatorname{supp}\Theta\subset\{\theta:|A\theta|\ge c_0\}.
\end{equation}
No lower bound on the least singular value of $A$ is imposed. Choose
a measurable representative of $\Theta$ that vanishes everywhere on
$\{\theta:|A\theta|<c_0\}$. The full weighted kernel is defined to be
zero wherever $\rho_A(z)=0$ or $\Theta(z')=0$; the value $b(0)$ is
never evaluated. In particular, $A=0$ gives the zero operator. Define
\[
 S^{Q,A,b}_{\Theta,t}f(x)
 =\int_{\rho_A(x-y)>t}e^{iQ(y)}K_\Theta(x-y)
       b(\rho_A(x-y))f(y)\,dy.
\]
\begin{theorem}[Uniform conical estimate]\label{mod:cone}
For all data in \eqref{mod:cone-data}, every fixed
$b\in\mathcal V^\rho$ with $1\le\rho<\infty$, all finite
$\mathcal F\subset\PP_d$ and $D_0\subset(0,\infty)$, and every
bounded compactly supported $f$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:cone-bound}
 \left\|\max_{Q\in\mathcal F}\NN_{D_0}(S^{Q,A,b}_{\Theta,t}f)\right\|_p
 \le C_{n,d,p,c_0,\rho}\|\Theta\|_\infty
       \|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}\|f\|_p,
 \qquad1<p<\infty.
\end{equation}
The constant is independent of $A$, $\mathcal F$, and $D_0$.
\end{theorem}
By homogeneity it suffices to assume
$\|\Theta\|_\infty\le1$ and $\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}\le1$.
The geometry is Euclidean. The function $\rho_A$ enters both the
radial amplitude and the cutoff; the spatial grid and the polynomial
metric are Euclidean. All subsequent analytic constants may depend on
the fixed finite exponent $\rho$.




\section{A pointwise jump inequality for dyadic martingales}
\label{mod:sec-martingale-pointwise}

We prove the unweighted martingale estimate with the jump-amplitude
supremum \emph{inside} the spatial norm. A bound obtained by taking
that supremum outside $L^p$ is not used in this argument.

For a continuous scalar path $F$ on an interval $I$, let
\[
 \mathcal N_I(F)=
 \sup_{N\ge1}\ \sup_{\substack{s_1<t_1\le\cdots\le s_N<t_N\\s_i,t_i\in I}}
 N^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^N|F(t_i)-F(s_i)|.
\]
The same definition is used for a finite ordered sequence. It is
unchanged by addition of a constant and is homogeneous. Its relation
to the paired strict jump functional is
\begin{equation}\label{mod:martingale-NJ}
 \mathbf J_I(F)\le\mathcal N_I(F)\le2\mathbf J_I(F).
\end{equation}
Indeed, a strict jump witness gives the first inequality. For the
second, arrange a witness's increment magnitudes as $c_k^*$ in
decreasing order. Its largest $k$ increments still form an admissible
paired family, so $c_k^*\sqrt{k}\le\mathbf J_I(F)$. Now use
$\sum_{k=1}^N k^{-1/2}\le2\sqrt N$.

\begin{lemma}[Concatenation]\label{mod:martingale-concatenation}
If $a\le s\le t$, then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:martingale-concatenation-eq}
 \mathcal N_{[a,t]}(F)
 \le\mathcal N_{[a,s]}(F)+\mathcal N_{[s,t]}(F).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix a witness with $N$ pairs in $[a,t]$. At most one pair crosses $s$.
Split that increment at $s$ and apply the triangle inequality to it.
After zero intervals are removed, each side is an admissible paired
family containing at most $N$ pairs. If their cardinalities are
$N_1,N_2$, their contributions divided by $\sqrt N$ are at most
$\sqrt{N_1/N}\mathcal N_{[a,s]}(F)$ and
$\sqrt{N_2/N}\mathcal N_{[s,t]}(F)$. Each coefficient is at most one.
Take the supremum over the original witness.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The binary martingale estimate}

\begin{theorem}[Pointwise critical jumps for dyadic martingales]
\label{mod:martingale-pointwise}
Let $1<p<\infty$ and let $(M_k)_{k=0}^N$ be a finite martingale on a
finite binary probability tree, each branch having conditional
probability $1/2$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:binary-jump}
 \|\mathcal N(M_k:0\le k\le N)\|_{L^p}
 \le C_p\|M_N\|_{L^p}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, if $E_j$ denotes expectation on the dyadic cubes of
sidelength $2^j$ in $\mathbb R^n$, then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:dyadic-pointwise-jump}
 \left\|\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda
     N_\lambda(E_jf:j\in\mathbb Z)^{1/2}\right\|_{L^p(\mathbb R^n)}
 \le\|\mathcal N(E_jf:j\in\mathbb Z)\|_{L^p(\mathbb R^n)}
 \le C_p\|f\|_{L^p(\mathbb R^n)}.
\end{equation}
The constants are independent of the length of the martingale.
For $1<p\le2$ they may be chosen at most $C p/(p-1)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We give a direct finite-tree proof. First take one root and real
values. Label the nodes at depth $k$ by $0\le r<2^k$, with children
$2r,2r+1$, and give each terminal leaf mass $2^{-N}$. The martingale
identity says that the two increments from any parent are $d$ and
$-d$, with no restriction that $d\ne0$. Expectations are taken with respect to this finite probability law.
Along a branch put
\[
 Q_k=\sum_{i=1}^k(M_i-M_{i-1})^2,\quad S=\sqrt{Q_N},\quad
 X=\max_{0\le k\le N}|M_k|,\quad
 A_k=\mathcal N(M_i:0\le i\le k),\quad A=A_N.
\]
Write $J(F)=\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda N_\lambda(F)^{1/2}$.
We use the deterministic comparison
$J(F)\le\mathcal N(F)\le2J(F)$ from
\eqref{mod:martingale-NJ}. To recall why the upper bound has an
absolute constant, arrange the lengths of any $m$ paired increments
in decreasing order $a_1\ge\cdots\ge a_m$. Their subfamilies show
$a_i\sqrt i\le J(F)$, by taking thresholds increasing to $a_i$.
Hence $\sum_{i=1}^m a_i\le J(F)\sum_{i=1}^m i^{-1/2}
\le2J(F)\sqrt m$. The lower bound follows by using a jump witness
in the definition of $\mathcal N$. Splitting every paired increment
at a common time also gives
\[
 A_k\le A_{k-1}+|M_k-M_{k-1}|,\qquad
 A_N\le A_k+\mathcal N(M_i:k\le i\le N).
\]
Indeed, each of the two resulting families has at most the original
number of pairs; divide their sums by that original square root.
Empty or zero-length pieces contribute zero. In particular $A_k$
is nondecreasing, starts at zero, and all quantities above are finite.

\emph{A tail estimate with a quadratic budget.}
Fix $a>0$. Starting with base $M_0$, accept a new greedy exit
whenever the distance from the current base first exceeds $a$,
and reset the base to that new value. Let $G_k$ be the number of
completed exits and $b_k$ the current base at time $k$.
For $s\ge0$ consider the nonnegative potential
\[
 Z_k=\cosh(sa)^{G_k}\cosh\bigl(s(M_k-b_k)\bigr)
             \exp(-s^2Q_k/2).
\]
It starts at $1$. Before resetting a child, its unfinished factor is
$\cosh(s(M_{k+1}-b_k))$. If an exit is accepted, replacing this
factor by $\cosh(sa)$ can only decrease it. At a parent whose
increment size is $d$, the average of the two unreset factors is
\[
 \frac{\cosh(s(M_k-b_k+d))+\cosh(s(M_k-b_k-d))}{2}
 =\cosh\bigl(s(M_k-b_k)\bigr)\cosh(sd).
\]
Both children add $d^2$ to $Q_k$, and
$\cosh(sd)\le\exp(s^2d^2/2)$. The latter inequality follows, for
example, by integrating $\tanh t\le t$ for $t\ge0$ and using
evenness. Thus the average of the child potentials is at most the
parent potential, and induction on the finite levels gives
$\mathbb E Z_N\le1$. Since $\cosh t\ge e^t/2$, for $m\ge1$ and
$v>0$ this implies
\[
 \mathbb P(G_N\ge m,\ Q_N\le v)
 \le2^m\exp(s^2v/2-msa).
\]
Taking $s=ma/v$ proves
\[
 \mathbb P(G_N\ge m,\ Q_N\le v)
 \le2^m\exp\left(-\frac{m^2a^2}{2v}\right).
\]

This controls the amplitude supremum \emph{inside} the probability.
If a pair has length greater than $2a$, there must be a greedy exit
in its half-open time interval: otherwise both endpoints are within
$a$ of the same base. Ordered disjoint pairs therefore require
distinct exits. If $J(M)>u>0$, choose a witness with $m$ pairs of
length greater than $\lambda$, where $\lambda\sqrt m>u$, and choose
$j\ge0$ so that $4^j\le m<4^{j+1}$. Retaining $4^j$ pairs gives
lengths greater than $u/(2\cdot2^j)$. The greedy count at threshold
$a_j=u/(4\cdot2^j)$ is consequently at least $4^j$. A countable
union bound, followed by the preceding estimate, yields
\[
 \mathbb P(J(M)>u,\ Q_N\le v)
 \le\sum_{j=0}^\infty
       2^{4^j}\exp\left(-\frac{4^ju^2}{32v}\right).
\]
If $64v\log2\le u^2$, put $h=u^2/(32v)\ge2\log2$.
Since $4^j\ge j+1$, each summand is at most
$\exp(-(j+1)h/2)$, and $e^{-h/2}\le1/2$. Summing this geometric
majorant gives the depth-independent bound
\[
 \mathbb P(J(M)>u,\ Q_N\le v)
 \le2\exp\left(-\frac{u^2}{64v}\right).
\]
The argument applies verbatim on the descendants of every node,
with their normalized fair probability law.

\emph{The path-norm good-lambda inequality.}
Take $0<\delta\le1$ with $64\delta^2\log2\le1$, and set
$\varepsilon(\delta)=2\exp(-1/(64\delta^2))$.
On $\{A>4u,\ S\le\delta u\}$, let $\sigma$ be the first level
where $A_\sigma>u$. Each increment has size at most $S\le u$,
so the preceding one-step estimate gives $A_\sigma\le2u$.
The concatenation estimate then forces the suffix path norm to
exceed $2u$, hence its $J$ to exceed $u$. Its quadratic variation
is at most $(\delta u)^2$. The first-crossing nodes have disjoint
descendant cylinders, whose union is exactly $\{A>u\}$.
On each cylinder the preceding suffix estimate has conditional
probability at most $\varepsilon(\delta)$. Multiplying by the
cylinder masses and summing gives
\[
 \mathbb P(A>4u,\ S\le\delta u)
 \le\varepsilon(\delta)\mathbb P(A>u),\qquad u>0.
\]
Consequently, for any $p>0$, layer cake on this finite probability
space gives
\[
 \bigl(4^{-p}-\varepsilon(\delta)\bigr)\mathbb E A^p
 \le\delta^{-p}\mathbb E S^p.
\]
Indeed, first bound $\mathbb P(A>4u)$ by the right-hand side of
the good-lambda inequality plus $\mathbb P(S>\delta u)$, and
integrate against $pu^{p-1}\,du$. All moments are finite here,
so absorption is legitimate. The choice
\[
 \delta_p=\bigl(64(2p+3)\log2\bigr)^{-1/2}
\]
satisfies the preceding restrictions and
$\varepsilon(\delta_p)4^p=1/4$. Thus
\[
 \|A\|_p\le D_p\|S\|_p,\qquad
 D_p=\delta_p^{-1}
       \bigl(4^{-p}-\varepsilon(\delta_p)\bigr)^{-1/p}<\infty,
\]
with no dependence on the depth or the martingale values.

\emph{Square function, maximal function, and terminal value.}
We next prove the needed square-function estimate, rather than
assuming it. For a real binary martingale $Y$ and a number $R\ge0$,
freeze both descendants of a node before a step unless the current
value has absolute value at most $R$ and both child values have
absolute value at most $3R$; once frozen, stay frozen. This is a
predictable rule on the tree, so the resulting process $\widetilde Y$
is still a martingale. If $|Y_0|\le R$, it is bounded by $3R$.
On any branch on which $\max|Y_k|\le R$, no step is frozen:
the unused sibling is twice the parent minus the chosen child,
so its absolute value is at most $3R$. Therefore
$Q_N(\widetilde Y)=Q_N(Y)$ on those branches. Averaging the identity
\[
 \frac{(y+d)^2+(y-d)^2}{2}=y^2+d^2
\]
over the successive levels gives
$\mathbb E Q_N(\widetilde Y)
 =\mathbb E\widetilde Y_N^2-Y_0^2\le9R^2$.
For $v>0$ we obtain
\[
 \mathbb P\bigl(Q_N(Y)\ge v,\ \max_k|Y_k|\le R\bigr)
 \le\frac{9R^2}{v}.
\]
If $|Y_0|>R$, the event is empty, so the same bound remains true.

Return to $M$, and take $u>0$, $0\le R\le u/2$. On
$\{Q_N>4u^2,\ X\le R\}$ stop at the first node with $Q_k>u^2$.
The last increment has absolute value at most $2R$, so the
quadratic variation at that node is at most
$u^2+4R^2\le2u^2$. The suffix therefore has quadratic variation
at least $2u^2$ and all its values have absolute value at most $R$.
Apply the preceding bound, with $v=2u^2$, on each first-crossing
cylinder. Their disjoint union is $\{Q_N>u^2\}$, and hence
\[
 \mathbb P(S>2u,\ X\le\eta u)
 \le\tfrac92\eta^2\mathbb P(S>u),\qquad 0<\eta\le\tfrac12.
\]
Another layer-cake integration shows
\[
 \|S\|_p\le B_p\|X\|_p,\qquad
 B_p=\eta^{-1}\bigl(2^{-p}-\tfrac92\eta^2\bigr)^{-1/p},
\]
whenever the quantity in parentheses is positive. For every $p>0$
one may take $\eta=1/(4(2^p+1))$, since
$(9/2)\eta^2 2^p=9\cdot2^p/(32(2^p+1)^2)<1$.

For completeness, the finite-tree maximal estimate has exactly the
factor $p/(p-1)$ when $p>1$. On every first-crossing cylinder of
$\{|M_k|>t\}$, the node value is the average of its terminal
values, so its absolute value is at most the average of $|M_N|$.
Multiplying by the cylinder masses and summing gives
\[
 t\,\mathbb P(X>t)\le\mathbb E\bigl(|M_N|\mathbf1_{\{X>t\}}\bigr).
\]
Integrating against $pt^{p-2}\,dt$ and then applying H\"older gives
\[
 \mathbb E X^p\le\frac p{p-1}\mathbb E(|M_N|X^{p-1})
 \le\frac p{p-1}\|M_N\|_p\|X\|_p^{p-1}.
\]
Division when $\|X\|_p>0$, with the zero case immediate, proves
$\|X\|_p\le p(p-1)^{-1}\|M_N\|_p$. Combining the three estimates
proves the real assertion with
$K_p=D_pB_p\,p/(p-1)$ for every $1<p<\infty$.

For $1<p\le2$, fix $\delta=1/32$ and $\eta=1/16$ in the preceding
good-lambda estimates. Then
$4^{-p}-\varepsilon(\delta)\ge1/32$ and
$2^{-p}-(9/2)\eta^2\ge1/8$, so $D_p$ and $B_p$ are uniformly bounded
in this range. Consequently $K_p\le C p/(p-1)$.

\emph{Complex values and arbitrary initial roots.}
The real and imaginary parts satisfy the same midpoint identities, and
\[
 \mathcal N(M)\le\mathcal N(\operatorname{Re}M)
                     +\mathcal N(\operatorname{Im}M).
\]
Minkowski's inequality and
$|\operatorname{Re}M_N|,|\operatorname{Im}M_N|\le|M_N|$ therefore give
$\|J(M)\|_p\le2K_p\|M_N\|_p$ and
$\|\mathcal N(M)\|_p\le4K_p\|M_N\|_p$.

If the initial sigma field has finitely many atoms, index them by
$r$ and write their masses as $w_r\ge0$. A leaf below root $r$
has mass $w_r2^{-N}$, since each of the $N$ conditional branch
probabilities is $1/2$. Labeling such leaves identifies all integrals
of ancestral-path functionals with
$\sum_r w_r\mathbb E_r$, where $\mathbb E_r$ is the normalized
binary average on that root. Zero-mass roots contribute zero.
If $H_r$ is either of the two path functionals and the one-root
constant is $C_p$, raise that estimate to the $p$th power before
summing:
\[
 \sum_r w_r\mathbb E_r H_r^p
 \le C_p^p\sum_r w_r\mathbb E_r|M_N|^p.
\]
Taking the $p$th root proves exactly the same estimate on the whole
forest, with no loss depending on the number or masses of the
initial atoms. This argument needs neither equal root masses nor
$\sum_r w_r=1$; in the probability case the latter sum is $1$.
The same identity holds for nonnegative extended weights and
nonnegative integrals, with the usual convention $0\cdot\infty=0$.
This proves \eqref{mod:binary-jump}, including nontrivial finite
initial sigma fields and zero martingale increments.

\emph{Euclidean dyadic averages at all integer scales.}
Fix integers $a\le b$ and a half-open dyadic root cube $Q$ of side
$2^b$. Normalize Lebesgue measure on $Q$ by $|Q|^{-1}$.
Successively bisect in the $n$ coordinate directions, and repeat
this for $b-a$ generations. This is a fair binary tree of depth
$n(b-a)$: its leaves are exactly the subcubes $q\subset Q$ of
side $2^a$, each of normalized mass $2^{-n(b-a)}$.
Give a terminal leaf the value
\[
 f_q=\frac1{|q|}\int_q f.
\]
For $f\in L^p(\mathbb R^n)$ and $p>1$ these integrals exist by
H\"older on finite-volume cubes; global $L^1$ is not required.
At any intermediate node use the average of its terminal leaf
values. Splitting its equal-mass descendants into two equal halves
proves the midpoint identity. At binary levels $nt$, additivity of
the integral and equality of the leaf volumes identify that node
value \emph{exactly} with the average over the containing cube of
side $2^{b-t}$. Thus the sampled branch values at those levels are
exactly $E_{b-t}f(x)$ for $x\in Q$.

Restricting a path to these levels cannot increase $J$.
The complex binary estimate and Jensen on each terminal cube give
\[
 \begin{split}
 \frac1{|Q|}\int_Q J(E_{b-t}f:0\le t\le b-a)^p
 &\le (2K_p)^p\sum_{q\subset Q}\frac{|q|}{|Q|}|f_q|^p\\
 &\le\frac{(2K_p)^p}{|Q|}\int_Q|f|^p,
 \end{split}
\]
where the sum is over terminal cubes. The Jensen step follows
from $|f_q|\le |q|^{-1}\int_q|f|$ and H\"older on $q$.
Multiplying by $|Q|$ and summing over the countable disjoint cubes
at scale $b$ yields
\[
 \|J(E_jf:a\le j\le b)\|_{L^p(\mathbb R^n)}
 \le2K_p\|f\|_{L^p(\mathbb R^n)}.
\]
Here reversal of the finite order preserves every paired functional:
reverse the list of pairs and interchange their endpoints.
Neither the binary insertion, the normalization, Jensen, nor
root-cube summation has introduced a dimension or length factor.
If $n=0$, all scale averages coincide and the conclusion is immediate.

Finally take $[a,b]=[-L,L]$ and let $L$ increase. Every finite
paired witness on $\mathbb Z$ is contained in one of these windows,
so both their $J$'s and their $\mathcal N$'s increase to the
corresponding full-integer quantities. The cube averages are
measurable step functions; the path norm is a supremum over the
countably many finite integer pair families. For $J$, strict jump
inequalities permit restriction of $\lambda$ to positive rational
numbers, so it too is measurable. Thus monotone convergence gives
the full-integer $J$ estimate with constant $2K_p$, with the
amplitude supremum still inside $L^p$. Applying the pointwise
comparison $J\le\mathcal N\le2J$ gives
\eqref{mod:dyadic-pointwise-jump} with $C_p=4K_p$.
All constants were chosen before the tree, its depth, the dimension,
the root scales, and the input; the displayed numerical bounds give
exactly the asserted range $1<p\le2$.
\end{proof}


\subsection{A continuous-path counterpart}

The finite-tree estimate has a continuous-path counterpart for Brownian
motion stopped at an arbitrary stopping time. Let $B$ be a real standard
Brownian motion starting at zero, with continuous paths and its usual
augmented filtration. We use the strong Markov property and Brownian
scaling; see \cite{RevuzYor}. For deterministic $T>0$, write
$A_T=\mathcal N_{[0,T]}(B)$.

\begin{lemma}[A deterministic-time Brownian tail]
\label{mod:brownian-deterministic}
There are absolute constants $C,c,u_0>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{mod:brownian-deterministic-tail}
 \mathbb P(A_T>u)\le C\exp(-cu^2/T)
 \qquad (u\ge u_0\sqrt T).
\end{equation}
In particular, $A_T$ has moments of every finite positive order.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We first work on a finite deterministic time set
$\mathcal T=\{0=t_0\le t_1\le\cdots\le t_N\le T\}$; repeated times
are harmless.  For $a>0$, start with greedy base $b_0=B_{t_0}$ and
count $G_0=0$.  At step $i$, replace the base by $B_{t_i}$ and increase
the count by one precisely when $|B_{t_i}-b_{i-1}|>a$; otherwise keep
both values.  Write $G_a(\mathcal T)=G_N$.
Every strict paired $2a$-jump on this time set contains a distinct
greedy reset, because without a reset both endpoint values are within
$a$ of the same base.  Thus
\begin{equation}\label{mod:greedy-count}
 N_{2a,\mathcal T}(B)\le G_a(\mathcal T).
\end{equation}

For $s\ge0$, put
$W_i=\cosh(sa)^{G_i}\cosh(s(B_{t_i}-b_i))$.
The reset rule and monotonicity of $\cosh$ on $[0,\infty)$ give
\[
 W_i\le\cosh(sa)^{G_{i-1}}
             \cosh(s(B_{t_i}-b_{i-1})).
\]
If $Z$ is a centered Gaussian of variance $v$, its exponential moment
formula gives
$\mathbb E\cosh(s(u+Z))=\cosh(su)e^{vs^2/2}$.
The increment $B_{t_i}-B_{t_{i-1}}$ is independent of the entire finite
past, including its measurable greedy state.  Induction therefore gives
$\mathbb E W_N\le e^{s^2T/2}$, with all quantities integrable at each
finite step.  On $\{G_a(\mathcal T)\ge m\}$ one has
$W_N\ge\cosh(sa)^m$.  Markov's inequality and
$\cosh(sa)\ge e^{sa}/2$, followed by $s=ma/T$, yield
\begin{equation}\label{mod:greedy-tail}
 \mathbb P(G_a(\mathcal T)\ge m)
 \le2^m\exp\!\left(-\frac{m^2a^2}{2T}\right).
\end{equation}
This argument uses only deterministic-time independent Gaussian
increments, not a stopping-time or strong-Markov assertion.

If $\mathbf J_{\mathcal T}(B)>u>0$, choose a strict $\lambda$-jump
witness with $M$ pairs and $\lambda\sqrt M>u$.  For
$j=\lfloor\log_4 M\rfloor$, there are at least $4^j$ pairs and
$\lambda>u/(2\cdot2^j)$.  With $a_j=u/(4\cdot2^j)$,
\eqref{mod:greedy-count} gives $G_{a_j}(\mathcal T)\ge4^j$.
Consequently,
\[
 \mathbb P(\mathbf J_{\mathcal T}(B)>u)
 \le\sum_{j\ge0}2^{4^j}
       \exp\!\left(-\frac{4^ju^2}{32T}\right)
 \le2\exp\!\left(-\frac{u^2}{64T}\right)
 \quad\text{if }u^2\ge64T\log2.
\]
For the last inequality put $v=u^2/(32T)\ge2\log2$.
Each summand is at most $e^{-4^jv/2}$, hence at most
$e^{-v/2}2^{-j}$ because $4^j\ge j+1$.
The bound is uniform in the finite time set.  Exhaust a countable dense
subset of $[0,T]$ by increasing finite sets and use continuity from
below for the superlevel events.  Continuity of the Brownian paths and
the finite strict-witness inequalities identify the resulting jump
functional with the one on the whole interval.  This also supplies its
measurability.  By \eqref{mod:martingale-NJ}, $A_T\le2\mathbf J_{[0,T]}(B)$,
so
\[
 \mathbb P(A_T>u)\le2e^{-u^2/(256T)}
 \qquad(u\ge16\sqrt T).
\]
The finite-pair norm itself is measurable by the same dense-time
approximation of finite families.  Its tail implies almost-sure
finiteness and, for every $u>0$, the convenient bound
$\mathbb P(A_T>u)\le2e\,e^{-u^2/(256T)}$.
Layer cake and $u=\sqrt T\,v$ now give, for every $p>0$,
\[
 \mathbb E A_T^p\le
 \left(2ep\int_0^\infty v^{p-1}e^{-v^2/256}\,dv\right)T^{p/2}<\infty.
\]
The integral is finite at zero since $p>0$ and at infinity by Gaussian
decay.  This proves both claims with absolute tail constants.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Continuity of the prefix norm]
\label{mod:brownian-prefix}
Outside one null set, the process
$A_t=\mathcal N_{[0,t]}(B)$, $t\ge0$, is finite, increasing, and
continuous. It is adapted.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Continuity of $B$ permits rational endpoints in every finite witness
on a deterministic compact interval, with its right endpoint included
when necessary. This proves measurability and adaptedness of $A_t$.
It is increasing by inclusion of parameter intervals.

Fix an integer $M\ge1$, let $h_k=2^{-k}$, and cover all intervals
$[s,t]\subset[0,M]$ of length at most $h_k$ by the deterministic
intervals $[jh_k,(j+2)h_k]$, $0\le j\le\lceil M/h_k\rceil$.
Brownian stationary increments and Lemma~\ref{mod:brownian-deterministic}
give, for sufficiently large fixed $L$,
\[
 \sum_{k\ge1}\mathbb P\!\left(
  \max_{0\le j\le\lceil M/h_k\rceil}
  \mathcal N_{[jh_k,(j+2)h_k]}(B)
      >L\sqrt{k h_k}\right)<\infty.
\]
Indeed the number of intervals is $O_M(2^k)$, whereas each probability
is at most $C\exp(-cL^2k/2)$. The Borel--Cantelli lemma implies that
the norm of every subinterval of $[0,M]$ of length at most $h_k$
is at most $L\sqrt{k h_k}$ for all sufficiently large $k$.
By \eqref{mod:martingale-concatenation-eq},
\[
 0\le A_t-A_s\le\mathcal N_{[s,t]}(B)\qquad(0\le s\le t\le M).
\]
It follows that $A$ is uniformly continuous on $[0,M]$. Its finiteness
there follows from finiteness of $A_M$. Intersect over integer $M$.
Modify $A$ to be zero on the resulting null exceptional set. The
usual augmented filtration contains that null set at time zero, so
the modification is still adapted and has continuous paths.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Brownian jump control up to a stopping time]
\label{mod:brownian-stopped}
For every $p>0$ there is $C_p<\infty$ such that, for every stopping
time $\tau$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:brownian-stopped-bound}
 \mathbb E\bigl[\mathcal N_{[0,\tau]}(B)^p\bigr]
 \le C_p\mathbb E\left[\sup_{0\le t\le\tau}|B_t|^p\right].
\end{equation}
Both sides are allowed to be infinite. On $\{\tau=\infty\}$ the
left side is the supremum over all finite-time witnesses.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We use the continuous adapted version of $A_t$ from
Lemma~\ref{mod:brownian-prefix}; it agrees with the original finite-witness
norm simultaneously at all finite times outside one null set. Write
$M_\tau=\sup_{0\le t\le\tau}|B_t|$.
For a continuous path $W$, define the integer exit envelope
\[
 E_n(W)=\{|W_j|\le1\text{ for every integer }0\le j\le n\},
 \qquad
 H(W)=\sup_{n\ge0}\mathbf1_{E_n(W)}\mathcal N_{[0,n+1]}(W).
\]
If $|W_t|\le1$ for $0\le t\le T<\infty$, then
$\mathcal N_{[0,T]}(W)\le H(W)$: take $n=\lfloor T\rfloor$.
The envelope is measurable by the countable dense-time representation
of each finite-interval norm.

Let $Z$ be a standard normal variable and set
$q_0=\mathbb P(|Z|\le2)<1$. On $E_n(B)$ all $n$ successive unit
increments have modulus at most two. Independence of the Brownian
increments therefore gives $\mathbb P(E_n(B))\le q_0^n$.
If $E_n(B)$ fails, every nonzero term in $H(B)$ has index less
than $n$, so $H(B)\le A_{n+1}$. The explicit bound used to prove
\eqref{mod:brownian-deterministic-tail} consequently gives
\[
 \mathbb P(H(B)>u)
 \le q_0^n+2\exp\!\left(-\frac{u^2}{256(n+1)}\right)
 \qquad\bigl(u\ge16\sqrt{n+1}\bigr).
\]
In particular, with the numerical choices
\[
 R_n=32(n+1),\qquad
 b_n=q_0^n+2e^{-4(n+1)},\qquad
 \delta_n=\frac1{64(n+1)},
\]
we have $\mathbb P(H(B)>R_n)\le b_n$ and $b_n\to0$.
Thus $H(B)$ is finite almost surely. At these scales write
$q(\delta_n)=b_n$ for the small-probability factor. Fix $p>0$, and choose the least
$n=n(p)$ with $b_n<2^{-p}$, independently of the process and
stopping time. Put $R=R_n$, $b=b_n$, and $\delta=\delta_n$.

First suppose $\tau\le m$ for some deterministic $m\ge1$. For $\lambda>0$
let $\sigma=\inf\{t\ge0:A_t\ge\lambda\}$ and
$\widehat\sigma=\sigma\wedge m$. These are stopping times, and
$E=\{\sigma\le\tau\}=\{A_\tau\ge\lambda\}$ belongs to the sigma
field at $\widehat\sigma$, since $\tau\le m$.
Set $a=2\delta\lambda=\lambda/R$. The strong Markov property at the
finite time $\widehat\sigma$ and Brownian scaling show that
\[
 W_t=a^{-1}\bigl(B_{\widehat\sigma+a^2t}-B_{\widehat\sigma}\bigr)
\]
is a standard Brownian motion independent of that sigma field.
On $\{A_\tau>2\lambda,\ M_\tau\le\delta\lambda\}$ we have
$\widehat\sigma=\sigma\le\tau$ and $A_\sigma=\lambda$, with no
overshoot. Concatenation and the maximal bound give
\[
 \mathcal N_{[\sigma,\tau]}(B)>\lambda,
 \qquad |B_t-B_\sigma|\le2\delta\lambda=a
 \quad(\sigma\le t\le\tau).
\]
With $T=(\tau-\sigma)/a^2$, positive linear time change and
homogeneity therefore imply
\[
 H(W)\ge\mathcal N_{[0,T]}(W)
       =a^{-1}\mathcal N_{[\sigma,\tau]}(B)>R.
\]
The event under consideration is thus contained, up to a null set,
in $E\cap\{H(W)>R\}$. The envelope tail and independence yield
\[
 \mathbb P(A_\tau>2\lambda,\ M_\tau\le\delta\lambda)
 \le b\,\mathbb P(A_\tau\ge\lambda),
\]
and hence
\begin{equation}\label{mod:brownian-goodlambda}
 \mathbb P(A_\tau>2\lambda)
 \le\mathbb P(M_\tau>\delta\lambda)
     +b\,\mathbb P(A_\tau\ge\lambda).
\end{equation}
Since $A_\tau\le A_m$, its $p$th moment is finite by
Lemma~\ref{mod:brownian-deterministic}. Multiply
\eqref{mod:brownian-goodlambda} by $p\lambda^{p-1}$ and integrate.
The strict and non-strict layer-cake formulas give the same moment,
including when the distribution has atoms, so
\[
 2^{-p}\mathbb E A_\tau^p
 \le\delta^{-p}\mathbb E M_\tau^p+b\,\mathbb E A_\tau^p.
\]
Absorbing the finite last term proves the bounded-stopping estimate
with the positive finite, process-independent constant
\[
 C_p=\frac{\delta_{n(p)}^{-p}}{2^{-p}-b_{n(p)}}.
\]
No finiteness assumption on $\mathbb E M_\tau^p$ is needed: if it
is infinite the inequality is automatic.

For an arbitrary extended stopping time, set $\tau_m=\tau\wedge m$.
Every finite witness in $[0,\tau]$ has all its endpoints in
$[0,\tau_m]$ for some integer $m$, even on $\{\tau=\infty\}$.
Consequently the original quantities satisfy exactly
\[
 \mathcal N_{[0,\tau]}(B)=\sup_m\mathcal N_{[0,\tau_m]}(B),
 \qquad M_\tau=\sup_m M_{\tau_m},
\]
with both sequences increasing. The stopped quantities at each finite
horizon are measurable on the completed space, by adapted continuous
paths and countable dense-time approximation. They, and then their
countable suprema above, are measurable up to null sets for the original
measure; their nonnegative expectations are unchanged by completion.
Apply the bounded estimate to $\tau_m$ and use monotone convergence.
This gives \eqref{mod:brownian-stopped-bound} for every $p>0$, with
both expectations permitted to be infinite and with the original
finite-witness interpretation at infinity.
\end{proof}


\section{Uniform annular estimates and rough correlations}\label{mod:sec-analysis}
Throughout this section the hypotheses of \eqref{mod:cone-data} hold and
$\|\Theta\|_\infty\le1$, $\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}\le1$.
All compact radial support intervals and all
Euclidean enlargements are fixed. Their constants may depend on $c_0$.


Fix $1\le\rho<\infty$. Let $\Theta\in L^\infty(\mathbb S^{n-1})$ have
integral zero, let $\|A\|\le1$, and suppose
\[
 \operatorname{supp}\Theta\subset\{\theta:|A\theta|\ge c_0\},
 \qquad 0<c_0<1.
\]
Write $g_A(\theta)=|A\theta|$. Every integrand containing
$g_A(\theta)^{-1}$ is restricted to $\{g_A\ge c_0\}$ and is
defined to be zero elsewhere. Let $b$ be a fixed bounded measurable
function on $(0,\infty)$ of finite $\rho$-variation. The radial function
is fixed throughout every phase supremum. By homogeneity, the proofs
below assume
\[
 \|\Theta\|_\infty\le1,
 \qquad \|b\|_\infty+V^\rho(b;(0,\infty))\le1.
\]
The constants may depend on $n,c_0,\rho$, and subsequently on the
polynomial degree and the spatial exponent, but not on $A$, the radial
location of the variation, any phase coefficient, or finite cutoffs.
A fixed right-continuous representative of $b$ may be used. All the
integrals are Lebesgue integrals; no Stieltjes measure associated with
$b$ is introduced.

\subsection{A one-dimensional oscillatory estimate}

\begin{lemma}\label{radialmod:linear-vdc}
Let $I\subset I_0$, where $I_0$ is a fixed bounded interval. If $a$ is bounded
and $V^\rho(a;I)<\infty$, then
\[
 \left|\int_I e^{i\lambda s}a(s)\,ds\right|
 \le C_{I_0,\rho}(\|a\|_\infty+V^\rho(a;I))
       \min\{1,|\lambda|^{-1/\rho}\}.
\]
The same conclusion holds with $e^{i\lambda s}$ replaced by $e^{i\phi(s)}$
when $\phi'$ is monotone and $|\phi'|\ge L\ge1$, with $L^{-1/\rho}$
on the right.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Only $L\ge1$ needs proof. Partition $I$ into consecutive half-open
intervals $I_m$, with the final endpoint included in the last interval,
of length at most $L^{-1}$ and at least $(2L)^{-1}$ except for at most
one final interval. There are at most $C_{I_0}L$ such intervals. Choose
one sample $s_m$ from each interval, in increasing order. Since the
intervals have disjoint interiors, concatenation of finite partitions
and passage to the supremum give
\[
 \sum_mV^\rho(a;I_m)^\rho\le V^\rho(a;I)^\rho.
\]
Endpoint choices can equivalently be treated by taking interior limits;
the integral does not depend on their values. Consequently the error
in replacing $a$ by $a(s_m)$ is at most
\[
 \sum_m|I_m|\operatorname{osc}_{I_m}a
 \le L^{-1}(C_{I_0}L)^{1-1/\rho}V^\rho(a;I)
 \le C L^{-1/\rho}V^\rho(a;I).
\]
Let $w_m=\int_{I_m}e^{i\phi(s)}\,ds$. The first derivative van der
Corput estimate gives $|\sum_{m=u}^vw_m|\le C L^{-1}$ for every
consecutive block. This estimate follows directly by integrating
$e^{i\phi}=(i\phi')^{-1}(e^{i\phi})'$ and using monotonicity of
$1/\phi'$; thus its constant is independent of the upper size of
$\phi'$. Abel summation now gives
\[
 \left|\sum_m a(s_m)w_m\right|
 \le C L^{-1}\left(\|a\|_\infty+
               \sum_m|a(s_{m+1})-a(s_m)|\right)
 \le C L^{-1/\rho}(\|a\|_\infty+V^\rho(a;I)).
\]
Combining the two estimates proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Weighted partial shells}

For $j\in\mathbb Z$ and $1\le u\le2$, set
\[
 \nu^{A,b}_{j,u}(z)
 =K_\Theta(z)b(|Az|)\mathbf1_{\{2^ju<|Az|\le2^{j+1}\}}.
\]
Write $\nu_j=\nu^{A,b}_{j,1}$. Polar coordinates and the substitution
$v=r g_A(\theta)$ give the exact Fourier identity
\begin{equation}\label{mod:shell-multiplier}
 \widehat\nu^{A,b}_{j,u}(\xi)
 =\int_{\mathbb S^{n-1}}\Theta(\theta)
       \int_u^2 b(2^jv)
       e^{-i2^jv\theta\cdot\xi/g_A(\theta)}\,\frac{dv}{v}
       \,d\sigma(\theta).
\end{equation}
In particular, every partial shell has zero mass. The cancellation is
valid for arbitrary $b$ because the inner integral at $\xi=0$ is
independent of $\theta$.

\begin{lemma}\label{mod:shell-envelope}
Put $s=2^j|\xi|$ and $\alpha=1/(2\rho)$. Then
\begin{align}
 \sup_{1\le u\le2}|\widehat\nu^{A,b}_{j,u}(\xi)|
  &\le C_\rho\min\{s,s^{-\alpha}\},\label{mod:shell-envelope-eq}\\
 \esssup_{1\le u\le2}|\partial_u\widehat\nu^{A,b}_{j,u}(\xi)|
  &\le C\min\{s,1\}.
 \label{mod:shell-derivative-eq}
\end{align}
The derivative is the derivative of an absolutely continuous function
of $u$. The assertions are uniform in $j,A$, and the location of the
variation of $b$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The low-frequency estimate follows by subtracting the zero-frequency
value in \eqref{mod:shell-multiplier}, using $g_A\ge c_0$ on the angular
support and $|e^{it}-1|\le|t|$. The product rule for variation and
monotone composition show that
\[
 \|v\mapsto b(2^jv)/v\|_{L^\infty([1,2])}
 +V^\rho(v\mapsto b(2^jv)/v;[1,2])\le C_\rho.
\]
Lemma~\ref{radialmod:linear-vdc} therefore bounds the inner integral in
\eqref{mod:shell-multiplier} by
\[
 C_\rho\min\{1,(s|e\cdot\theta|)^{-1/\rho}\},
 \qquad e=\xi/|\xi|.
\]
For $n\ge2$ the spherical measure of
$\{\theta:|e\cdot\theta|<s^{-1/2}\}$ is at most $C_ns^{-1/2}$ when
$s\ge4$. On its complement the preceding bound is at most
$C_\rho s^{-1/(2\rho)}$. This proves the asserted high-frequency
estimate. For $n=1$ there is no small-projection set and the stronger
bound $C_\rho s^{-1/\rho}$ applies. The remaining bounded range of $s$
is absorbed in the constant.

Differentiation of the lower endpoint in the Lebesgue integral yields
for almost every $u$
\[
 \partial_u\widehat\nu^{A,b}_{j,u}(\xi)
 =-\frac{b(2^ju)}u\int\Theta(\theta)
   e^{-i2^ju\theta\cdot\xi/g_A(\theta)}\,d\sigma(\theta).
\]
The trivial estimate gives the upper bound by a constant. Subtraction
of $1$ from the exponential, followed by angular cancellation, gives
the upper bound $Cs$.
\end{proof}

The same conclusions hold for any fixed ratio of shell endpoints and
for a fixed lacunary sequence $R_j=D^j$, after splitting each shell into
a fixed number of dyadic shells. Constants may depend on $D$; the
positive exponent $1/(2\rho)$ remains available.

\subsection{The static unphased conical operator}

\begin{lemma}[Static conical bound]\label{mod:static-conical-radial}
Under the normalized hypotheses of this section, the unphased static
operator $S^{0,A,b}_\Theta$ is bounded on $L^p(\mathbb R^n)$ for every
$1<p<\infty$, with norm at most $C_{n,p,c_0,\rho}$. On smooth compactly
supported inputs it equals the principal value of the conical integral.
The constants are independent of the least singular value of $A$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
