Choose a smooth radial function $\chi$ equal to one on $|\xi|\le1$
and zero on $|\xi|\ge2$, and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dual-cutoff-reproduction}
 \psi(\xi)=\chi(\xi)-\chi(2\xi),\qquad
 \widetilde\psi(\xi)=\psi(\xi/2)+\psi(\xi)+\psi(2\xi)
                  =\chi(\xi/2)-\chi(4\xi).
\end{equation}
Then $\supp\psi\subset\{1/2\le|\xi|\le2\}$,
$\supp\widetilde\psi\subset\{1/4\le|\xi|\le4\}$, and
$\widetilde\psi=1$ on $\supp\psi$. Telescoping gives
$\sum_{m\in\mathbb Z}\psi(2^{-m}\xi)=1$ for $\xi\ne0$;
this is a dual-cutoff resolution, not a square-partition assertion.
Let $\Delta_{m,j}$ and $\widetilde\Delta_{m,j}$ have multipliers
$\psi(2^{j-m}\xi)$ and $\widetilde\psi(2^{j-m}\xi)$, respectively. Thus
\[
 \widetilde\Delta_{m,j}\Delta_{m,j}=\Delta_{m,j},\qquad
 f=\sum_m\widetilde\Delta_{m,j}\Delta_{m,j}f
 \quad\hbox{in }L^p,\quad 1<p<\infty.
\]
Indeed, finite sums telescope to differences of smooth low-pass
operators; their limits at small and large physical scales are $f$ and
zero in $L^p$, respectively. Inverse Fourier transformation gives
Schwartz kernels for both cutoffs, and the multiplier identity gives
exact convolution reproduction. With the convention
$\widehat f_{\rm ang}(\xi)=\int e^{-ix\cdot\xi}f(x)\,dx$, the precise
conversion is $\widehat f_{\rm ang}(\xi)=\widehat f_{\rm cyc}(\xi/(2\pi))$;
no normalization factor changes the reproduction identity. For finite shell sums, define
\[
 S_m f=\sum_j\nu_j*\widetilde\Delta_{m,j}\Delta_{m,j}f.
\]
Every summand has Fourier support in the annulus
$|\xi|\simeq2^{m-j}$; these annuli have bounded overlap as $j$ varies.
Plancherel and Lemma~\ref{mod:shell-envelope} imply
\begin{equation}\label{radialmod:static-l2}
 \|S_mf\|_2\le C a_m\|f\|_2,
 \qquad
 a_m=\begin{cases}2^m,&m\le0,\\2^{-\alpha m},&m>0.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The support and size of $\nu_j$ give
$|\nu_j*g|\le C M g$, where $M$ is the Hardy--Littlewood maximal
operator. For every $1<r<\infty$, Littlewood--Paley almost orthogonal
summation and the Fefferman--Stein vector maximal inequality give
\begin{align*}
 \|S_mf\|_r
 &\le C_r\left\|\left(\sum_j|\nu_j*\widetilde\Delta_{m,j}\Delta_{m,j}f|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_r\\
 &\le C_r\left\|\left(\sum_j|M(\widetilde\Delta_{m,j}\Delta_{m,j}f)|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_r
 \le C_r\|f\|_r.
\end{align*}
All these estimates are uniform over finite shell sets. Interpolating
with \eqref{radialmod:static-l2}, choosing $r$ on the other side of a
fixed $p$ from $2$, gives
\[
 \|S_m\|_{p\to p}\le C_{p,\rho}a_m^{\theta_p},\qquad\theta_p>0.
\]
Thus $\sum_mS_m$ converges in the operator norm on $L^p$. The individual
$j$ sums converge by the Littlewood--Paley square-function estimate and
the vector maximal inequality; their tails tend to zero by dominated
convergence in the input Littlewood--Paley square function. These facts
construct a bounded operator $S^{0,A,b}_\Theta$ with
\begin{equation}\label{radialmod:static-bound}
 \|S^{0,A,b}_\Theta f\|_p\le C_{n,p,c_0,\rho}\|f\|_p.
\end{equation}
No static theorem for a homogeneous radial kernel has been applied to
the nonhomogeneous factor $b$.

For smooth compactly supported inputs this operator is the usual
principal value, in the following precise sense. At the origin, subtract $f(x)$ on each partial shell;
its integral against the kernel is zero, and the remaining integrand is
bounded in absolute integral by $C\|\nabla f\|_\infty\,dr$. At infinity
there is no issue on compactly supported inputs for each fixed $x$.
Finite symmetric shell sums converge in $L^p$ by the construction
above, and the preceding cancellation argument gives their pointwise
principal-value limit on smooth compactly supported inputs. A subsequence
of the $L^p$ convergence identifies these two limits almost everywhere.
For $p>1$ every positive truncation is absolutely convergent, since the
kernel restricted to $|Az|>t$ and to the angular support belongs to
$L^{p'}$ with norm at most $C t^{-n/p}$. The construction above also
shows that its dyadic tails converge in $L^p$ and vanish in $L^p$ at
infinity: first fix $m$ and use the input square-function tail, then sum
in $m$ using $a_m^{\theta_p}$. For each positive dyadic cutoff,
absolute convergence of the integral and a subsequence of the $L^p$
shell convergence identify the tail sum with that integral. Taking the
intersection over countably many dyadic cutoffs gives their common
representative.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Comparison of smooth averages with a dyadic martingale}

Let $E_j$ denote conditional expectation on the standard dyadic cubes
of sidelength $2^j$ in $\mathbb R^n$. The scale index is decreasing in
the filtration; reversal of a finite ordered set does not change the
paired-increment norm. Let $\Phi$ be any Schwartz function with
$\int\Phi=1$, and let $\Phi_t=t^{-n}\Phi(t^{-1}\,\cdot)$ for $t>0$.

\begin{lemma}\label{radialmod:smooth-comparison}
For every $1<p<\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{radialmod:comparison-square}
 \left\|\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}
       |\Phi_{2^j}*f-E_jf|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_p
 \le C_{n,p,\Phi}\|f\|_p.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Set $D_m=E_{m-1}-E_m$. Then $D_mf$ is constant on dyadic cubes of
sidelength $2^{m-1}$, and its integral over each dyadic cube of
sidelength $2^m$ is zero. Also $f=\sum_mD_mf$ in $L^p$. Indeed
$E_jf\to f$ in $L^p$ as $j\to-\infty$, by approximation with smooth
functions and the contraction property of $E_j$, and $E_jf\to0$ in
$L^p$ as $j\to\infty$, first for bounded compactly supported functions
and then by density. The orthogonality identity in $L^2$ is
$\sum_m\|D_mf\|_2^2=\|f\|_2^2$.

We first establish
\begin{equation}\label{radialmod:dyadic-almost-orthogonal}
 \|(\Phi_{2^{m+k}}*-E_{m+k})D_mf\|_2
 \le C2^{-|k|/2}\|D_mf\|_2.
\end{equation}
For $k\ge0$, $E_{m+k}D_mf=0$. Write $u=D_mf$, put $s=2^{m+k}$,
and use its zero integral on each dyadic cube $Q$ of sidelength $2^m$:
\[
 \Phi_{2^{m+k}}*u(x)
 =\sum_Q\int_Q\{\Phi_{2^{m+k}}(x-y)-\Phi_{2^{m+k}}(x-c_Q)\}u(y)\,dy.
\]
The mean value theorem and the Schwartz estimates, using
$|y-c_Q|\le C2^m\le Cs$, give
\[
 |\Phi_{2^{m+k}}(x-y)-\Phi_{2^{m+k}}(x-c_Q)|
 \le C_N 2^{-k}s^{-n}(1+|x-y|/s)^{-N}.
\]
For $N>n$, convolution with the positive decreasing envelope is
bounded by $C Mu(x)$. Hence
$\|\Phi_{2^{m+k}}*u\|_2\le C2^{-k}\|u\|_2$.

For $k\le-1$, one has $E_{m+k}u=u$. Put $L=2^{m-1}$ and
$s=2^{m+k}\le L$. A function constant on cubes in the grid of
sidelength $L$ obeys the translation bound
\begin{equation}\label{radialmod:step-translation}
 \|u(\,\cdot-y)-u\|_2
 \le C_n\min\{1,(|y|/L)^{1/2}\}\|u\|_2.
\end{equation}
To verify it for $|y|\le L$, the integrand vanishes unless $x$ and
$x-y$ belong to distinct grid cubes. Inside each cube, this exceptional
set is contained in boundary strips of total volume at most
$C_n(|y|/L)|Q|$. Since $u$ is constant on each cube, integration of
$2|u(x)|^2+2|u(x-y)|^2$ over the exceptional set proves the square of
\eqref{radialmod:step-translation}. The change of variable $x-y=z$
handles the second term with the same bound. For $|y|>L$, translation
invariance of the $L^2$ norm gives the stated trivial bound.
Since $\int\Phi=1$, Minkowski and
\eqref{radialmod:step-translation} give
\begin{align*}
 \|\Phi_{2^{m+k}}*u-u\|_2
 &\le\int |\Phi_{2^{m+k}}(y)|\,\|u(\,\cdot-y)-u\|_2\,dy\\
 &\le C(s/L)^{1/2}\|u\|_2\int|\Phi(z)||z|^{1/2}\,dz
 \le C2^{k/2}\|u\|_2.
\end{align*}
This proves \eqref{radialmod:dyadic-almost-orthogonal} in both ranges.

For fixed $k$, define the linear vector-valued map
\[
 \mathcal G_k f
 =\{(\Phi_{2^{m+k}}*-E_{m+k})D_mf\}_{m\in\mathbb Z}.
\]
By \eqref{radialmod:dyadic-almost-orthogonal} and orthogonality,
\[
 \|\mathcal G_k f\|_{L^2(\ell^2)}
 \le C2^{-|k|/2}\|f\|_2.
\]
For every $1<r<\infty$, the bound $|\Phi_{2^j}*g|\le C M g$,
the identities $E_{m+k}D_mf\in\{0,D_mf\}$, the Fefferman--Stein vector
maximal inequality, and the classical martingale square-function
inequality give
\begin{align*}
 \|\mathcal G_kf\|_{L^r(\ell^2)}
 &\le C\left\|\left(\sum_m|M(D_mf)|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_r
       +\left\|\left(\sum_m|D_mf|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_r\\
 &\le C_r\left\|\left(\sum_m|D_mf|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_r
 \le C_r\|f\|_r.
\end{align*}
All inequalities first hold for finite $m$-sets with uniform constants.
Interpolating the bounds $L^2\to L^2(\ell^2)$ and
$L^r\to L^r(\ell^2)$ for this linear map, taking $r$ beyond a fixed
$p$ from $2$, gives
\[
 \|\mathcal G_kf\|_{L^p(\ell^2)}
 \le C_p2^{-\epsilon_p|k|}\|f\|_p,\qquad\epsilon_p>0.
\]
Finally, the exact coordinate identity
\[
 \Phi_{2^j}*f-E_jf
 =\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}(\Phi_{2^j}*-E_j)D_{j-k}f
\]
holds in $L^p$. The summands, regarded as an $\ell^2(j)$-valued
function, have the norms just estimated after relabeling $j=m+k$.
Minkowski and summation over $k$ prove
\eqref{radialmod:comparison-square}. Convergence in $L^p(\ell^2)$
also justifies the coordinate identity for the resulting sequence.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Smooth pointwise jumps]\label{radialmod:smooth-jump}
For every $1<p<\infty$,
\[
 \|\mathcal N(\Phi_{2^j}*f:j\in\mathbb Z)\|_p\le C_{n,p,\Phi}\|f\|_p.
\]
The same estimate holds for any fixed geometric subsequence
$\Phi_{D^j}*f$ when $D$ is a positive integral power of $2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The paired norm triangle inequality and the elementary sequence bound
give
\[
 \mathcal N(\Phi_{2^j}*f:j)
 \le\mathcal N(E_jf:j)
      +2\left(\sum_j|\Phi_{2^j}*f-E_jf|^2\right)^{1/2}.
\]
The first term is bounded by Theorem~\ref{mod:martingale-pointwise},
using the equivalence of the paired norm with the pointwise jump
functional. The second is bounded by
Lemma~\ref{radialmod:smooth-comparison}. Restriction of an ordered path
to a subsequence can only decrease its paired norm.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The unphased critical-jump estimate}

\begin{proposition}[Unphased conical jumps]\label{mod:unphased}
Under the normalized hypotheses of this section, for every
$1<p<\infty$ and $f\in L^p(\mathbb R^n)$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:unphased-bound}
 \|\NN(S^{0,A,b}_{\Theta,t}f:t>0)\|_p
 \le C_{n,p,c_0,\rho}\|f\|_p.
\end{equation}
The pointwise trajectory is the absolutely convergent positive-radius
integral and tends to zero at infinity. The estimate is uniform in
$A$ and in the location of the variation of $b$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}

Let $F_j=\sum_{k\ge j}\nu_k*f$ and take a smooth low-pass multiplier
$\widehat\Phi$ equal to $1$ near zero and equal to $0$ outside a fixed
ball. Put $S=S^{0,A,b}_\Theta$. Then
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal E_j:=F_j-\Phi_{2^j}*Sf
  &=(I-\Phi_{2^j}*)\sum_{r\ge0}\nu_{j+r}*f
       -\Phi_{2^j}*\sum_{r<0}\nu_{j+r}*f.
\end{align*}
Use the exact resolution
$f=\sum_m\widetilde\Delta_{m,j}\Delta_{m,j}f$ from
\eqref{eq:dual-cutoff-reproduction}. For fixed $m,r$, Plancherel, the shell
envelope, and interpolation with the uniform vector maximal bound yield
\[
 \left\|\{\nu_{j+r}*\widetilde\Delta_{m,j}\Delta_{m,j}f\}_j\right\|_{L^p(\ell^2)}
 \le C_{p,\rho}2^{-c_{p,\rho}|m+r|}\|f\|_p.
\]
Insertion of $I-\Phi_{2^j}*$ or $\Phi_{2^j}*$ preserves the uniform vector
bound. Moreover the first factor vanishes on the support of
$\Delta_{m,j}$ for all sufficiently negative $m$, and the second
vanishes there for all sufficiently positive $m$. Consequently both
double sums
\[
 \sum_{r\ge0}\sum_{m\ge-C}2^{-c|m+r|},
 \qquad
 \sum_{r<0}\sum_{m\le C}2^{-c|m+r|}
\]
converge. Minkowski therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{radialmod:long-error}
 \|\{\mathcal E_j\}_j\|_{L^p(\ell^2)}\le C_{p,\rho}\|f\|_p.
\end{equation}
For every scalar sequence $v_j$, the paired-increment norm satisfies
$\mathcal N(v_j)\le2(\sum_j|v_j|^2)^{1/2}$. Lemma~\ref{radialmod:smooth-jump} gives
\[
 \|\mathcal N(\Phi_{2^j}*g:j\in\mathbb Z)\|_p\le C_p\|g\|_p.
\]
Its proof uses the pointwise martingale theorem
(Theorem~\ref{mod:martingale-pointwise}) and a square-function comparison
proved above. No exchange of a jump-amplitude supremum with an $L^p$
norm is made. Combining this estimate with
\eqref{radialmod:static-bound} and \eqref{radialmod:long-error} controls
the long jumps.

For the short jumps, let $G_{j,u}=\nu^{A,b}_{j,u}*g$. This path is
absolutely continuous in $u$, and $G_{j,2}=0$. On a fixed parameter
interval, the elementary inequalities
\[
 V^2(G)^2\le2\sup|G|\int|G'|,
 \qquad \sup|G|^2\le2\int|G||G'|
\]
imply
\begin{equation}\label{mod:sobolev-variation}
 V^2(G)\le C\|G\|_{L^2_u}^{1/4}\|G'\|_{L^2_u}^{3/4}.
\end{equation}
Applying this pointwise and then H\"older and Plancherel gives, for an
input with Fourier support $|\xi|\simeq2^{m-j}$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:short-frequency}
 \|V^2(\nu^{A,b}_{j,u}*g:u\in[1,2])\|_2
 \le C_\rho b_m\|g\|_2,
 \qquad
 b_m=\begin{cases}2^m,&m\le0,\\2^{-m/(8\rho)},&m>0.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The uniform vector $L^r$ bound follows from the pointwise estimate
\[
 V^2(\nu^{A,b}_{j,u}*g:u)\le V^1(\nu^{A,b}_{j,u}*g:u)
 \le\int_{2^j<|Az|\le2^{j+1}}|K_\Theta(z)|\,|g(x-z)|\,dz
 \le C M g(x).
\]
It is the integral over the full shell that is dominated by the maximal
operator; no pointwise domination of a single spherical endpoint
integral is needed. Summing \eqref{mod:short-frequency} in $j$ in
$L^2(\ell^2)$, interpolating with the vector $L^r$ bound, and summing in
$m$ proves
\[
 \left\|\left(\sum_jV^2(\nu^{A,b}_{j,u}*f:u)^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_p
 \le C_{p,\rho}\|f\|_p.
\]
The deterministic long--short jump inequality in
Lemma~\ref{lem:jumpcalc} now yields the asserted inequality \eqref{mod:unphased-bound}.
Every estimate may first be applied to finite phase-independent paths;
the paired norm is the supremum of finite witnesses. Thus passage to
all positive radii uses monotone convergence over rational witnesses
and local absolute continuity of the integral trajectory.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Coefficient estimates used for radial oscillation}
\begin{lemma}[A polynomial sublevel estimate]\label{mod:sublevel}
On a fixed box in $\R^k$, nonzero polynomials of degree at most $D$ satisfy
\[
 |\{x:|p(x)|\le t\|p\|_{\mathrm{coeff}}\}|
 \le C_{k,D}t^{\alpha_{k,D}},\qquad 0<t<1,
\]
for some $\alpha_{k,D}>0$. The same statement holds for a finite polynomial coefficient vector that
is not identically zero, with its Euclidean norm in place of $|p|$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $D=0$ the assertion is immediate. In one variable with $D\ge1$,
if a subset has measure $\mu$, it contains $D+1$ points
with consecutive separation at least $c_D\mu$. Lagrange interpolation
then bounds the coefficient norm by $C_D\mu^{-D}$ times the maximum of
$|p|$ at these points. This proves the assertion with exponent $1/D$.
For several variables, view $p$ as a polynomial in its last variable.
One coefficient polynomial in the remaining variables has coefficient
norm at least a fixed multiple of $\|p\|_{\mathrm{coeff}}$. Delete where
that coefficient is less than $t^a$ times its norm, apply the induction
hypothesis there, and apply the one-dimensional result on all remaining
sections. Choose a fixed $a\in(0,1)$ to balance the two powers.
For a vector polynomial $P(x)=\sum_\beta a_\beta x^\beta$, let $M=M(k,D)$
be the number of allowed monomials and choose $a_{\beta_0}$ of largest
Euclidean norm. Then
$\|a_{\beta_0}\|\ge M^{-1/2}\|P\|_{\mathrm{coeff}}$.
Projecting onto $u=a_{\beta_0}/\|a_{\beta_0}\|$ gives the scalar
polynomial $q=\langle u,P\rangle$, whose $\beta_0$ coefficient equals
$\|a_{\beta_0}\|$, while $|q(x)|\le\|P(x)\|$.
Thus the vector sublevel set is contained in the scalar sublevel set
at threshold $t\sqrt M$. Apply the scalar estimate when $t\sqrt M<1$
and the trivial box bound otherwise. After increasing the constant,
this proves the claim independently of the number of vector components.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Polynomial radial oscillation with $V^{q_*}$ profiles]
\label{mod:radial-vdc}
Let $d\ge1$ and let $S$ be a real polynomial on a fixed annulus, of degree at most $d$,
with nonconstant coefficient norm $\Lambda$. Let $v$ have bounded
$L^\infty+V^{q_*}$ norm and fixed compact radial support. If $A_0$ is Lipschitz
on the annulus, then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:radial-vdc-eq}
 \left|\int e^{iS(z)}K_\Theta(z)v(|Az|)A_0(z)\,dz\right|
 \le C(1+\Lambda)^{-\kappa}
 (\|A_0\|_\infty+\operatorname{Lip}(A_0)),
\end{equation}
for some $\kappa>0$. Mean zero of $\Theta$ is not needed in this lemma.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a one-variable phase of bounded degree and coefficient size $G$,
the BV oscillatory bound follows by applying Lemma~\ref{mod:sublevel}
to its derivative, followed by integration by parts on the boundedly
many derivative-monotonicity intervals. It has the form
$C(1+G)^{-c}(\|v\|_\infty+\operatorname{Var}v)$.
For $0<u<1$, a normalized finite-$q_*$-variation amplitude admits
a step approximation $v_u$ with uniform error at most $2u$, at most
$C u^{-q_*}$ intervals, and
\[
 \|v_u\|_\infty+\operatorname{Var}(v_u)
 \le C(1+u^{1-q_*}).
\]
One obtains it by recording consecutive values whenever their distance
from the last recorded value reaches $u$; one-sided limits are used at
discontinuities. Every retained nonzero increment has modulus at least
$u$, their $q_*$-powers sum to at most a fixed constant, and therefore
their moduli sum to at most $u^{1-q_*}$ times that constant. There are
only finitely many retained increments; interval endpoints are
irrelevant for the Lebesgue integral. The bounded-variation estimate
and the approximation error give
$C[(1+G)^{-c}u^{1-q_*}+u]$. Taking
$u=(1+G)^{-c/q_*}$ gives a positive power depending only on the degree
and $q_*$. A detailed equivalent finite-variation argument is given in
Lemma~\ref{lem:allq-first-derivative} and
Corollary~\ref{cor:allq-polynomial-vdc}.

Write $S(r\theta)=\sum_{k=0}^d r^kS_k(\theta)$. At least one $k\ge1$
has $\|S_k\|_{\mathrm{coeff}}\ge c\Lambda$. On fixed rational sphere
charts, the restriction of a nonzero homogeneous polynomial is injective
in coefficients after clearing the fixed denominator. Lemma~\ref{mod:sublevel}
therefore bounds the directions where its size is smaller than
$\Lambda u$ by $Cu^\alpha$. Elsewhere the radial polynomial has coefficient
size at least $c\Lambda u$. Along each ray $|Az|=rg_A(\theta)$, and
monotone composition preserves $V^{q_*}$. The multiplier $r^{-1}A_0(r\theta)$
has the required uniform variation on the fixed radial interval. Apply
the one-variable estimate, bound the bad directions absolutely, and
optimize $u$. This proves \eqref{mod:radial-vdc-eq}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Equal-shell correlations for conical profiles}
\begin{proposition}[Conical equal-shell correlation]\label{mod:equal-correlation}
Let $d\ge1$. For two profiles $a,c$ of uniformly bounded $L^\infty+V^{q_*}$ norm, supported
in a fixed compact subset of $(0,\infty)$, put
\[
 k_a^A(z)=K_\Theta(z)a(|Az|),\qquad
 k_c^A(z)=K_\Theta(z)c(|Az|).
\]
There are $C,\gamma_*>0$, depending on $n,d,c_0,q_*$ and the fixed
support annuli but independent of the matrix degeneration, such that
\begin{equation}\label{mod:equal-correlation-eq}
 \left|\int e^{iS(z)}\overline{k_a^A(z)}k_c^A(z-h)\,dz\right|
 \le C\min\{1,(\Lambda|h|^d)^{-\gamma_*}\}
\end{equation}
when $n\ge2$. Here $\Lambda$ is the nonconstant coefficient norm of $S$,
and the right side is interpreted as $C$ if $h=0$ or $\Lambda=0$.
For $n=1$ it may be replaced by $C\min\{1,\Lambda^{-\gamma_*}\}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
We give the extension of Lemma~\ref{lem:rough-correlation} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:allq-rough-correlation}; their rational phase and
coefficient estimates do not use the shape of the radial profile.
By \eqref{mod:cone-data}, both Euclidean radii lie in a fixed compact
annulus. Insert a smooth Euclidean radial cutoff equal to one there.
This is an exact insertion and fixes all Euclidean Jacobian support
bounds independently of $A$.

For $h\ne0$, use the two-ray coordinates in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:rough-correlation}. On a normalized transverse block the
profile arguments become
\[
 \gamma_1(X,Y)=\frac{|A\mathcal O(Ue_1+\alpha\omega)|}{|q(X,Y)|},
 \qquad
 \gamma_2(X,Y)=\frac{|A\mathcal O(Ve_1+\alpha\omega)|}{|q(X,Y)|},
\]
where $U=U_0+HX$, $V=V_0+HY$, $q=\Delta+X-Y$, and $q$ has fixed sign and
is bounded away from zero on the enlarged block. The square of either
numerator is a nonnegative quadratic polynomial, say $p_A(U)$. A
horizontal derivative can vanish only where
\[
 \tfrac H2p_A'(U)q-p_A(U)=0.
\]
This equation has bounded degree. The zeros of $p_A$ add at most two
break points; identically zero sections are constant. The other sections
are treated identically. Thus both arguments have uniformly bounded
numbers of monotonicity intervals. On each such interval,
$V^{q_*}(a\circ\gamma_i)\le V^{q_*}(a)$, with zero extension accounting for support
crossings. The product variation inequality and the fixed smooth Euclidean
cutoffs now give uniformly bounded sectional $V^{q_*}$ norms for the
complete amplitude. This verifies the amplitude and Schur hypotheses
of Lemma~\ref{lem:allq-rational-operator}, used with exponent $q_*$.

For clarity, the phase lower bound is unchanged. With
$\varphi=\sum_{m=0}^d\varphi_m$ in homogeneous pieces and
$f_m(X)=\varphi_m(U_0+HX,\alpha)$, its pullback satisfies
\[
 q^{d+2}\partial_X\partial_Y
 \left(\sum_{m=0}^df_m(X)q^{-m}\right)
 =\sum_{m=1}^dq^{d-m}\{mqf_m'-m(m+1)f_m\}.
\]
The coefficient of $q^{d-m}$ is
$-m(m+1)f_m+(m+1)f_{m+1}'$, with $f_{d+1}=0$. Backward recovery of the
$f_m$ gives a uniform inverse on the fixed coefficient spaces. Affine
substitution then gives the factor $(\alpha H)^d$ in
Lemma~\ref{lem:equal-pullback}. The matrix $A$ does not occur in this
calculation.

The one-variable rational estimate and its $TT^*$ proof in
Lemma~\ref{lem:allq-rational-operator} now apply. The rough angular factors
remain respectively functions of the two ray variables; they are used as
bounded $L^2$ input and output functions and are not differentiated.
The transverse-band bound is
\[
 C\alpha^{n-1}\int_{\Sph^{n-2}}
 \min\{1,[\Gamma(\omega)(\alpha H)^d]^{-c_1}\}\,d\omega.
\]
The chart coefficient estimate and polynomial sublevel bound used in
Lemma~\ref{lem:rough-correlation} give a positive power in
$\Lambda(\alpha H)^d$. Summing dyadic $\alpha$ gives
\eqref{mod:equal-correlation-eq}, after decreasing the exponent.
The cases $h=0$ and constant $S$ use the size bound. In dimension one,
a bounded partition into signed overlap intervals reduces directly to
the one-variable $V^{q_*}$ estimate.
\end{proof}



\section{A fixed polynomial tile geometry}\label{mod:sec-geometry}
For $d=0$, Theorem~\ref{mod:cone} is Proposition~\ref{mod:unphased}, since constant phases do not affect the path norm. In this section and the ensuing polynomial tile analysis assume $d\ge1$.

This section specifies the external geometric input. The analytic
estimates for its operators are not imported from a smooth-kernel
Carleson theorem.

Fix first the analytic exponent $\beta=1/(16\rho)$, furnished by
the shell estimates and the single-tree proof below independently of
the grid base. Choose a fixed integer $K$ with $K\beta\ge2$.
Then choose one sufficiently large structural integer $a\ge4$ for the
Euclidean space and the polynomial space $\PP_d$, with ball metric given
by the oscillation of the difference of two polynomials. The
finite-dimensional norm comparisons and Lemma~\ref{mod:sublevel} verify
the compatibility and polynomial cancellation conditions of
\cite{BDJST}. Set $D=2^{100a^2}$. Increasing $a$ once, before any finite
cutoffs are imposed, is permitted. It will never depend on a smoothing
parameter, an angular atom, or a density generation.

At scale $j$ use the disjoint radial shell
\begin{equation}\label{mod:raw-shell}
 c_0D^j/(4D)<\rho_A(z)\le c_0D^j/4.
\end{equation}
These shells partition the positive radial axis. On the angular support,
\[
 c_0D^j/(4D)<|z|\le D^j/4,
 \qquad |k_{j,x}(z)|\le C D^{-jn},
\]
where $k_{j,x}=K_\Theta b(\rho_A)h_x(\rho_A)\mathbf1_{\text{shell }j}$.
The wider fixed shell ratio only changes structural constants in
Section~\ref{mod:sec-analysis}.

Fix bounded sets $F,G$ and a finite-range measurable phase choice $Q_x$.
The finite spatial grid and nested polynomial cells are those of
\cite[Proposition 2.1]{BDJST}. A tile $p$ consists of a spatial cube
$I_p$, a scale $s(p)$, a phase cell $\Omega_p$, and its center polynomial
$Q_p$. Write $r_p=D^{s(p)}$ and $c_p=c(I_p)$. In particular,
\[
 B(c_p,r_p/4)\subset I_p\subset B(c_p,4r_p).
\]
The polynomial metric $d_p$ is the oscillation metric on $B(c_p,r_p)$.
Changing this ball by any one fixed factor changes constants only.
Phase cells at a fixed spatial cube partition the finite phase range;
nested spatial cubes have nested or disjoint corresponding phase cells.
The order is $p\le p'$ if $I_p\subset I_{p'}$ and
$\Omega_{p'}\subset\Omega_p$.

\paragraph{Same-family nets and full ambient phase cells.}
The finite-net selection in the proof of the geometric input must be
made in the full ambient phase metric, not merely after intersecting
balls with the given finite range $\mathcal Q$ of the phase choice.
For any such finite family and any one ball metric $d$, choose a maximal
$3/5$-separated subfamily $Z\subset\mathcal Q$. Then every member of
$\mathcal Q$ has distance strictly less than $3/5$ from $Z$, and the full
ambient open $3/10$ balls about distinct centers in $Z$ are disjoint.
Both assertions follow directly from maximality and the triangle
inequality. The centers remain in the original family. In contrast,
disjointness only after intersecting with $\mathcal Q$ is insufficient:
for $\mathcal Q=\{0,2/5\}\subset\R$ the two ambient radius-$3/10$
balls meet at $1/5$, although their intersections with $\mathcal Q$ are
disjoint. This corrects the intermediate selection step in
\cite[Lemma 3.2 and (3.1)]{BDJST}, without changing the geometric
statement being used.

Enumerate $Z=\{z_i\}$. Assign each point at distance less than $7/10$
from $Z$ to its nearest center, resolving ties by the least index, and
call the resulting ambient cells $V_i$. They are disjoint, cover
$\mathcal Q$, and satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ambient-base-cells}
 B_d(z_i,3/10)\subset V_i\subset B_d(z_i,7/10).
\end{equation}
For the first inclusion, the triangle inequality and separation show
that every other center is strictly farther away. The cells are
measurable whenever the distance coordinates are measurable, since
their definition uses finitely many strict or non-strict comparisons.

We construct the nested phase cells recursively along each ball chain.
Number levels from coarse to fine, write $d_k$ for their polynomial
oscillation metrics, and suppose
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ball-contraction}
 d_{k+1}(P,Q)\le\tfrac1{10}d_k(P,Q)\quad\hbox{for every }P,Q.
\end{equation}
At each level choose the preceding separated net from the same
$\mathcal Q$, with centers $z_{k,i}$ and base cells $V_{k,i}$. Start with
$C_{0,i}=V_{0,i}$ and define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ambient-refinement}
 C_{k+1,i}=B_{d_{k+1}}(z_{k+1,i},1/5)
 \ \cup\!\!\bigcup_{j:\,z_{k,j}\in V_{k+1,i}} C_{k,j}.
\end{equation}
Induction gives pairwise disjoint cells at each level, coverage of
$\mathcal Q$, and
\[
 B_{d_k}(z_{k,i},1/5)\subset C_{k,i}\subset B_{d_k}(z_{k,i},1).
\]
Indeed, each inherited cell has radius less than $1/10$ in $d_{k+1}$
about its old center. An intersection with a new $1/5$ core forces that
old center into the corresponding $3/10$ core and hence into the same
base cell. This proves disjointness; the triangle inequality gives
outer radius less than $1/10+7/10<1$. Each old center belongs to
$\mathcal Q$, hence is assigned, proving coverage and containment of
each old cell in a new one. Iteration gives nesting or disjointness at
arbitrary depth. Measurability propagates by finite unions.

For polynomial oscillation metrics, the contraction is uniform along
ball chains with bounded center displacement. More precisely, for fixed
degree, dimension, and $A\ge0$, one can choose an integer $J$ such that
\[
 c_{k+1}=c_k+r_k a_k,\qquad
 r_{k+1}=r_k/2^J,\qquad |(a_k)_i|\le A
\]
implies \eqref{eq:ball-contraction}. Finite-dimensional polynomial
norm equivalence also gives uniform doubling and separated packing.
The preceding recursion therefore supplies the required phase-cell
hierarchy. We use the spatial grid and its small-boundary property from
\cite{BDJST}; the analytic tree estimates are proved below.

Let $\sigma_-(x),\sigma_+(x)$ enclose all shells meeting the selected
witness in \eqref{mod:witness}. Define
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tile-E}
 E_p=G\cap I_p\cap\{Q_x\in\Omega_p\}
       \cap\{\sigma_-(x)\le s(p)\le\sigma_+(x)\}.
\end{equation}
Zero-selector scales between two nonzero intervals are retained in
\eqref{mod:tile-E}. Their kernels are zero, but deleting their tiles would
destroy the interval property of the active scales in a convex tree.
The local operator is
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tile-op}
 T_pf(x)=\mathbf1_{E_p}(x)
 \int k_{s(p),x}(x-y)e^{i(Q_x(y)-Q_x(x))}f(y)\,dy.
\end{equation}
The common factor $e^{-iQ_x(x)}$ is harmless for spatial norms and is
fixed for all tiles. The sum over all tiles is exactly the linearized
witness on $G$.

We use the two densities in \cite[(2.24)--(2.27)]{BDJST}. For precision,
$E_2(\lambda,p)=I_p\cap G\cap\{d_p(Q_x,Q_p)<\lambda\}$;
$\lambda p\lesssim\lambda p'$ means spatial inclusion and reverse
inclusion of the corresponding phase balls. If $\mathfrak P(\mathfrak C)$
is the set of tiles whose spatial cube is contained in the spatial cube
of some member of $\mathfrak C$, set
\begin{align}
 \delta_1(\mathfrak C)
 &=\sup_{p'\in\mathfrak C}\sup_{\lambda\ge2}\lambda^{-a}
   \sup_{p\in\mathfrak P(\mathfrak C):\,\lambda p'\lesssim\lambda p}
   \frac{|E_2(\lambda,p)|}{|I_p|},\label{mod:density1}\\
 \delta_2(\mathfrak C)
 &=\sup_{p\in\mathfrak C}\sup_{r\ge4r_p}
   \frac{|F\cap B(c_p,r)|}{|B(c_p,r)|}.\label{mod:density2}
\end{align}
Empty suprema are zero. Fixed changes of ball radii are absorbed into
structural constants.

An antichain has no two comparable tiles. A tree $\TT(u)$ is convex in
the tile order, satisfies $4p\lesssim u$ for every tile $p$, and has the
support margin
\begin{equation}\label{mod:top-margin}
 B(c_p,8r_p)\subset I_u.
\end{equation}
Its top polynomial is $Q_u$. A forest of density generation $m$ has
pairwise disjoint tile trees, top multiplicity at most $2^m$, tree
density at most $C2^{-m}$, and cross-top separation
\begin{equation}\label{mod:forest-sep}
 d_p(Q_p,Q_u)>2^{Z(m+1)}
 \quad(p\in\TT(u'),\ u'\ne u,\ I_p\subset I_u).
\end{equation}
Here $Z$ is a fixed structural integer, chosen above the geometric
threshold and the analytic threshold specified below. Increasing $Z$
does not change the already chosen spatial grid, its base $D$, or the
parameter $a$ in the density definition. Effective output
sets of distinct trees in one forest are disjoint: at a point belonging
to two trees, the smaller spatial tile is inside the other top, while
tree coherence makes both phases close in that tile metric, contradicting
\eqref{mod:forest-sep}.

\begin{proposition}[Geometric decomposition and count]\label{mod:geometry}
The following conclusions hold for the preceding finite model.

\smallskip\noindent
(i) Suppose $|G|>0$. There is a measurable ambient set $H\subset\R^n$,
$|H|\le|G|/2$, such that the tiles whose spatial cube is not contained
in $H$ partition into $O((m+2)^2)$ forests
and $O(Z(m+2)^3)$ antichains at every generation $m\ge0$. The forests
satisfy \eqref{mod:forest-sep}; the antichains satisfy
$\delta_1\le C2^{-m}$. Every part has
$\delta_2\le C\min\{1,|F|/|G|\}$. Put $G'=G\cap H$.
Then $G'\subset G$ and $|G'|\le|G|/2$; the spatial discard
condition is $I_p\subset H$, not $I_p\subset G'$.

\smallskip\noindent
(ii) Given $\kappa>0$, a fixed $q_0>2$ can be chosen so that, for any
antichain $\mathfrak A$, any subfamily $\mathfrak B$, and any polynomial
$Q$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:antichain-count}
 \left\|\sum_{p\in\mathfrak B}(1+d_p(Q_p,Q))^{-\kappa}
 \mathbf1_{E_p}\right\|_{q_0}
 \le C\delta_1(\mathfrak A)^{1/q_0}
 \left|\bigcup_{p\in\mathfrak B}I_p\right|^{1/q_0}.
\end{equation}
The sets $E_p$ of an antichain are pairwise disjoint.

\smallskip\noindent
(iii) For a nonempty tree, the input stopping partition $\mathcal J(\TT)$
and the output stopping partition $\mathcal L(\TT)$ described below satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{mod:stopping-density}
 |F\cap J|\le C\delta_2(\TT)|J|,
 \qquad
 |L\cap\textstyle\bigcup_{p\in\TT}E_p|
 \le C\delta_1(\TT)|L|.
\end{equation}
Empty trees act by zero raw and smoothed operators and may be omitted.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For (i), apply the geometric decomposition of
\cite[Proposition~2.4 and Section~4]{BDJST} to obtain the ambient
exceptional set $H$. The counting and density estimates depend only on
the grid, phase cells, and admissible scale interval in
\eqref{mod:tile-E}. Set $G'=G\cap H$. Every discarded tile has
$I_p\subset H$ and hence vanishes on $G\setminus G'$. Thus the
surviving operator agrees with the full finite operator on the required
major subset of $G$.

For (ii), first recall the finite-reference consequence of the counting
argument of \cite[Lemmas~5.6--5.9]{BDJST}: if $Q_0$ belongs to the
original finite phase range and $\mathfrak C$ is a sub-antichain satisfying
$d_p(Q_p,Q_0)<2^{M+1}$ for every $p\in\mathfrak C$, then
\[
 \sum_{p\in\mathfrak C}|E_p|
 \le C\delta_1(\mathfrak A)2^{C_aM}
       \left|\bigcup_{p\in\mathfrak C}I_p\right|.
\]
The ball version displayed here follows by summing the distance layers
up to $M+1$; the resulting geometric sum only changes the structural
constant.

To allow an arbitrary ambient polynomial $Q$ without changing the grid,
put
\[
 \mathfrak B_N=\{p\in\mathfrak B:
          2^N\le1+d_p(Q_p,Q)<2^{N+1}\},\qquad N\ge0.
\]
If $\mathfrak B_N$ is empty, its contribution is zero. Otherwise, let
$\mathcal U_N$ be the inclusion-maximal spatial cubes among
$\{I_p:p\in\mathfrak B_N\}$. These cubes are pairwise disjoint and cover
all spatial cubes in this finite collection. For each $L\in\mathcal U_N$
choose $p_L\in\mathfrak B_N$ with $I_{p_L}=L$, and let
$\mathfrak B_{N,L}=\{p\in\mathfrak B_N:I_p\subset L\}$.
The centers were selected from the original finite phase range, so
$Q_{p_L}$ is an admissible finite reference. For $I_p\subset L$, the ball defining $d_p$ lies in
$B(c_L,5r_L)$. Polynomial norm equivalence on this fixed enlargement
gives $d_p(R,S)\le C_{n,d}d_{p_L}(R,S)$. The triangle inequality
therefore gives, for $p\in\mathfrak B_{N,L}$,
\[
 \begin{split}
 d_p(Q_p,Q_{p_L})
 &\le d_p(Q_p,Q)+d_p(Q,Q_{p_L})\\
 &\le d_p(Q_p,Q)+C_{n,d}d_{p_L}(Q,Q_{p_L})
 <(1+C_{n,d})2^{N+1}.
 \end{split}
\]
Choose a fixed integer $c_*$ with $2^{c_*}\ge1+C_{n,d}$.
The finite-reference estimate with $M=N+c_*$ applies to this bucket. Since $p_L$ belongs to the bucket, its spatial union is exactly
$L$. Summing over the disjoint $L$ yields
\[
 \sum_{p\in\mathfrak B_N}|E_p|
 \le C\delta_1(\mathfrak A)2^{C_aN}
       \left|\bigcup_{p\in\mathfrak B}I_p\right|.
\]
Every cube and phase cell used in this reduction belongs to the
original grid. Antichain output sets are disjoint, so the $q_0$-th power
of the left side of \eqref{mod:antichain-count} is bounded by
\[
 C\delta_1(\mathfrak A)\left|\bigcup_{p\in\mathfrak B}I_p\right|
       \sum_{N\ge0}2^{-\kappa q_0N+C_aN}.
\]
Choose $q_0>\max\{2,(C_a+1)/\kappa\}$.

Part (iii) is the purely geometric local density
argument of \cite[Lemmas 6.8--6.9]{BDJST}, with the effective output set in
\eqref{mod:tile-E}.
\end{proof}

The input partition $\mathcal J(\mathfrak C)$ consists of the maximal
grid cubes $J$ for which either $s(J)$ is the bottom scale or no spatial
cube of $\mathfrak C$ is contained in
$B(c(J),100D^{s(J)+1})$. The output partition consists of the maximal
cubes lying in some $I_p$, $p\in\mathfrak C$, that contain no such spatial
cube, with the bottom-scale convention. We write $P_{\mathcal J}$ and
$P_{\mathcal L}$ for their averaging projections. In applications these
partitions are restricted to the relevant top.

We will use one further geometric consequence, stated explicitly in
Section~\ref{mod:sec-local-correlation}: two separated trees admit a
partition into a large-phase portion and a thin boundary portion, with
uniform lower bounds on their active scales. These consequences are
\cite[Lemmas 6.13--6.24]{BDJST}. In the subsequent estimates the geometric
parameter $a$ is fixed and kernel regularity is denoted separately by
$L$. For this fixed geometry, the actual small-boundary estimate and
polynomial oscillation estimate supply positive decay exponents; their
combination gives a fixed $\eta>0$ in the row correlation estimate.
Let $Z_{\rm geo}$ be a structural integer above which the same-grid
geometric decomposition is valid. Choose integers $K\ge1$ and $Z$ so that
\begin{equation}\label{mod:parameter-choice}
 K\beta\ge2,\qquad Z\ge\max\{Z_{\rm geo},4\},\qquad
 \eta Z>2K+3.
\end{equation}
Such choices exist by $\beta,\eta>0$. The geometric construction and
its boundary budget remain valid for every larger $Z$, while $a$, $D$
and the density exponent stay unchanged. With
$\varepsilon_m=\tfrac18 2^{-K(m+1)}$, the two numerical estimates are
\[
 2^{m/2}\varepsilon_m^\beta
 \le(\tfrac18)^\beta2^{-3m/2},\qquad
 2^m\varepsilon_m^{-2}2^{-\eta Z(m+1)}\le64\,2^{-2m}.
\]
Moreover $1+\log(1/\varepsilon_m)\le C_K(1+m)$.
All these choices precede the finite cutoffs, angular atoms, witness
lengths and density generations. No parameter is subsequently enlarged
as a function of a density level or a smoothing radius.



\section{Antichains and a collective high-frequency remainder}\label{mod:sec-antichains}
The central point is to estimate the remainder after grouping all phases
at one spatial scale. Estimating one large tile at a time would introduce
the multiplicity of enlarged spatial supports.

\subsection{A single-shell estimate after frequency translation}
Let $A_h$ be a normalized unphased shell operator with an actual witness
\eqref{mod:witness}, restricted to that shell. Section~\ref{mod:sec-analysis}
gives
\[
 \|A_h\Delta_m\|_{2\to2}\le C2^{-\beta_0m}\quad(m\ge1),
 \qquad \|A_h\|_{2\to2}\le C,
\]
with $\beta_0=1/(8\rho)$. The fixed radial factor $b(\rho_A)$ is included in $A_h$. The operator is not
assumed translation invariant: it is pointwise dominated by the short
variation of the translation-invariant partial-shell path.
For the real, even mollifier $P_t=\phi_t*$ one consequently has
\begin{equation}\label{mod:translated-highpass}
 \|A_h[e^{i\ell\cdot y}(I-P_t)f]\|_2
 \le Ct^{\beta_0}(1+|\ell|)^{\beta_0}\|f\|_2,
 \qquad0<t\le1/8.
\end{equation}
Indeed, after modulation the multiplier of $I-P_t$ is
$1-\widehat\phi(t(\xi-\ell))$. On the annulus $|\xi|\simeq2^m$ its
modulus is at most
$C\min\{1,t2^m\}+C\min\{1,t|\ell|\}$.
The low-frequency piece is estimated as one piece; the high-frequency
pieces are summed with $2^{-\beta_0m}$. This gives
\eqref{mod:translated-highpass}.

\begin{lemma}[Collective demodulated remainder]\label{mod:collective-remainder}
Let outputs be restricted to a fixed bounded cube $I$. Let $Q_x,V_x$
be measurable polynomial fields of degree at most $d$ such that
$\|Q_x-V_x\|_{I^+,*}\le C_0$ on a fixed enlargement containing all kernel
supports. Put
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal R_tf(x)=\mathbf1_I(x)\int k_x(y)e^{iQ_x(y)}
 \bigl[f(y)-e^{-iV_x(y)}P_t(e^{iV_x}f)(y)\bigr]\,dy,
\end{align*}
where $k_x(y)=K_\Theta(x-y)b(\rho_A(x-y))h_x(\rho_A(x-y))$ is restricted to one fixed
normalized shell. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:collective-remainder-eq}
 \|\mathcal R_t\|_{2\to2}\le Ct^\gamma
\end{equation}
for a fixed $\gamma>0$. The constant is independent of the number and
size of the phase coefficients. The scale $t$ is common to this operator.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Partition the finite-dimensional coefficient space modulo constants into
cubes of sidelength $L\ge L_0$. Let $E_\nu$ be the outputs whose $V_x$
belongs to the $\nu$th cube. Each coefficient cube has only a fixed number
of neighbors within a fixed multiple of $L$.

On one cube, remove its center polynomial $W_\nu$ by an input modulation
and an output factor of modulus one. We may then assume
$\|Q_x\|_{I^+,*}+\|V_x\|_{I^+,*}\le CL$. We have the exact identity
\begin{align*}
 f-e^{-iV_x}P_t(e^{iV_x}f)
 ={}&(I-P_t)f\\
 &+\int\phi_t(v)
 [1-e^{i(V_x(y-v)-V_x(y))}]f(y-v)\,dv.
\end{align*}
The second term contributes an operator bounded pointwise by $CLtMf$.
For the first term expand $e^{iQ_x(y)}$ on the fixed input support, using
one fixed compact smooth extension:
\[
 e^{iQ_x(y)}\chi(y)=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb Z^n}c_\ell(x)e^{i\lambda_\ell\cdot y},
 \qquad |c_\ell(x)|\le C_M L^M(1+|\ell|)^{-M}.
\]
Choose $M>n+\beta_0+2$ and apply
\eqref{mod:translated-highpass}. This proves
\begin{equation}\label{mod:near-coefficient-block}
 \|\mathbf1_{E_\nu}\mathcal R_t\|_{2\to2}
 \le CL^Mt^{\beta_0}.
\end{equation}
The number of neighboring coefficient cubes is fixed, so all near-block
contributions to $\mathcal R_t\mathcal R_t^*$ have norm at most
$CL^{2M}t^{2\beta_0}$.

For far blocks set $r_x=Q_x-V_x$ and
$d\mu_t(v)=d\delta_0(v)-\phi_t(v)\,dv$. Direct computation, using that
$\phi$ is even, gives the adjoint kernel
\begin{equation}\label{mod:Rstar-kernel}
 \mathcal R_t^*(y,x)
 =e^{-iQ_x(y)}\int
 e^{-i[r_x(y-v)-r_x(y)]}\overline{k_x(y-v)}\,d\mu_t(v).
\end{equation}
The coefficient norm of $r_x(\cdot-v)-r_x$ is $O(t)$ on the fixed
support. For far coefficient blocks the nonconstant part of
$Q_x-Q_{x'}$ has norm at least $cL$. The total phase in the correlation
of \eqref{mod:Rstar-kernel}, with shifts $v,w$, therefore still has norm
at least $cL$. Translation from global normalized coordinates to the
kernel center changes this norm by at most a fixed factor.

Proposition~\ref{mod:equal-correlation} gives the correlation majorant
\[
 C\iint\Psi_L(x-x'+v-w)\,d|\mu_t|(v)d|\mu_t|(w),
 \qquad \Psi_L(z)=\min\{1,(L|z|^d)^{-c}\}.
\]
In dimension one use its stronger bound. On a fixed bounded region,
\[
 \int\Psi_L(z)\,dz\le CL^{-\tau},\qquad
 \tau=\min\{n/(2d),c/2\}>0.
\]
To verify this split at $|z|=L^{-1/(2d)}$. The signed measure $\mu_t$ is
common to all outputs and has total variation at most two. Tonelli
therefore gives both row and column integral bounds $CL^{-\tau}$.
Schur's test bounds the complete far-block operator by that quantity;
no sum over the number of far coefficient cubes is taken.

Combining near and far blocks yields
\[
 \|\mathcal R_t\|_{2\to2}^2
 \le C(L^{2M}t^{2\beta_0}+L^{-\tau}).
\]
Take $L=L_0t^{-2\beta_0/(2M+\tau)}$. Then
\eqref{mod:collective-remainder-eq} holds with
$\gamma=\beta_0\tau/(2M+\tau)>0$.
\end{proof}

Localize a fixed spatial scale $R_j$ into grid cubes. Their fixed
Euclidean enlargements have bounded overlap. Scaling
Lemma~\ref{mod:collective-remainder} gives
\begin{equation}\label{mod:scale-remainder}
 \left\|\sum_{p:\,s(p)=j}
 M_{-Q_p}(I-P_{tR_j})M_{Q_p}T_p^*g\right\|_2
 \le Ct^\gamma\|g\|_2.
\end{equation}
A common unit-modulus output factor in \eqref{mod:tile-op} is absorbed
into $g$. At a given output the center field $V_x$ is the phase of its
unique active tile at that scale. Thus all hypotheses of the lemma hold.
Spatial cube overlap, not phase-tile overlap, is used in this localization.

\subsection{Mass-sensitive estimates for the principal correlation}
Let $g_p=g\mathbf1_{E_p}$ and $M_p=\|g_p\|_1$. Put
$F_p=M_{Q_p}T_p^*g_p$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:mass-bound}
 \|F_p\|_\infty\le CR_p^{-n}M_p.
\end{equation}
If $r=R_q\le R=R_p$, choose $0<\varepsilon\le1$ and mollify $F_p$ at the smaller scale $\varepsilon r$.
This is a choice of the auxiliary proof parameter; the fixed-scale remainder
estimate above remains valid for every positive smoothing parameter.
The size and derivative bounds are
\[
 \|P_{\varepsilon r}F_p\|_\infty
 +r\|\nabla P_{\varepsilon r}F_p\|_\infty
 \le C\varepsilon^{-1}R^{-n}M_p.
\]
Apply Lemma~\ref{mod:radial-vdc} inside the $q$th tile operator. The
comparison of local phase metrics, together with $Q_x\in\Omega_q$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{mod:main-correlation}
 \left|\langle M_{-Q_p}P_{\varepsilon r}F_p,T_q^*g_q\rangle\right|
 \le C\varepsilon^{-1}(1+d_q(Q_q,Q_p))^{-\kappa}
 R^{-n}M_pM_q.
\end{equation}
The local norm comparison is uniform when the two supports meet: then
$I_q$ lies in a fixed enlargement of $I_p$, and translation between the
centers of the small integration annulus and $I_q$ is bounded in units
of $R_q$. For bounded phase distance use the size estimate instead.

\begin{proposition}[Antichain density estimate]\label{mod:antichain}
There is $\vartheta>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{mod:antichain-L2}
 \|T_{\mathfrak A}\|_{2\to2}
 \le C\delta_1(\mathfrak A)^\vartheta
\end{equation}
for every finite antichain. Constants are independent of the number of
scales, phase cells, and selected jumps.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Write $\delta=\delta_1(\mathfrak A)$ and choose $q_0>2$ as in
\eqref{mod:antichain-count}, for the exponent in
\eqref{mod:main-correlation}. Set $\sigma=1/q_0$.
For a fixed larger tile $p$, the raw adjoint is supported, up to a null
set, in $B(c_p,5R_p)$: the output cell lies in $B(c_p,4R_p)$ and
the raw kernel has displacement at most $R_p/4$. Mollification at
$\varepsilon R_q\le R_p$ enlarges this to $B(c_p,6R_p)$.
If this support meets $B(c_q,5R_q)$ and $R_q\le R_p$, the whole cell
$I_q$ lies in $B_p=B(c_p,16R_p)$. Otherwise the Hilbert pairing is zero.
Thus only the stated smaller tiles interact, and $|B_p|\simeq R_p^n$.
H\"older and \eqref{mod:antichain-count} give
\[
 R_p^{-n}\sum_q(1+d_q(Q_q,Q_p))^{-\kappa}M_q
 \le C\delta^\sigma
 \left(\frac1{|B_p|}\int_{B_p}|g|^{q_0'}\right)^{1/q_0'}.
\]
For $x\in E_p$ the right side is at most $C\delta^\sigma M_{q_0'}g(x)$.
Since all $E_p$ are disjoint and $q_0'<2$, the sum of the absolute main
correlations is at most
\begin{equation}\label{mod:antichain-main-sum}
 C\varepsilon^{-1}\delta^\sigma\|g\|_2^2.
\end{equation}

For the errors, group by scales before applying any norm. Write
$g_j=g\mathbf1_{\cup_{p\in\mathfrak A,\,s(p)=j}E_p}$ and
$F_j=\sum_{s(p)=j}T_p^*g_p$. Disjointness gives
$\sum_j\|g_j\|_2^2\le\|g\|_2^2$.
The corresponding forward operator is bounded pointwise by $CMf$, so
$\|F_j\|_2\le C\|g_j\|_2$.
At scale gap $k\ge0$, use \eqref{mod:scale-remainder} with
$t=\varepsilon D^{-k}$. Then
\[
 \left|\sum_j\left\langle
 \sum_{s(p)=j}M_{-Q_p}(I-P_{\varepsilon R_{j-k}})M_{Q_p}T_p^*g_p,
 F_{j-k}\right\rangle\right|
 \le C\varepsilon^\gamma D^{-\gamma k}\|g\|_2^2.
\]
The sum over $k$ is geometric. Expand
$\|\sum_jF_j\|_2^2$ with all same-scale pairs in the diagonal and with
$2\operatorname{Re}\langle F_j,F_{j-k}\rangle$ for $k>0$.
Its main terms are covered by \eqref{mod:antichain-main-sum}; its errors
are covered by the last display. Thus
\[
 \|T_{\mathfrak A}^*g\|_2^2
 \le C(\varepsilon^{-1}\delta^\sigma+\varepsilon^\gamma)\|g\|_2^2.
\]
The density satisfies $0\le\delta\le1$, directly from its supremum
definition. Fix $0<\varepsilon_0\le1$. If $\delta>0$, take
$\varepsilon=\varepsilon_0\delta^{\sigma/(1+\gamma)}$, which lies
in the permitted range. If $\delta=0$, let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$
in the preceding quadratic bound instead. This proves
\eqref{mod:antichain-L2} with
$\vartheta=\sigma\gamma/[2(1+\gamma)]$.
There is no enlarged-support multiplicity in this calculation.
\end{proof}



\section{Trees, input smoothing, and local correlations}\label{mod:tree:mod:sec-trees}
The definitions and densities are those of Section~\ref{mod:sec-geometry}.
In particular $E_p$ includes $G$ and the whole enclosing interval of
witness scales. A tree is never replaced by its nonzero-selector subset.
We first prove the local correlation estimate, retaining a separate
kernel regularity constant; we then prove its single-tree norm inputs.
For a tree with top phase $Q_i$ and $x\in E_p$, the metric growth and
coherence give
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree-coherence}
 \|Q_x-Q_i\|_{B(c_p,20r_p),*}\le C.
\end{equation}
These constants depend only on the fixed spatial and phase geometry.


Suppose $I_1\subset I_2$; the case of disjoint tops is immediate by support. Put
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:high}
 \cS=\{p\in\TT_1\cup\TT_2:
 d_p(Q_1,Q_2)\ge A^{1/2}\},\qquad
 \TT_2^+=\TT_2\cap\cS,\quad \TT_2^-=\TT_2\setminus\cS.
\end{equation}
The following are geometric facts. Their statements do not mention a kernel or its regularity constant.

\begin{lemma}[Geometric separation and stopping regions]\label{mod:tree:lem:geometry}
There are fixed positive constants $c_*,\kappa_*$ and fixed finite constants $C_*,N_*$ with the following properties.

(a) $\TT_1\subset\cS$, and every tile in $\TT_2^-$ has $I_p\cap I_1=\varnothing$.

(b) Let $\cJ_+=\{J\in\cJ(\cS):J\subset I_1\}$. There is a partition of unity $\{\chi_J\}_{J\in\cJ_+}$ on $I_1$, supported in $B_J=B(c(J),8r_J)$, with Lipschitz constants at most $C_*/r_J$. Put $B_J^0=B(c(J),r_J/8)$. If the support ball $B(c(I_p),5r_p)$ of a tile in $\TT_1$ or $\TT_2^+$ meets $2B_J$, then $s(p)\ge s(J)-C_*$. Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:local-phase-separation}
 \norm{Q_2-Q_1}_{B_J,*}\ge c_*A^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
Only at most $N_*$ consecutive scales of $\TT_2^-$ can have their support balls meet $B_J^0$.

(c) Put $\cJ_0=\{J\in\cJ(\TT_1):J\subset I_1\}$. If $p\in\TT_2^-$ and $B(c(I_p),5r_p)$ meets $J\in\cJ_0$, then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:scale-gap}
 s(p)\le s(J)-c_*\log_D A+C_*.
\end{equation}
At relative scale $k=s(J)-s(p)$, the union of these support balls inside $J$ is a set $F_{J,k}$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:thin}
 |F_{J,k}|\le C_*D^{-\kappa_* k}|J|.
\end{equation}
At a fixed relative scale their overlap is bounded by $C_*$. The corresponding lower-scale exclusion in (b) holds for $\TT_1$ and $\cJ_0$ as well.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
These are the geometric conclusions of \cite[Lemmas 6.13--6.14, 6.17, 6.21, 6.23--6.24]{BDJST}, with $A$ denoting the cross-top separation parameter. We record why the use here does not alter their hypotheses. Membership in $\cS$ is determined by local phase distances; nestedness of spatial cubes, coherence, and cross-top separation give (a). Maximality of $\cJ(\cS)$ supplies a nearby high-separation tile at the parent scale of each $J$; comparing the finitely enlarged ball metrics gives \eqref{mod:tree:eq:local-phase-separation}. The same maximality excludes smaller contributing tiles. A low-separation tile meeting $B_J^0$ cannot be smaller than $J$ by the interior ball property, or more than a fixed number of scales larger without contradicting the high-separation tile at the parent scale. This gives the last assertion in (b).

For (c), a low-separation tile has spatial cube outside $I_1$. Comparing its phase metric with the metric of a nearby tile in $\TT_1$, provided by maximality of $\cJ(\TT_1)$, forces a scale gap proportional to $\log_D A$. Its support can enter $J$ only through a boundary layer of relative width $O(D^{-k})$. The grid small-boundary property gives \eqref{mod:tree:eq:thin}; the fixed-scale packing property gives bounded overlap. All comparisons use only the fixed grid, phase metrics, and the support radius $5r_p$. The parameter controlling kernel smoothness does not occur.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The smoothed operators}\label{mod:tree:sec:smoothing}
First multiply the full linearized output by $e^{-iQ_x(x)}$. This is a common unit-modulus factor for all tiles and does not change any spatial norm. The operators below refer to that normalization.

In this subsection write $\varrho(z)=|A_{\mathrm{cone}}z|$, where $A_{\mathrm{cone}}$ is the fixed matrix denoted by $A$ in Theorem~\ref{mod:cone}. The scalar $A$ in the separated-tree estimates is a distinct phase-separation parameter. The Euclidean case is $A_{\mathrm{cone}}=I$. Choose disjoint radial scale intervals so that the associated raw kernels $k_{j,x}$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:raw-size}
 \supp k_{j,x}\subset\{z:c_-D^j\le |z|\le D^j/4\},\qquad
 |k_{j,x}(z)|\le C D^{-jn}.
\end{equation}
Here $k_{j,x}$ includes the angular factor, the fixed amplitude $b(\varrho(z))$, the sharp radial scale cutoff, and the actual equal-amplitude selector $h_x(\varrho(z))$. The size estimate uses $\|b\|_\infty\le1$ and $\norm{h_x}_\infty\le1$. The positive constant $c_-$ may depend on $D$ and the cone aperture, but is independent of the least singular value of $A_{\mathrm{cone}}$.

Let $\phi\in C_c^\infty(B(0,1))$ be real, even, and nonnegative, with integral one. For $0<\varepsilon\le\varepsilon_0<1/8$, put $P_{\varepsilon r}f=\phi_{\varepsilon r}*f$. For $p\in\TT_i$, let
\[
 \psi_{i,x}(z)=Q_x(z)-Q_i(z)-Q_x(x)+Q_i(x).
\]
Define the phase-removed kernel
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:bkernel}
 b_{i,p}^\varepsilon(x,y)
 =\mathbf1_{E_p}(x)\int k_{s(p),x}(x-z)e^{i\psi_{i,x}(z)}
 \phi_{\varepsilon r_p}(z-y)\,dz.
\end{equation}
If $B_{i,p}^\varepsilon$ is the operator with this kernel, the actual smoothed tile is
\[
 U_{i,p}^\varepsilon=M_{-Q_i}B_{i,p}^\varepsilon M_{Q_i},\qquad
 M_Qf=e^{iQ}f.
\]
Thus it is the original tile conjugated by the top phase, smoothed on its input, and conjugated back. It is not obtained by smoothing only the angular function.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform kernel bounds]\label{mod:tree:lem:kernel}
The kernels \eqref{mod:tree:eq:bkernel} satisfy
\begin{align}
 |b_{i,p}^\varepsilon(x,y)|&\le C\mathbf1_{E_p}(x)r_p^{-n},\label{mod:tree:eq:bsize}\\
 |\nabla_yb_{i,p}^\varepsilon(x,y)|&\le C\varepsilon^{-1}\mathbf1_{E_p}(x)r_p^{-n-1}.\label{mod:tree:eq:bgrad}
\end{align}
Their supports in $y$ lie in $B(c(I_p),5r_p)\subset I_i$. The constants are independent of the top phase, all other phase coefficients, the number of jumps, and, in the conical case, $\tau$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The size bound follows from \eqref{mod:tree:eq:raw-size} and $\norm\phi_1=1$. Differentiation in $y$ falls only on $\phi_{\varepsilon r_p}$, whose derivative has $L^1$ norm $C/(\varepsilon r_p)$. This proves \eqref{mod:tree:eq:bgrad}; no derivative of the rough angular factor or of $h_x$ is taken. If the kernel is nonzero, $|x-y|\le r_p/4+\varepsilon r_p<r_p/2$. Since $x\in I_p\subset B(c(I_p),4r_p)$, the asserted support follows. Its inclusion in the top cube follows from \eqref{mod:top-margin}.
\end{proof}

For $\mathfrak C\subset\TT_i$, write
\[
 U_{i,\mathfrak C}^\varepsilon=\sum_{p\in\mathfrak C}U_{i,p}^\varepsilon,
 \qquad
 H_{i,\mathfrak C}=e^{iQ_i}(U_{i,\mathfrak C}^\varepsilon)^*g_i.
\]
Because $M_{Q_i}$ is an isometry and only introduces a unit-modulus factor on the input of the adjoint, Lemma~\ref{mod:tree:lem:kernel} gives all estimates below for $H_{i,\mathfrak C}$ with $|g_i|$ on their right-hand sides.

\subsection{A localized separated-tree theorem}\label{mod:sec-local-correlation}\label{mod:tree:sec:local}
The proof is stated for a general $L\ge1$ in place of $C\varepsilon^{-1}$ in \eqref{mod:tree:eq:bgrad}. Assume \eqref{mod:tree:eq:bsize}, the support conclusion of Lemma~\ref{mod:tree:lem:kernel}, and
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:Lgrad}
 |\nabla_yb_{i,p}(x,y)|\le C L\mathbf1_{E_p}(x)r_p^{-n-1}.
\end{equation}
All constants except $L$ remain fixed. Denote the resulting tree operators by $U_i$ and set $g_i^0=g_i\mathbf1_{\cup_{p\in\TT_i}E_p}$.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:majorant}
 \mathscr S_i g_i
 =|U_i^*g_i|+C_1L M(|g_i^0|)+|g_i^0|,
\end{equation}
where $C_1$ is a sufficiently large fixed structural constant.

\begin{theorem}[Localized correlation with separate regularity]\label{mod:tree:thm:local}
Under the fixed geometric conditions of Section~\ref{mod:sec-geometry}, there is $\eta>0$, independent of $L$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:local-correlation}
 |\langle U_1^*g_1,U_2^*g_2\rangle|
 \le CA^{-\eta}
 \prod_{i=1}^{2}\norm{\mathscr S_i g_i}_{L^2(I_1\cap I_2)}.
\end{equation}
The constant $C$ and exponent $\eta$ depend only on the fixed geometry, dimension, and degree. In particular, neither the dyadic base nor the phase-distance exponent changes with $L$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Local control of phase-removed adjoint outputs}
\begin{lemma}\label{mod:tree:lem:local-osc}
For $J\in\cJ_+$, $\mathfrak C=\TT_1$ or $\TT_2^+$, and $y,y'\in2B_J$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:osc}
 |H_{i,\mathfrak C}(y)-H_{i,\mathfrak C}(y')|
 \le CL\frac{|y-y'|}{r_J}\essinf_JM(|g_i^0|).
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:low-inner}
 \sup_{B_J^0}|H_{2,\TT_2^-}|
 \le C\essinf_JM(|g_2^0|).
\end{equation}
The oscillation estimate for the full first tree also holds on $J\in\cJ_0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
At a fixed scale, the output sets $E_p$ are disjoint. For tiles whose support meets $2B_J$, their output sets lie in a ball about $c(J)$ of radius $Cr_p$. Consequently the sum of the gradient estimates \eqref{mod:tree:eq:Lgrad} at that scale is at most
\[
 CLr_p^{-n-1}\int_{B(c(J),Cr_p)}|g_i^0|
 \le CLr_p^{-1}\essinf_JM(|g_i^0|).
\]
The lower-scale exclusion in Lemma~\ref{mod:tree:lem:geometry}(b) allows the geometric sum over $s(p)\ge s(J)-C_*$. It is at most $CLr_J^{-1}\essinf_JM(|g_i^0|)$. Integrating the gradient on the segment from $y$ to $y'$ proves \eqref{mod:tree:eq:osc}. The fixed enlargement can be increased once in the geometric construction to include these segments.

For \eqref{mod:tree:eq:low-inner}, only $N_*$ consecutive scales can contribute. At each such scale use \eqref{mod:tree:eq:bsize} and fixed-scale disjointness instead of \eqref{mod:tree:eq:Lgrad}. Each scale is bounded by $C\essinf_JM(|g_2^0|)$. The same lower-scale argument for $\cJ_0$ proves the final assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The contribution with large phase separation}
Assume $I_1\subset I_2$ and let $\Delta=Q_2-Q_1$. Define
\[
 A_i(J)=\essinf_{B_J^0}|U_i^*g_i|+C_2L\essinf_JM(|g_i^0|).
\]
Lemma~\ref{mod:tree:lem:local-osc}, including \eqref{mod:tree:eq:low-inner} for the second tree, gives
\[
 \sup_{2B_J}|H_{1,\TT_1}|+r_J\sup_{2B_J}|\nabla H_{1,\TT_1}|\le CA_1(J),
\]
\[
 \sup_{2B_J}|H_{2,\TT_2^+}|+r_J\sup_{2B_J}|\nabla H_{2,\TT_2^+}|\le CA_2(J).
\]
Thus
\[
 a_J=\chi_JH_{1,\TT_1}\overline{H_{2,\TT_2^+}}
\]
satisfies $\norm{a_J}_\infty+r_J\operatorname{Lip}(a_J)\le CA_1(J)A_2(J)$. This function can be extended by zero off $I_1$ without changing the bound by more than a fixed constant. Indeed the first-tree kernel support lies in $B(c(I_p),5r_p)$ while $B(c(I_p),8r_p)\subset I_1$; the contributing first-tree scales are at least comparable to $r_J$. Hence the first-tree factor vanishes in a boundary neighborhood of $I_1$ of width comparable to $r_J$ whenever this extension is relevant.

For completeness, on a normalized fixed ball a real polynomial $S$ of bounded degree with nonconstant coefficient norm $\Lambda\ge1$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:poly-vdc}
 \left|\int e^{iS}a\right|
 \le C\Lambda^{-\xi}\bigl(\norm a_\infty+\operatorname{Lip}(a)\bigr),
 \qquad\xi>0,
\end{equation}
for Lipschitz amplitudes supported in that ball. To see this, one derivative $\partial_jS$ has coefficient norm at least $c\Lambda$. The bounded-degree polynomial sublevel estimate bounds $\{ |\partial_jS|\le\Lambda u\}$ by $Cu^\alpha$ for a fixed $\alpha>0$. On its complement, divide the $j$-sections at the zeros of the relevant bounded-degree polynomials so that $\partial_jS$ is monotone and has fixed sign. One integration by parts yields $C(\Lambda u)^{-1}(\norm a_\infty+\operatorname{Lip}(a))$. Optimizing $u$ gives \eqref{mod:tree:eq:poly-vdc}. The elementary sublevel estimate follows by one-variable Lagrange interpolation and induction on the number of variables.

Apply \eqref{mod:tree:eq:poly-vdc} after scaling $B_J$ and use \eqref{mod:tree:eq:local-phase-separation}. It follows that
\[
 \left|\int e^{i\Delta}a_J\right|
 \le CA^{-\xi/2}|J|A_1(J)A_2(J).
\]
For almost every $y\in B_J^0\subset J$, the definition gives
\[
 A_i(J)\le C\mathscr S_i g_i(y).
\]
One must use the inner ball here: a bound on the low part only on $B_J^0$ is not a pointwise bound on all of $J$. Since $|J|\le C|B_J^0|$ and the balls $B_J^0$ are pairwise disjoint, we have
\[
 \sum_J |J|A_1(J)A_2(J)
 \le C\sum_J\int_{B_J^0}\mathscr S_1g_1\,\mathscr S_2g_2
 \le C\prod_i\norm{\mathscr S_ig_i}_{L^2(I_1)}.
\]
Thus the high-separation contribution satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:high-final}
 |\langle U_1^*g_1,(U_{2,\TT_2^+})^*g_2\rangle|
 \le CA^{-\xi/2}
 \prod_i\norm{\mathscr S_i g_i}_{L^2(I_1)}.
\end{equation}
No global norm has replaced the local norms in this step.

\subsection{The small-boundary contribution}
Let $H_2^-=e^{iQ_2}(U_{2,\TT_2^-})^*g_2$. For $J\in\cJ_0$, the final assertion of Lemma~\ref{mod:tree:lem:local-osc} gives
\[
 \sup_J|U_1^*g_1|
 \le \langle|U_1^*g_1|\rangle_J+CL\essinf_JM(|g_1^0|)
 \le C\langle\mathscr S_1g_1\rangle_J.
\]
Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:near-pairing}
 |\langle U_1^*g_1,(U_{2,\TT_2^-})^*g_2\rangle|
 \le C\norm{\mathscr S_1g_1}_{L^2(I_1)}
 \norm{P_{\cJ_0}|H_2^-|}_{L^2(I_1)}.
\end{equation}
Here $P_{\cJ_0}$ denotes averaging over the disjoint cubes of $\cJ_0$. This argument uses local oscillation of the first tree; it does not require an unstated projection estimate for a different tree operator.

We estimate the second factor explicitly. At a fixed relative scale $k=s(J)-s(p)$, the absolute sum of adjoint kernels from $\TT_2^-$ is bounded on $J$ by
\[
 CM(|g_2^0|)\mathbf1_{F_{J,k}}.
\]
This follows from the size bound and the disjointness of $E_p$ at a fixed scale. By Lemma~\ref{mod:tree:lem:geometry}(c), only $k\ge c_*\log_D A-C_*$ occur. Jensen and Cauchy--Schwarz give
\[
 |J|\left(\frac1{|J|}\int_J M(|g_2^0|)\mathbf1_{F_{J,k}}\right)^2
 \le CD^{-\kappa_*k}\int_JM(|g_2^0|)^2.
\]
Minkowski for the outer $\ell^2(J)$ norm and the geometric sum in $k$ show that
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:projection-small}
 \norm{P_{\cJ_0}|H_2^-|}_{L^2(I_1)}
 \le CA^{-\xi_1}\norm{M(|g_2^0|)}_{L^2(I_1)},\qquad \xi_1>0.
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{mod:tree:eq:near-pairing}--\eqref{mod:tree:eq:projection-small} proves
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:near-final}
 |\langle U_1^*g_1,(U_{2,\TT_2^-})^*g_2\rangle|
 \le CA^{-\xi_1}\prod_i\norm{\mathscr S_ig_i}_{L^2(I_1)}.
\end{equation}
Equations \eqref{mod:tree:eq:high-final} and \eqref{mod:tree:eq:near-final} prove Theorem~\ref{mod:tree:thm:local}, with $\eta=\min(\xi/2,\xi_1)$. Disjoint tops give zero correlation, and nestedness handles the remaining order of the two tops.

\section{Single-tree norm and density estimates}\label{mod:tree:sec:tree-inputs}
We establish the approximation and projection estimates for the same operators used in the local correlation theorem. Assume that the raw kernels at scale $R_j=D^j$ are disjoint annular pieces of $K_\Theta(z)b(\rho_A(z))$, with bounded mean-zero angular part and a fixed normalized radial amplitude $b\in\mathcal V^\rho$. The truncation may be Euclidean or conical elliptic. The selector is an actual equal-amplitude paired-jump witness, and restrictions to intervals of active scales are allowed. Constants below are uniform over finite cutoffs.


The radial function $b$ is fixed, satisfies
$\|b\|_\infty+V^\rho(b)\le1$, and is included in every raw shell.
The path norm $\NN$ is the paired-increment norm of
Lemma~\ref{mod:pathlemma}. Lemma~\ref{radialmod:smooth-jump} gives
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:smooth-jump-input}
 \|\NN(\Phi_{R_j}*f:j\in\mathbb Z)\|_2\le C\|f\|_2,
 \qquad R_j=D^j.
\end{equation}
Here $D$ is an integral power of two, so this is a subsequence of the
smooth dyadic path. The pointwise martingale theorem and the proved
square-function comparison supply this estimate with the jump-amplitude
supremum inside the spatial norm.

\subsection{Single-shell and frequency-ordered estimates}

Write $H_{j,u}$ for a weighted partial raw shell, where $u$ ranges over
a fixed compact interval and the path vanishes at its upper endpoint.
The fixed shell ratio may depend on $D$. Lemma~\ref{mod:shell-envelope}
and its proof give, for $R_j|\xi|\simeq2^m$,
\[
 \sup_u|\widehat H_{j,u}(\xi)|
 \le C_\rho\min\{2^m,2^{-m/(2\rho)}\},\qquad
 \esssup_u|\partial_u\widehat H_{j,u}(\xi)|
 \le C\min\{2^m,1\}.
\]
The shell still has zero mass because both its endpoints and the
amplitude $b$ are expressed in the same radial variable $|Az|$.
The proof of \eqref{mod:sobolev-variation} therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:shell-short-input}
 \|V^2(H_{j,u}\Delta_{m,j}f:u)\|_2
 \le C_\rho b_m\|f\|_2,
 \qquad
 b_m=\begin{cases}2^m,&m\le0,\\2^{-m/(8\rho)},&m>0.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The constants are independent of the smallest singular value of $A$,
the radial location of the variation, and all finite cutoffs.

We next prove a frequency-ordered tail estimate. Suppose that $u_j$ has
Fourier support in a fixed annulus at frequency $2^m/R_j$, initially
for finitely many $j$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:ordered-tail}
 \left\|\NN\left(\sum_{\ell\ge j}u_\ell:j\in\mathbb Z\right)\right\|_2
 \le C\left(\sum_j\|u_j\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
To prove this, put $U=\sum_ju_j$ and compare the tail with
$\Phi_{R_j2^{-m}}*U$, taking $\widehat\Phi=1$ near zero and
$\widehat\Phi=0$ outside a fixed ball. All annuli except a bounded
number adjacent to the transition agree exactly. Since each annulus
participates in only a bounded number of errors, Plancherel yields
\[
 \left\|\left(\sum_j\left|
   \sum_{\ell\ge j}u_\ell-\Phi_{R_j2^{-m}}*U
               \right|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_2
 \le C\left(\sum_j\|u_j\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}.
\]
The elementary bound $\NN(v_j:j)\le2(\sum_j|v_j|^2)^{1/2}$ controls
this error path. Because $R_j2^{-m}$ is a subsequence of the positive
dyadic scales, Lemma~\ref{radialmod:smooth-jump} controls the smooth
path by $C\|U\|_2$. Frequency overlap gives
$\|U\|_2^2\le C\sum_j\|u_j\|_2^2$, proving
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:ordered-tail}.

Let $A_j^{b,h}$ denote the unphased weighted shell with the common
output-dependent actual witness
\[
 h_x(r)=N(x)^{-1/2}\sum_{\nu=1}^{N(x)}\zeta_\nu(x)
          \mathbf1_{(a_\nu(x),b_\nu(x)]}(r),
 \qquad |\zeta_\nu(x)|\le1.
\]
The intervals are ordered and disjoint. For arbitrary $f_j\in L^2$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:diagonal-unphased}
 \left\|\sum_jA_j^{b,h}\Delta_{m,j}f_j\right\|_2
 \le C_\rho b_m\left(\sum_j\|f_j\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
Here and below the sum may initially be finite. Concatenate the
partial-shell paths $H_{j,u}\Delta_{m,j}f_j$ in radial order, with
zero at the outer endpoint of all selected shells. Its complete-shell
increments $u_j$ have the frequency supports required by
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:ordered-tail}. The shell envelope gives
\[
 \left(\sum_j\|u_j\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le C_\rho a_m\left(\sum_j\|f_j\|_2^2\right)^{1/2},
 \qquad
 a_m=\begin{cases}2^m,&m\le0,\\2^{-m/(2\rho)},&m>0.\end{cases}
\]
Equation~\eqref{mod:tree:eq:ordered-tail} controls the long part, and
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:shell-short-input}, summed in $j$, controls the
short-square part. Since $a_m\le b_m$, the deterministic long--short
inequality controls the full paired norm of this concatenated path by
the right side of \eqref{mod:tree:eq:diagonal-unphased}. For every
$x$, the sum on its left is one normalized paired witness for that
path, which proves the asserted estimate.

A restriction to an output-dependent consecutive interval of shell
indices preserves the estimate. Intersect every interval of $h_x$ with
the corresponding radial interval and discard the empty intersections.
If $N'\le N$ intervals remain, then
$N^{-1/2}=\sqrt{N'/N}\,(N')^{-1/2}$; the resulting function is an
actual paired witness multiplied by a scalar of modulus at most one.
The case $N'=0$ gives the zero operator. Tree convexity supplies exactly
such restrictions. The weight $b$ remains fixed in this argument;
the product $bh_x$ is not substituted for an unrestricted element of
a variation space when applying
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:diagonal-unphased}.

\subsection{Tree approximation in \texorpdfstring{$L^2$}{L2}}

Let $j_+(x)$ be the largest active shell index at $x$ in a tree; the
operator is zero when no shell is active. Remove the top phase as in
Section~\ref{mod:tree:sec:smoothing}. Polynomial metric growth and tree
coherence give, on normalized shell $j$, fixed-order derivative bounds
$C_M2^{j-j_+(x)}$, $j\le j_+(x)$, for the phase difference; see
\cite[(6.1) and the proof of Lemma 6.2]{BDJST}. Multiplying by a fixed
smooth cutoff on the normalized annulus gives the Fourier expansion
\[
 e^{i\psi_{i,x}(x-R_jv)}-1
 =\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb Z^n}c_{j,\ell}(x)e^{i\lambda_\ell\cdot v},
 \qquad
 |c_{j,\ell}(x)|\le C_M2^{j-j_+(x)}(1+|\ell|)^{-M}.
\]
The frequencies $\lambda_\ell$ are constant multiples of $\ell$.
The constants do not depend on the radial amplitude.

For each $\ell$, Cauchy--Schwarz in $j$ uses
$\sum_{j\le j_+(x)}2^{2(j-j_+(x))}\le C$. If $m>0$ and
$|\ell|\le c2^m$ for a sufficiently small fixed $c$, modulation by
$\lambda_\ell/R_j$ leaves the input frequency in an annulus comparable
to $2^m/R_j$. Equation~\eqref{mod:tree:eq:shell-short-input} then
bounds the remaining square sum. For $|\ell|>c2^m$, use the uniform
single-shell $L^2$ bound and sum $(1+|\ell|)^{-M}$ with $M>n+2$.
For $m\le0$ use this uniform bound for all Fourier modes. Combining
these phase-error estimates with
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:diagonal-unphased} proves
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:diagonal-tree}
 \left\|\sum_jB_j\Delta_{m,j}f_j\right\|_2
 \le C_\rho w_m\left(\sum_j\|f_j\|_2^2\right)^{1/2},
 \qquad
 w_m=\begin{cases}1,&m\le0,\\2^{-\beta m},&m>0,\end{cases}
 \qquad \beta=\frac1{16\rho}.
\end{equation}
Here $B_j$ is the phase-removed unsmoothed weighted tree shell.
The use of $w_m=1$ at low frequencies records the loss caused by the
phase error explicitly.

Apply a Littlewood--Paley resolution to
$\sum_jB_j(I-P_{\varepsilon R_j})f$. At fixed relative frequency $m$,
the input square sum is at most
$C\min\{1,\varepsilon2^m\}\|f\|_2$, because the corresponding frequency
annuli have bounded overlap and
$|1-\widehat\phi(\varepsilon R_j\xi)|
 \le C\min\{1,\varepsilon2^m\}$ there. Equation
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:diagonal-tree} and summation in $m$ give
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:approx}
 \left\|\sum_jB_j(I-P_{\varepsilon R_j})\right\|_{2\to2}
 \le C_\rho\sum_mw_m\min\{1,\varepsilon2^m\}
 \le C_\rho\varepsilon^\beta.
\end{equation}
The final series is geometric after division at
$2^m\simeq\varepsilon^{-1}$, since $0<\beta<1$. This is the exact
input smoothing in \eqref{mod:tree:eq:bkernel}; the common output
phase and top modulation are isometries.

The unphased part of one unsmoothed tree is controlled on $L^2$ by
Proposition~\ref{mod:unphased}, because its active scales restrict one
actual witness to a radial interval. The phase error is bounded
pointwise by
\[
 C\sum_{j\le j_+(x)}2^{j-j_+(x)}Mf(x)\le CMf(x).
\]
Thus the unsmoothed tree is uniformly bounded on $L^2$. Combining this
with \eqref{mod:tree:eq:approx} gives
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:single-tree-uniform}
 \|U_\TT\|_{2\to2}+\|U_\TT^\varepsilon\|_{2\to2}
 \le C_{n,d,c_0,\rho}.
\end{equation}


\subsection{The logarithmic projection estimate}
Let $\cJ=\cJ(\TT)$ and $\cL=\cL(\TT)$ be the input and output stopping
partitions of Section~\ref{mod:sec-geometry}. Define $P_{\cJ}$ and $P_{\cL}$
to average over their respective disjoint cubes. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:tree:eq:projection}
 |\langle U_\TT^\varepsilon f,g\rangle|
 \le C\bigl(1+\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr)
 \|P_{\cJ}|f|\|_2\|P_{\cL}|g|\|_2.
\end{equation}
Here is the proof, including the dependence on $\varepsilon$.

Remove the top phase. Split the smoothed tree into its unphased part and
its small phase error. The latter is bounded by $CM P_{\cJ}|f|(x')$ for
any $x'$ in the same output stopping cube as $x$. Indeed, its kernel is
bounded by $C2^{j-j_+(x)}R_j^{-n}$ on $|x-y|\le CR_j$; every input stopping
cube meeting that support has radius at most $CR_j$, by the definition
of $\cJ$. Enlarge the ball to include those cubes, replace $|f|$ by
$P_{\cJ}|f|$, and sum the geometric coefficients.

For the unphased part, write $f=P_{\cJ}f+(f-P_{\cJ}f)$. At fixed output
$x$, freeze the actual selector $h_x$. Its smoothed shell kernel obeys
\[
 |k_{j,x}^\varepsilon(y)-k_{j,x}^\varepsilon(y')|
 \le CR_j^{-n}\min\{1,|y-y'|/(\varepsilon R_j)\}.
\]
Using mean zero of $f-P_{\cJ}f$ on each input cube gives a positive
boundary-average operator with relative-scale weights
\[
 \omega_\varepsilon(k)=\min\{1,C\varepsilon^{-1}D^{-k}\},\qquad k\ge0.
\]
Indeed, with $r_J=D^{s(J)}$, a dominating operator at relative scale $k$ is
\[
 S_ku(x)=\sum_{J\in\cJ}
 \frac{\mathbf1_{B(c(J),C D^kr_J)}(x)}{(D^kr_J)^n}
 \int_Ju(y)\,dy.
\]
Every input point belongs to at most one $J$. Hence, for $y\in J$,
\[
 S_k^*v(y)=(D^kr_J)^{-n}
 \int_{B(c(J),C D^kr_J)}v(x)\,dx\le C Mv(y),
\]
with a constant independent of $k$. In this application
$u=P_{\cJ}|f|$, since its integral on $J$ equals that of $|f|$.
Moving the output point inside its stopping cube changes the ball
constant only: its distance to the original point is bounded by the
smallest active radius. Thus $\|S_k\|_{2\to2}\le C$. Since
\[
 \sum_{k\ge0}\omega_\varepsilon(k)\le C(1+\log(1/\varepsilon)),
\]
the full boundary-average operator has this logarithmic $L^2$ bound.

For the averaged input $P_{\cJ}f$, use the path obtained by smoothing the
input on each shell at scale $\varepsilon R_j$. Its unphased $L^2$ jump
norm is uniformly bounded: compare it to the original unphased path by
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:diagonal-unphased}, exactly as in
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:approx}. The non-tangential version, in which the output
center is moved within the initial-scale cube, costs at most
$C(1+\log(1/\varepsilon))Mf$. To verify this, freeze the same selector
and the same active-scale interval at both centers, use the last kernel
difference bound at each scale, and sum $\omega_\varepsilon(k)$. At the
second center the frozen expression is one paired witness for its full
smoothed path, so it is bounded by that path norm. Convexity and the
output partition guarantee that the distance between the centers is at
most a fixed multiple of the smallest active scale. No continuity of
$h_x$ in $x$ is used.

Consequently a nonnegative function $b_f$ exists such that
$|U_\TT^\varepsilon f(x)|\le b_f(x')$ for all $x,x'$ in the same output
stopping cube, and
\[
 \|b_f\|_2\le C(1+\log(1/\varepsilon))\|P_{\cJ}|f|\|_2.
\]
Average in $x'$ on each cube and use Cauchy--Schwarz. This proves
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:projection}. The step replaces pointwise Hölder
regularity by the displayed summable difference modulus; it does not
replace the actual jump selector by an arbitrary bounded-$V^2$ function.

If $|E\cap L|\le\delta|L|$ for every $L\in\cL$, then
\[
 \|P_{\cL}(|g|\mathbf1_E)\|_2\le\sqrt\delta\|g\mathbf1_E\|_2.
\]
Thus \eqref{mod:tree:eq:projection} supplies the smoothed row diagonal
bound at density $\delta\le C2^{-m}$. Equations
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:approx} and \eqref{mod:tree:eq:projection} verify both
single-tree inputs used in the subsequent forest assembly.




\section{Forests and the conical theorem}\label{mod:sec-assembly}
We combine the estimates while keeping the same kernel and normalization
at every density generation.

\subsection{A double-density estimate for one tree}
\begin{proposition}\label{mod:double-density}
Suppose the input and output supports satisfy
$|F\cap J|\le C\delta_2|J|$ for $J\in\mathcal J(\TT)$ and
$|E\cap L|\le C\delta_1|L|$ for $L\in\mathcal L(\TT)$, where $E$ is the
effective output set. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:double-density-eq}
 \|\mathbf1_EU_\TT\mathbf1_F\|_{2\to2}
 \le C\sqrt{\delta_1\delta_2}
 \left[1+\log\frac1{\delta_1\delta_2}\right]
\end{equation}
when $0<\delta_1,\delta_2\le1$; the operator is zero if either density
vanishes. Constants absorb fixed factors in the density conditions.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Cauchy--Schwarz on each stopping cube gives
\[
 \|P_{\mathcal J}|f|\|_2\le C\sqrt{\delta_2}\|f\|_2,
 \qquad
 \|P_{\mathcal L}|g|\|_2\le C\sqrt{\delta_1}\|g\|_2
\]
for the restricted inputs. Combine
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:approx} and
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:projection}. With
$\delta_{12}=\sqrt{\delta_1\delta_2}$ this gives
\[
 |\langle U_\TT f,g\rangle|
 \le C\{\delta_{12}[1+\log(1/\varepsilon)]+\varepsilon^\beta\}
 \|f\|_2\|g\|_2.
\]
Choose $\varepsilon=\varepsilon_0\delta_{12}^{1/\beta}$. This proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Local correlation summed into rows}
A row is a family of trees with pairwise disjoint tops. The support
margin makes a row act diagonally on the corresponding orthogonal sum
of input spaces. Let $R_i^\varepsilon$ be its smoothed operator.
For two different rows, Theorem~\ref{mod:tree:thm:local} gives
\begin{equation}\label{mod:row-correlation}
 \|R_i^\varepsilon(R_j^\varepsilon)^*\|_{2\to2}
 \le C\varepsilon^{-2}A^{-\eta}.
\end{equation}
Here is the summation without a tree-count loss. Localize $g$ to each
tree's effective output set, and let
$\mathscr S_\TT g=|U_\TT^*g|+C\varepsilon^{-1}M(|g^0|)+|g^0|$.
The single-tree bound in \eqref{mod:tree:eq:single-tree-uniform} gives
$\|\mathscr S_\TT g\|_2\le C\varepsilon^{-1}\|g^0\|_2$.
For a fixed tree in the first row, its intersections with tops of the
second row are disjoint. Therefore
\[
 \sum_{\TT,\TT'}
 \|\mathscr S_\TT g\|_{L^2(I_\TT\cap I_{\TT'})}^2
 \le\sum_\TT\|\mathscr S_\TT g\|_2^2
 \le C\varepsilon^{-2}\|g\|_2^2.
\]
The second row has the same bound. Cauchy--Schwarz over tree pairs proves
\eqref{mod:row-correlation}. A global two-tree inequality would not
justify this step; the intersection in the local theorem is used exactly.

\begin{proposition}[Two forest estimates]\label{mod:forest}
Let $\mathfrak F_m$ be a forest of density generation $m$, and suppose
$\delta_2(\mathfrak F_m)\le\zeta\le1$. Then
\begin{align}
 \|T_{\mathfrak F_m}\|_{2\to2}
 &\le C(1+m)2^{-m/2},\label{mod:forest-first}\\
 \|T_{\mathfrak F_m}\mathbf1_F\|_{2\to2}
 &\le C\sqrt\zeta[1+m+\log(1/\zeta)].\label{mod:forest-second}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Fix $\varepsilon_0=1/8$ and use the common smoothing scale
$\varepsilon_m=\varepsilon_0 2^{-K(m+1)}$ and separation
$A_m=2^{Z(m+1)}$. The constants satisfy
\eqref{mod:parameter-choice}. Because tops have multiplicity at most
$2^m$ and effective outputs of distinct trees are disjoint,
\[
 \|T_{\mathfrak F_m}-T_{\mathfrak F_m}^{\varepsilon_m}\|_{2\to2}
 \le C2^{m/2}\varepsilon_m^\beta\le C2^{-3m/2}.
\]
The smoothed projection estimate and the output density give
$\|R_i^{\varepsilon_m}\|_{2\to2}
\le C(1+m)2^{-m/2}$ for every row. There are at most $2^m$ rows.
Using the disjoint effective output sets in the Gram matrix and
\eqref{mod:row-correlation},
\[
 \|T_{\mathfrak F_m}^{\varepsilon_m}\|_{2\to2}^2
 \le C(1+m)^2 2^{-m}
       +C2^m\varepsilon_m^{-2}A_m^{-\eta}
 \le C(1+m)^2 2^{-m}.
\]
This proves \eqref{mod:forest-first}.

For \eqref{mod:forest-second}, use
Proposition~\ref{mod:double-density} with output density $C2^{-m}$ and
input density $\zeta$. Sum the squared norms over trees, using disjoint
outputs and top overlap at most $2^m$. The factors $2^{m/2}$ and
$2^{-m/2}$ cancel. This proves the second estimate. These are estimates
for the same unsmoothed operator; their proofs need not choose the same
auxiliary smoothing scale.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Strong bounds for \texorpdfstring{$1<p<2$}{1<p<2}}
Fix $1<p<2$. A geometric mean of the two bounds in
Proposition~\ref{mod:forest}, with powers $1-1/p$ and $1/p$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{mod:forest-lowp}
 \|T_{\mathfrak F_m}\mathbf1_F\|_{2\to2}
 \le C_p(1+m)2^{-c_pm}\zeta^{1/p-1/2},
 \qquad c_p=(p-1)/(2p)>0.
\end{equation}
To verify the logarithm absorption, the initial exponent of $\zeta$ is
$1/(2p)$ and
$1/(2p)-(1/p-1/2)=(p-1)/(2p)>0$.
The positive difference of exponents absorbs the logarithmic factor.

For an antichain and $|f|\le\mathbf1_F$, size and input density give
$\|T_\mathfrak A f\|_\infty\le C\zeta$. Since a point belongs to only one
antichain output tile, $|T_\mathfrak A f|\le CMf$. Put $r=2-1/p>1$.
Interpolation between these elementary $L^r$ and $L^\infty$ bounds gives
\[
 \|T_\mathfrak A f\|_2\le C_p\zeta^{1/(2p)}|F|^{1/2}.
\]
