Take the geometric mean of this bound with
Proposition~\ref{mod:antichain}, with powers $2-p$ and $p-1$.
At density generation $m$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:antichain-lowp}
 \|T_{\mathfrak A_m}f\|_2
 \le C_p2^{-\vartheta(p-1)m}
 \zeta^{1/p-1/2}|F|^{1/2}.
\end{equation}

For $|G|>0$, apply Proposition~\ref{mod:geometry} with its ambient
set $H$, put $G'=G\cap H$, and pair the surviving operator with
$g\mathbf1_{G\setminus G'}$, $|g|\le1$. The discarded tiles vanish
on this set, so this is exactly the same restricted full operator.
For $|G|=0$ the desired integral is zero. The number of families per level
is polynomial in $m$, while \eqref{mod:forest-lowp} and
\eqref{mod:antichain-lowp} decay geometrically. With
$\zeta\le C\min\{1,|F|/|G|\}$, this yields
\begin{equation}\label{mod:restricted-major-subset}
 \int_{G\setminus G'}|Tf|
 \le C_p|F|^{1/p}|G|^{1-1/p},\qquad |G'|\le|G|/2.
\end{equation}
Every constant is uniform over the fixed finite linearization.
Apply this to finite subsets of level sets of $|Tf|$ to obtain restricted
weak type $(p,p)$. Restricted weak-type interpolation between two indices
in $(1,2)$ proves the strong estimate throughout that interval. Apply the
argument to the linearization maximizing the finite path norm; its
sublinearity follows from Lemma~\ref{mod:pathlemma}.

\subsection{Strong bounds for \texorpdfstring{$2<p<\infty$}{2<p<infinity}}
No adjoint identity for the maximally modulated operator is used.
For each fixed $r>1$, remove a tree's top phase. Its unphased part is a
restriction of one jump witness and is bounded by
Proposition~\ref{mod:unphased}. Its phase error is bounded by
$C\sum_{j\le j_+(x)}2^{j-j_+(x)}Mf\le CMf$. Hence
\[
 \|T_\TT\|_{r\to r}\le C_r.
\]
Rows have disjoint tops, and different rows have disjoint effective
outputs, so a forest with at most $2^m$ rows satisfies
\[
 \|T_{\mathfrak F_m}\|_{r\to r}\le C_r2^{m/r}.
\]
For $p>2$ take $r=2p$ and interpolate with
\eqref{mod:forest-first}. The $L^2$ interpolation weight is
$1/(p-1)$; consequently
\begin{equation}\label{mod:forest-highp}
 \|T_{\mathfrak F_m}\|_{p\to p}
 \le C_p(1+m)^{1/(p-1)}2^{-m/[p(p-1)]}.
\end{equation}
An antichain is pointwise bounded by $CM$, so interpolation with
\eqref{mod:antichain-L2} gives
\begin{equation}\label{mod:antichain-highp}
 \|T_{\mathfrak A_m}\|_{p\to p}
 \le C_p2^{-\vartheta m/(p-1)}.
\end{equation}
For bounded compactly supported $f$, choose $F=\operatorname{supp}f$ in
the geometric decomposition and discard its input-density improvement.
Again use $G'=G\cap H$ for the output restriction and remove spatial
tiles only when $I_p\subset H$; null $G$ gives a zero integral.
Summing \eqref{mod:forest-highp}--\eqref{mod:antichain-highp} gives
\[
 \int_{G\setminus G'}|Tf|
 \le C_p\|f\|_p|G|^{1-1/p}.
\]
The level-set argument proves weak type $(p,p)$ for each $p>2$.
Interpolation gives strong type for every $p>2$. Interpolation between an exponent below two and an exponent above two
then yields the case $p=2$. This completes the proof of
Theorem~\ref{mod:cone} for finite phase and parameter sets.

\begin{remark}[Uniformity of constants]\label{mod:uniformity}
The dimension, degree, finite radial exponent, cone aperture, and
normalized support annuli are fixed first. The positive shell and
correlation exponents depend only on these data. In particular the
single-tree exponent $\beta=1/(16\rho)$ is independent of the geometric
integer $a$ and base $D$. Choose the fixed geometric $a$ and
$D=2^{100a^2}$, then choose $K$ and a sufficiently large structural $Z$
by \eqref{mod:parameter-choice}, using the positive correlation exponent
for this same grid. The parameter $a$ used in $D$ and in the density is
never changed in this selection. Splitting a fixed-ratio shell changes
constants but does not change the stated positive exponents.
The smoothness cost is recorded as $L=C\varepsilon^{-1}$ and never
absorbed into a varying geometric parameter. The analytic estimates are
uniform in the least singular value of $A$, in phase coefficients, in
finite cutoffs, and in witness lengths. These uniformities permit the angular normalization in the next section.
\end{remark}




\section{Cancellative atomic decomposition on the sphere}\label{mod:atom:sec:atoms}\label{mod:sec-atoms}
Throughout the atomic argument assume $n\ge2$. For $n=1$, put
$H^1_0(\mathbb S^0)=\{\Omega:\Omega(1)+\Omega(-1)=0\}$ with its
$L^1$ norm. Then $\|\Omega\|_\infty\le\|\Omega\|_{H^1}$, and
Theorem~\ref{mod:cone} with $A=I$ proves the main estimate, including
every fixed radial profile $b\in\mathcal V^\rho$, $1\le\rho<\infty$.
No strict angular enlargement is asserted in this two-point case. Put
$m_n=\sigma(\Sph^{n-1})$, fix $r_*=\pi/16$, and let
\[
 A_\tau=\operatorname{diag}(1,\tau,\ldots,\tau),\quad
 \Gamma=\{\theta:\theta_1\ge2^{-1/2}\},\quad \tau_*=\tan r_*.
\]
The angular Hardy norm is fixed below. The spatial input exponent
remains $1<p<\infty$. For comparison with the earlier angular
hypothesis, write $\LL(\Omega)=\mathfrak L(\Omega)$ as in
\eqref{eq:gauge}. A logarithm will be used only to prove the inclusion
of mean-zero $L\log L$ data in the angular Hardy space; the Hardy-space
estimate itself uses its atomic norm.

\begin{definition}[The real angular Hardy space]\label{mod:atom:def:H1}
A cancellative spherical $(1,\infty)$-atom is a measurable function
$a$ supported in a geodesic ball $B\subset\mathbb S^{n-1}$ such that
$\int a\,d\sigma=0$ and $\|a\|_\infty\le\sigma(B)^{-1}$. The whole
sphere is allowed as a supporting ball. The exceptional constant atom
is $a_*=m_n^{-1}\mathbf1_{\mathbb S^{n-1}}$. We use the standard real
atomic definition on the sphere: $\Omega\in H^1(\mathbb S^{n-1})$ if
\[
 \Omega=c a_*+\sum_{\nu\ge1}\lambda_\nu a_\nu,
 \qquad |c|+\sum_{\nu\ge1}|\lambda_\nu|<\infty.
\]
The norm $\|\Omega\|_{H^1}$ is the infimum of this coefficient sum.
All these expansions converge absolutely in $L^1$, because every
atom has $L^1$ norm at most one. This is the real atomic Hardy space
of the angular variable; see \cite{CoifmanWeiss77,Colzani85}. It is
not a spatial $H^1(\mathbb R^n)$ assumption on the input.
Write
\[
 H^1_0(\mathbb S^{n-1})
 =\{\Omega\in H^1(\mathbb S^{n-1}):\textstyle\int\Omega\,d\sigma=0\}.
\]
Integration of the absolutely convergent expansion shows that
$c=\int\Omega\,d\sigma$. Thus every mean-zero element admits an
expansion containing only cancellative atoms, and
$\|\Omega\|_1\le\|\Omega\|_{H^1}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
A small-cap atom is a measurable complex function $a$ supported in a
geodesic ball $B(e,r)\subset\Sph^{n-1}$, $0<r\le r_*$, such that
\[
 \int a\,d\sigma=0,\qquad \norm a_\infty\le\sigma(B(e,r))^{-1}.
\]
A global atom satisfies $\int a=0$ and $\norm a_\infty\le m_n^{-1}$,
without a support restriction.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[A dyadic spherical filtration]\label{mod:atom:lem:grid}
There is a nested measurable partition of $\Sph^{n-1}$, beginning with
the whole sphere, with the following properties. The number of children
of each set is uniformly bounded; child and parent measures are
comparable from below; the filtration differentiates $L^1$ functions;
and each non-root set $Q$ is contained in a spherical ball $B_Q$ with
$\sigma(B_Q)\le C_n\sigma(Q)$. Sets with $\operatorname{rad}(B_Q)>r_*$
have measure bounded below by a positive dimensional constant.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Partition the boundary of the cube $[-1,1]^n$ into its faces, assign
shared boundaries by a fixed measurable rule, and project radially to the
sphere. The radial maps on the faces are bi-Lipschitz with surface
Jacobians bounded above and below by dimensional constants. Subdivide
each face into its usual dyadic cubes and transfer the subdivisions to
the sphere. Boundaries have surface measure zero and can retain the
same fixed assignment convention at every generation. The assertions
follow from Euclidean dyadic differentiation and the fixed bounds on
these finitely many charts. Diameter and measure are comparable to the
appropriate powers of the face-cube sidelength. Consequently only
finitely many generations can have enclosing balls larger than $r_*$.
\end{proof}

Write $M_{\mathcal D}$ for the maximal operator of this filtration, applied
to $|\Omega|$.
\begin{lemma}[The single logarithmic estimate]\label{mod:atom:lem:entropy}
For $\Omega\in L\log L(\Sph^{n-1})$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:atom:eq:entropy}
 \norm{M_{\mathcal D}\Omega}_1\le C_n\LL(\Omega).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The zero function requires no argument. Put $A_0=\norm\Omega_1$ and
$\lambda_0=A_0/m_n$. For $\lambda\ge\lambda_0$, the part of $|\Omega|$
bounded by $\lambda/2$ has maximal function at most $\lambda/2$.
The maximal disjoint stopping sets for the remaining part therefore give
\[
 \sigma\{M_{\mathcal D}\Omega>\lambda\}
 \le\frac2\lambda\int_{\{|\Omega|>\lambda/2\}}|\Omega|\,d\sigma.
\]
Integrate in $\lambda$. Tonelli yields
\[
 \norm{M_{\mathcal D}\Omega}_1
 \le A_0+2\int|\Omega|\log^+(2|\Omega|/\lambda_0)\,d\sigma.
\]
Since $m_n$ depends only on $n$, this is bounded by the right-hand side
of \eqref{mod:atom:eq:entropy}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Summable small-cap Hardy expansion]\label{mod:atom:prop:atoms}
If $\Omega\in H^1_0(\mathbb S^{n-1})$, then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:atom:eq:atoms}
 \Omega=\sum_{\nu=1}^\infty\lambda_\nu a_\nu
 \quad\text{in }L^1(\mathbb S^{n-1}),\qquad
 \sum_\nu|\lambda_\nu|\le C_n\|\Omega\|_{H^1},
\end{equation}
where every $a_\nu$ is a small-cap atom. Conversely, every such
absolutely summable series belongs to $H^1_0$, with Hardy norm at most
its coefficient sum. In particular, restricting the atomic norm to
small caps gives an equivalent norm on $H^1_0$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The zero element has the zero expansion.  Otherwise choose a mean-zero
Hardy expansion with coefficient sum at most twice its positive Hardy
norm; its exceptional constant term is zero.  Atoms supported in caps of radius greater than $r_*$ are uniformly
bounded multiples of global atoms, by positivity and rotational
invariance of the measure of a cap of the fixed radius $r_*$.  It remains
to decompose a global atom uniformly.

Choose a finite cover of the sphere by open geodesic caps $B_i$ of a
fixed radius at most $r_*$.  The overlap graph is connected: otherwise
the unions of two graph components would disconnect the connected
sphere.  Every nonempty overlap has positive surface measure.
Disjointize the cover to measurable sets $P_i\subset B_i$ and put
$\psi_i=\mathbf1_{B_i}/\sigma(B_i)$.  For a global atom $a$, set
$m_i=\int_{P_i}a$.  The functions
$\mathbf1_{P_i}a-m_i\psi_i$ are bounded, mean zero, supported in $B_i$,
and normalize to finitely many small-cap atoms with uniformly bounded
coefficient sum.  Also $\sum m_i=0$ and $\sum|m_i|\le\|a\|_1\le1$.

Fix a root of the overlap graph and, for each vertex $i$, one finite
path from $i$ to the root.  Then
\[
 \sum_i m_i\psi_i=\sum_i m_i(\psi_i-\psi_{\rm root}).
\]
Telescope each difference along its fixed path.  For an edge $uv$ let
$\psi_{uv}=\mathbf1_{B_u\cap B_v}/\sigma(B_u\cap B_v)$ and write
$\psi_u-\psi_v=(\psi_u-\psi_{uv})+(\psi_{uv}-\psi_v)$.
Each difference is bounded, mean zero, and supported in one of the
small caps.  All paths and all positive measures are fixed in advance,
so normalization gives a uniform finite coefficient bound.  No smooth
partition, smooth bump, or spanning-tree existence is needed.

Apply this genuine finite decomposition to every atom of the original
absolutely summable expansion.  The resulting countable double series
has absolutely summable coefficients; reindex it by the natural numbers.
Its $L^1$ limit is the original datum, and its coefficient sum is bounded
by a dimensional constant times the original coefficient sum.
Absorb the initial factor two into the dimensional constant.  Taking
infima also gives the asserted comparison of atomic norms.  The converse follows directly from the definition,
absolute $L^1$ convergence, and termwise integration.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The $L\log L$ inclusion]\label{mod:atom:prop:LlogL-H1}
If $\int\Omega\,d\sigma=0$ and $\LL(\Omega)<\infty$, then
\[
 \Omega\in H^1_0(\mathbb S^{n-1}),\qquad
 \|\Omega\|_{H^1}\le C_n\LL(\Omega).
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Write $d=n-1\ge1$, $M=\|\Omega\|_1$, and
$E_A(f)=\int |f|\log^+(|f|/A)$.  The case $M=0$ is immediate.
We give a finite-chart proof using ordinary Euclidean dyadic cubes;
in particular, this proof does not require the preceding spherical
filtration lemma.

Cover the sphere by the $2n$ closed coordinate patches
$U_{i,\varepsilon}=\{\theta:|\theta|=1,
\varepsilon\theta_i\ge1/n\}$, $\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}$.
Disjointize this finite cover to measurable sets $P_\alpha\subset U_\alpha$.
For each patch choose the normalized indicator $\psi_\alpha$ of the
geodesic cap of radius $\pi/16$ about its signed coordinate pole.
This cap has positive surface measure and is contained in $U_\alpha$.
Set
\[
 m_\alpha=\int_{P_\alpha}\Omega,\qquad
 \Omega_\alpha=\mathbf1_{P_\alpha}\Omega-m_\alpha\psi_\alpha,
 \qquad R=\sum_\alpha m_\alpha\psi_\alpha.
\]
Then $\sum|m_\alpha|\le M$, each $\Omega_\alpha$ is supported in its
patch and has mean zero, $\|\Omega_\alpha\|_1\le2M$, and
$\Omega=R+\sum\Omega_\alpha$ in $L^1$.
The remainder has mean zero and $\|R\|_\infty\le C_nM$, so it is a
constant multiple, of size at most $C_nM$, of a global atom.
The elementary inequality, for $x,y\ge0$,
\[
 (x+y)\log^+((x+y)/M)
 \le2\log2\,(x+y)+2x\log^+(x/M)+2y\log^+(y/M)
\]
and the fixed bounds for the normalized indicators give
\[
 E_M(\Omega_\alpha)\le C_n\{M+E_M(\Omega)\}.
\]

It remains to treat one mean-zero function $F$ on a coordinate patch,
with $\|F\|_1\le2M$.  By the antipodal isometry it suffices to use a
positive patch.  Its gnomonic parameterization is
$T(z)=(1,z)/|(1,z)|$, with the designated coordinate inserted in the
appropriate position.  The inverse coordinate norm on the patch is at
most $n$.  Fix, dimensionally, a translated dyadic root box containing
this inverse image in its interior, of volume at least one.  Choose a fixed bounded source ball $S$ containing the translated-back
root box.
The actual pullback measure satisfies
\[
 K_n^{-1}\,dz\le T^*(d\sigma)\le dz\quad\hbox{on }S.
\]
Indeed the chart is Lipschitz with constant one, and its inverse is
Lipschitz on this bounded domain; the already proved identification of
surface measure with Euclidean-normalized Hausdorff measure gives both
inequalities.  The Radon--Nikodym derivative $w$ therefore satisfies
$K_n^{-1}\le w\le1$ almost everywhere.  The zero extension of
$f(z)=w(z)F(Tz)$ has mean zero, $L^1$ mass $\|F\|_1$, and
$E_M(f)\le E_M(F)$.  Translating the source makes its support lie in the
fixed ordinary dyadic root cube.

For the usual Euclidean dyadic grid perform the maximal-cube
Calder\'on--Zygmund decomposition of $f$ at
$t_k=2^{k+1}M$.  Write its good part as $g_k$ and its disjoint maximal
bad cubes as $\mathcal Q_k$.  These cubes lie inside the root cube:
an ancestor of the root has average at most $\|f\|_1/|Q_0|\le2M$,
and any bad cube intersecting the support is nested with the root.
The usual parent-cube estimate gives $|g_k|\le2^dt_k$ almost everywhere.
For $Q\in\mathcal Q_k$ put
\[
 h_{k,Q}=\mathbf1_Q(g_{k+1}-g_k).
\]
Actual nesting of the stopping cubes gives $\int h_{k,Q}=0$ and
$|h_{k,Q}|\le3\cdot2^dt_k$, and
$g_{k+1}-g_k=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal Q_k}h_{k,Q}$.
The good part preserves the integral.  Moreover
$\|f-g_k\|_1\le2\int_{O_k}|f|\to0$, since
$|O_k|\le\|f\|_1/t_k\to0$.

The coefficient payment is a direct tail count, not an invocation of a
spherical maximal theorem.  For a bad cube at height $2a$,
$a|Q|\le\int_Q|f|\mathbf1_{\{|f|>a\}}$.
Summing the disjoint cubes and then $a=2^kM$ yields
\[
 \sum_{k\ge0}t_k\sum_{Q\in\mathcal Q_k}|Q|
 \le2\left(\|f\|_1+(\log2)^{-1}E_M(f)\right).
\]
Consequently $f=g_0+\sum_{k,Q}h_{k,Q}$ absolutely in $L^1$,
with total amplitude-times-cube-volume bounded by
$C_n(M+E_M(f))$; the initial term is bounded, mean zero, and supported
in the fixed root box, with the same dimensional payment.

Transport signed densities back by $h\mapsto(h/w)\circ T^{-1}$,
extended by zero.  This is an $L^1$ isometry on the restricted source
measure and preserves integrals, hence transports the countable series.
The image of any translated cube of sidelength $\ell$ is contained in
a spherical supporting ball of measure at most $C_n\ell^d$.
For small $\ell$ this follows from the chart Lipschitz bound, the
angle--chord comparison, and the proved cap measure bound; for large
$\ell$ use the whole sphere.  Since $w^{-1}\le K_n$, every transported
$h_{k,Q}$ is a cancellative spherical atom times a coefficient at most
$C_nt_k|Q|$.  Thus
$\|F\|_{H^1}\le C_n(M+E_M(F))$.
Apply this to the finitely many $\Omega_\alpha$ and add the global
remainder.  The result is
$\|\Omega\|_{H^1}\le C_n(M+E_M(\Omega))=C_n\mathfrak L(\Omega)$,
with an actual absolutely summable atomic expansion.
\end{proof}

For completeness the inclusion holds without imposing mean zero.
For $\Omega\in L\log L$, write $c=\int\Omega\,d\sigma$ and
$\Omega=c a_*+(\Omega-c a_*)$. The first summand has Hardy norm
$|c|\le\|\Omega\|_1$. The second has mean zero and satisfies
$\mathfrak L(\Omega-c a_*)\le C_n\mathfrak L(\Omega)$ directly:
with $M=\|\Omega\|_1$ and $M'=\|\Omega-c a_*\|_1\le2M$, the
entropy subtraction inequality above bounds $E_M(\Omega-c a_*)$ by
$C_n(M+E_M(\Omega))$.  Changing the normalization from $M$ to $M'>0$
costs at most $M'\log^+(M/M')\le M$; the case $M'=0$ is immediate.
The gauge bound and the mean-zero inclusion therefore give
$\|\Omega\|_{H^1}\le C_n\mathfrak L(\Omega)$.

\begin{proposition}[Strictness of the angular extension]\label{mod:atom:prop:strict-H1}
For $n\ge2$ the inclusion
$L\log L(\mathbb S^{n-1})\cap\{\int\Omega=0\}\subset H^1_0(\mathbb S^{n-1})$
is strict.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Put $d=n-1\ge1$ and fix a positive gnomonic chart
$T:\mathbb R^d\to\mathbb S^d$.  On each fixed bounded source ball its
surface density $w$ satisfies $0<c_d\le w\le1$.
For $j\ge0$ put $N_j=4^j+j+3$.  Take the dyadic cube $Q_j$ of
sidelength $2^{-N_j}$ whose first-coordinate interval begins at
$2^{-j}$ and whose other intervals begin at zero.  These cubes are
pairwise disjoint: their first-coordinate intervals are contained in
$[2^{-j},(1+1/8)2^{-j}]$.  They lie in a fixed bounded source ball.
Choose two distinct dyadic children $Q_j^+,Q_j^-$ of $Q_j$ and set
\[
 A_j=(2|Q_j^+|)^{-1},\qquad
 u_j=A_j(\mathbf1_{Q_j^+}-\mathbf1_{Q_j^-}),\qquad
 g_j(Tx)=u_j(x)/w(x),
\]
extending $g_j$ by zero outside the chart images of these children.
The exact change of measure gives $\int g_j=0$ and $\|g_j\|_1=1$.
Its support is contained in $TQ_j$, and the cap envelope bound
$\sigma(B_j)\le C_d|Q_j|$, together with $w^{-1}\le c_d^{-1}$,
shows that $g_j$ is a multiple of an actual cancellative spherical atom
with coefficient at most a fixed dimensional constant, since
$A_j|Q_j|=2^{d-1}$.
Consequently
\[
 \Omega=\sum_{j\ge0}2^{-j}g_j
\]
converges absolutely in $L^1$ and defines an element of $H^1_0$.
The supports are disjoint, and $w\le1$ implies
$|g_j|\ge A_j$ wherever $g_j\ne0$.  Thus the entropy integral on the
$j$th support alone is at least
\[
 2^{-j}\log^+(2^{-j}A_j)
 \ge 2^{-j}4^j\log2=2^j\log2.
\]
Indeed $\log A_j=(d(N_j+1)-1)\log2$, so
$\log(2^{-j}A_j)\ge4^j\log2$ for every $d\ge1$.
On this finite-measure sphere, $L\log L$ membership implies finiteness
of the unnormalized entropy integral.  The displayed lower bounds are
unbounded, whereas each would then be bounded by that full integral.
Hence $\Omega\notin L\log L$, proving strictness.
\end{proof}



\section{Affine normalization and the exact sharp truncation}\label{mod:atom:sec:affine}
\begin{lemma}[Spherical Jacobian]\label{mod:atom:lem:jacobian}
For an invertible real matrix $L$, the map
$\theta\mapsto L\theta/|L\theta|$ has surface Jacobian
$|\det L||L\theta|^{-n}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For any bounded measurable $a$, make the substitution $w=Lz$ in
\[
 \int_{1<|Lz|<2}|\det L|\,a((Lz)')|Lz|^{-n}\,dz.
\]
Integrating in polar coordinates first in $w$ and then in $z$ gives,
respectively,
\[
 (\log2)\int a(\omega)\,d\sigma(\omega),\qquad
 (\log2)\int |\det L||L\theta|^{-n}a((L\theta)')\,d\sigma(\theta).
\]
This identity for every $a$ proves the formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Uniform normalization of one cap atom]\label{mod:atom:prop:cap}
Let $a$ be a small-cap atom supported in $B(e,r)$, $0<r\le r_*$. Choose
$U\in O(n)$ with $Ue_1=e$, let $\tau=\tan r$, and set $L=UA_\tau$.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{mod:atom:eq:thetaL}
 \Theta_L(\theta)=|\det L||L\theta|^{-n}
 a\left(\frac{L\theta}{|L\theta|}\right).
\end{equation}
Then
\[
 \supp\Theta_L\subset\Gamma,\qquad
 \norm{\Theta_L}_\infty\le C_n,\qquad
 \int\Theta_L\,d\sigma=0.
\]
For $f_L(v)=f(Lv)$, the exact transformed trajectory is
\begin{equation}\label{mod:atom:eq:transform}
 T_{a,t}^{Q,b}f(Lu)
 =S_{\Theta_L,t}^{Q\circ L,A_\tau,b}f_L(u).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
If $\Theta_L(\theta)\ne0$, the cap condition gives
$\theta_1>0$ and
$\tau|\theta_\perp|/\theta_1\le\tan r=\tau$.
Hence $|\theta_\perp|\le\theta_1$, so $\theta_1\ge2^{-1/2}$ and
$|A_\tau\theta|\ge2^{-1/2}$. Since
$|\det L|=\tau^{n-1}$ and $\sigma(B(e,r))\asymp_nr^{n-1}$,
\eqref{mod:atom:eq:thetaL} gives the uniform $L^\infty$ bound. Mean zero follows
from Lemma~\ref{mod:atom:lem:jacobian}.

The change of variables $x=Lu$, $y=Lv$ gives
\[
 |\det L|K_a(L(u-v))=K_{\Theta_L}(u-v),\qquad
 |L(u-v)|=|A_\tau(u-v)|.
\]
It also sends $e^{iQ(y)}$ to $e^{iQ(Lv)}$ and the radial factor
$b(|x-y|)$ to $b(|A_\tau(u-v)|)$, proving
\eqref{mod:atom:eq:transform}. Both the sharp cutoff and the radial profile are transformed
exactly into functions of $|A_\tau(u-v)|$. No Euclidean cutoff
or Euclidean radial profile is substituted for them.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Uniform estimate for angular atoms]
\label{mod:atom:cor:atomop}
Let $d\ge0$, $1<p<\infty$, and $1\le\rho<\infty$. For every
small-cap atom, every finite $\mathcal F\subset\PP_d(\R^n)$ and
finite $D\subset(0,\infty)$, and every fixed
$b\in\mathcal V^\rho$, the following holds for bounded compactly
supported $f$:
\[
 \left\|\max_{Q\in\mathcal F}\NN_D(T_{a,t}^{Q,b}f:t\in D)\right\|_p
 \le C_{n,d,p,\rho}\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}\norm f_p.
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply \eqref{mod:cone-bound} to \eqref{mod:atom:eq:transform}. Composition by $L$ is
an invertible map on $\PP_d$, preserving total degree. The change of
variables in the output norm contributes $|\det L|^{1/p}$, whereas
$\norm{f_L}_p=|\det L|^{-1/p}\norm f_p$. These factors cancel exactly.
Use the uniform angular bound in Proposition~\ref{mod:atom:prop:cap}.
\end{proof}

\section{Absolute annular integrals and local tail bounds}\label{mod:atom:sec:tails}
\begin{lemma}[Local positive tail estimate]\label{mod:atom:lem:tail}
Let $\Omega\in L^1(\Sph^{n-1})$, $1\le p<\infty$, $a>0$, and $M>0$.
Set
\[
 k_a^+(z)=|\Omega(z')||z|^{-n}\mathbf1_{\{|z|>a\}}.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{mod:atom:eq:tail}
 \norm{k_a^+*|f|}_{L^p(B(0,M))}
 \le C_{n,a,M,p}\norm\Omega_1\norm f_p.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The portion in any fixed bounded annulus is an $L^1$ convolution kernel.
For the tail, write
$k_j^+(z)=|\Omega(z')||z|^{-n}\mathbf1_{\{2^j<|z|\le2^{j+1}\}}$.
The row integral is at most $C\norm\Omega_1$. For each fixed $y$, the
intersection of a ray $y+r\theta$ with $B(0,M)$ has length at most $2M$.
Consequently
\[
 \sup_y\int_{B(0,M)} k_j^+(x-y)\,dx
 \le 2M2^{-j}\norm\Omega_1.
\]
Thus the positive operator $f\mapsto\mathbf1_{B(0,M)}(k_j^+*f)$ has
$L^1\to L^1$ norm at most $CM2^{-j}\norm\Omega_1$ and
$L^\infty\to L^\infty$ norm at most $C\norm\Omega_1$. Interpolation gives
an $L^p$ norm at most
$C(M2^{-j})^{1/p}\norm\Omega_1$. This is summable for the large scales.
Adding the bounded annuli proves \eqref{mod:atom:eq:tail}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Common representatives]\label{mod:atom:cor:representatives}
For $\Omega\in L^1$, a fixed bounded measurable $b$, and
$f\in L^p$, $1\le p<\infty$, there is a full-measure set $X_f$,
independent of $Q$ and $t$, on which every $T_{\Omega,t}^{Q,b}f$ is absolutely
convergent. On $X_f$ the path is jointly continuous in positive $t$ and
the finite-dimensional coefficient vector of $Q$. Its absolute tail
tends to zero as $t\to\infty$, uniformly over all $Q$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply Lemma~\ref{mod:atom:lem:tail} on the countable collection of output balls
$B(0,M)$, $M\in\mathbb N$, and lower cutoffs $a=1/N$, $N\in\mathbb N$.
At a point outside the resulting null set, all positive truncations are
absolutely integrable after multiplying the positive majorant by
$\|b\|_\infty$. For sequences of positive cutoffs and polynomial
coefficients converging to a fixed cutoff and coefficients, use a fixed
smaller positive cutoff as the dominating integrable kernel. Spheres
have Lebesgue measure zero, so the cutoff indicators converge almost
everywhere in the integration variable. Dominated convergence proves
joint continuity. The uniform tail statement follows from $|e^{iQ(y)}|=1$, boundedness
of $b$, and the vanishing tail of an absolutely integrable function.
All coordinates are set to zero on $X_f^c$. The construction depends
on no individual phase and takes no uncountable union of null sets.
\end{proof}

\section{Proof of the angular endpoint theorem}\label{mod:atom:sec:mainproof}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{mod:main}]
Fix $b\in\mathcal V^\rho$, $1\le\rho<\infty$. Start with
bounded compactly supported $f$, and choose the atomic
expansion from Proposition~\ref{mod:atom:prop:atoms}. Let
$\Omega_N=\sum_{\nu\le N}\lambda_\nu a_\nu$.
For finite parameter and phase sets, Lemma~\ref{mod:pathlemma} and Corollary~\ref{mod:atom:cor:atomop} give
\[
 \left\|\max_Q\NN_D(T_{\Omega_N,t}^{Q,b}f)\right\|_p
 \le C\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}\sum_{\nu\le N}|\lambda_\nu|\norm f_p
 \le C\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}\|\Omega\|_{H^1}\norm f_p.
\]
All constants are uniform over these finite sets.

Let $\supp f\subset B(0,R_f)$. For fixed $x$ and $a>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:atom:eq:atomic-limit}
 \sup_Q\sup_{t\ge a}|T_{\Omega_N,t}^{Q,b}f(x)-T_{\Omega,t}^{Q,b}f(x)|
 \le\|b\|_\infty\norm f_\infty\norm{\Omega_N-\Omega}_1
 \log^+\left(\frac{|x|+R_f}{a}\right)\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
This follows by polar coordinates and absolute values. Hence every finite
strict jump witness for any fixed phase passes to the approximating
trajectories. Equivalently, one can use continuity of $\NN_D$ on the
finite-dimensional coordinate space. Fatou gives the same bound for
$\Omega$, first for finite phase and parameter sets. Take increasing
finite sets $\mathcal F_m,D_m$ exhausting, respectively, polynomials
with rational nonconstant coefficients and positive rational cutoffs.
Joint continuity from Corollary~\ref{mod:atom:cor:representatives} gives
\[
 \sup_{Q\in\PP_d}\NN_{(0,\infty)}(T^{Q,b}_{\Omega,t}f)
 =\sup_m\max_{Q\in\mathcal F_m}\NN_{D_m}(T^{Q,b}_{\Omega,t}f).
\]
Indeed, approximate the distinct endpoints of a finite witness by
rational endpoints preserving their order, using a single rational
approximation for each shared endpoint. Approximate its one phase by
a single rational coefficient vector for the entire witness. This
approximates the witness's value arbitrarily well. The reverse
inequality is immediate. The right side is a countable supremum of
measurable functions. Increasing finite exhaustions, monotone
convergence, and $\mathbf J\le\NN$ now give \eqref{mod:target} for
core data. In the equivalent argument with strict jumps, only the
finitely many strict margins of one witness are used at a time.

For general $f\in L^p$, choose bounded compactly supported $f_m$ with
$\sum_m\norm{f_m-f}_p<\infty$. For each positive rational $a$ and
integer $M>0$, Minkowski's inequality and
Lemma~\ref{mod:atom:lem:tail} give
\[
 \left\|\sum_m k_a^+*|f_m-f|\right\|_{L^p(B(0,M))}<\infty.
\]
Consequently, outside one null set, for every positive rational $a$,
\[
 k_a^+*|f_m-f|(x)\longrightarrow0.
\]
Consequently
\[
 \sup_Q\sup_{t\ge a}|T_{\Omega,t}^{Q,b}(f_m-f)(x)|
 \le\|b\|_\infty k_a^+*|f_m-f|(x)\longrightarrow0.
\]
Every finite strict witness therefore passes to the limit. Fatou and
$\norm{f_m}_p\to\norm f_p$ prove \eqref{mod:target}. Corollary~
\ref{mod:atom:cor:representatives} identifies these trajectories with the original
absolutely convergent positive truncations and supplies their common
representative and zero anchor.

Finally, at a fixed output point $x$, define
$Q_{P,x}(y)=P(x,y)-P(x,0)$. Then
\[
 T_{\Omega,t}^{P,b}f(x)=e^{iP(x,0)}T_{\Omega,t}^{Q_{P,x},b}f(x).
\]
The scalar factor is independent of $t$. Conversely every polynomial
$Q(y)$ occurs by setting $P(x,y)=Q(y)$. Thus the two phase suprema of the
jump functional agree pointwise. For fixed $x$, the coefficient
vector of $Q_{P,x}$ and the scalar $e^{iP(x,0)}$ depend continuously
on the coefficients of $P$. The common representative's joint
continuity therefore also holds for the complete two-variable phase
class. This proves all assertions of the theorem.
\end{proof}



\section{Maximal truncation, variation, and endpoint limits}\label{mod:consequences}
The common anchored trajectories of Theorem~\ref{mod:main} yield maximal
and variational estimates, simultaneous principal values, and a separate
angular improvement for odd kernels. The weak endpoint under a phase
supremum is excluded by Proposition~\ref{mod:no-weak-phase-sup}.

\begin{corollary}[Annular maximal truncation]\label{mod:annular-maximal}
Define
\[
 \mathcal C_{\Omega,b,d}^{\mathrm{ann}}f(x)=
 \sup_{Q\in\PP_d}\sup_{0<a<R<\infty}
 \left|\int_{a<|x-y|\le R}e^{iQ(y)}b(|x-y|)K_\Omega(x-y)f(y)\,dy\right|.
\]
Then
\[
 \norm{\mathcal C_{\Omega,b,d}^{\mathrm{ann}}f}_p
 \le C_{n,d,p,\rho}\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}\|\Omega\|_{H^1}\norm f_p.
\]
The same bound holds for the maximal positive-radius tail truncation.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Each annular integral is one increment of the tail trajectory, hence is
bounded by its jump functional. The tail maximum is bounded by the same
functional using the zero-at-infinity anchor. Take phase suprema before
the spatial norm.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Variation and simultaneous endpoint limits]\label{mod:variation-limits}
For $r>2$, let
$\kappa_r=(\sum_{k\ge1}k^{-r/2})^{1/r}$. Then
\[
 \left\|\sup_{Q\in\PP_d}V^r(T_{\Omega,t}^{Q,b}f:t>0)\right\|_p
 \le C_{n,d,p,\rho}\kappa_r\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}\|\Omega\|_{H^1}\norm f_p.
\]
On one full-measure set the principal-value limit exists for every
$Q\in\PP_d$. This does not assert convergence uniform over the entire
unbounded coefficient space.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For a fixed variation partition, rearrange the increment magnitudes as
$c_k^*$. The argument of Lemma~\ref{mod:pathlemma} gives
$c_k^*\le k^{-1/2}\JJ(F)$, whence $V^r(F)\le\kappa_r\JJ(F)$.
For almost every $x$, the phase supremum in Theorem~\ref{mod:main} is
finite. All trajectories have already been constructed on that same
set. Failure of the Cauchy criterion at zero for any one trajectory
would produce arbitrarily many disjoint jumps of a fixed positive
size, contradicting finiteness of its jump functional.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Distinction between maximal, variational, and jump control]\label{mod:nonimplications}
A maximal truncation theorem alone does not imply the critical jump
estimate. Nor does a fixed $r>2$ variation theorem imply it. For example,
the finite path obtained by repeating $0,1,0$ $M$ times has maximum one
and jump functional $\sqrt{2M}$. Replacing each peak by $M^{-1/r}$ gives
$V^r=2^{1/r}$ but jump functional $\sqrt2M^{1/2-1/r}$. Thus neither maximal control nor a fixed noncritical variation estimate
implies critical-jump control for general scalar paths.
\end{remark}



\begin{corollary}[Odd angular kernels in \texorpdfstring{$L^1$}{L1}]
\label{mod:odd-L1}
Let $\Omega\in L^1(\Sph^{n-1})$ be odd, let $d\ge0$, and let
$1<p<\infty$. Fix $1\le\rho<\infty$ and $b\in\mathcal V^\rho$.
Then, for every $f\in L^p(\R^n)$,
\begin{equation}\label{mod:odd-L1-bound}
 \left\|\sup_{\deg P\le d}
 \mathbf J(T^{P,b}_{\Omega,t}f:t>0)\right\|_p
 \le C_{n,d,p,\rho}\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}\|\Omega\|_1\|f\|_p.
\end{equation}
The maximal-truncation, $r>2$ variation, and simultaneous principal-value
conclusions of this section hold with $\|\Omega\|_{H^1}$ replaced by
$\|\Omega\|_1$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
First let $f$ be bounded and compactly supported, and restrict the phase
and truncation parameters to finite sets. It is enough to treat input
phases $Q(y)$ by the pointwise identity used in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{mod:main}. For $\theta\in\Sph^{n-1}$ define
\[
 H^{Q,b}_{\theta,t}f(x)
 =\int_{|u|>t}e^{iQ(x-u\theta)}b(|u|)f(x-u\theta)\,\frac{du}{u}.
\]
Polar coordinates and the oddness of $\Omega$ give the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{mod:odd-rotation-identity}
 T^{Q,b}_{\Omega,t}f(x)
 =\frac12\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}
 \Omega(\theta)H^{Q,b}_{\theta,t}f(x)\,d\sigma(\theta).
\end{equation}
All integrals in this finite-parameter identity are absolutely convergent.
Lemma~\ref{mod:pathlemma} therefore yields
\begin{equation}\label{mod:odd-rotation-norm}
 \max_Q\NN_{D_0}(T^{Q,b}_{\Omega,t}f)
 \le\frac12\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}|\Omega(\theta)|
 \max_Q\NN_{D_0}(H^{Q,b}_{\theta,t}f)\,d\sigma(\theta).
\end{equation}
For fixed $\theta$, write $x=z+s\theta$, $z\perp\theta$, and set
$f_{z,\theta}(v)=f(z+v\theta)$. On this line,
\[
 H^{Q,b}_{\theta,t}f(z+s\theta)
 =\int_{|s-v|>t}
 e^{iQ(z+v\theta)}b(|s-v|)f_{z,\theta}(v)\,\frac{dv}{s-v}.
\]
The function $v\mapsto Q(z+v\theta)$ is a real polynomial of degree at
most $d$. The one-dimensional case of Theorem~\ref{mod:main}, applied to
the Hilbert kernel, and the equivalence
$\mathbf J\le\NN\le2\mathbf J$ give
\[
 \left\|\max_Q\NN_{D_0}(H^{Q,b}_{\theta,t}f)\right\|_{L^p(\R^n)}
 \le C_{d,p,\rho}\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}\|f\|_p.
\]
Indeed, the bound holds on almost every line with a constant independent
of $z$ and $\theta$, and integration in $z$ gives the displayed estimate.
Minkowski's inequality in \eqref{mod:odd-rotation-norm} proves
\eqref{mod:odd-L1-bound} for the finite model.

Finally, $\Omega\in L^1$ satisfies the positive-tail estimate in
Lemma~\ref{mod:atom:lem:tail}. The common representatives and approximation
argument in Sections~\ref{mod:atom:sec:tails}--\ref{mod:atom:sec:mainproof}
therefore remove the finite parameter restrictions and extend the bound
to every $f\in L^p$. The anchored path calculus gives the asserted
maximal, variational, and convergence consequences.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Parity and the angular endpoint]\label{mod:parity-endpoint}
For a general angular function with zero mean, Theorem~\ref{mod:main}
uses its angular $H^1$ norm. Corollary~\ref{mod:odd-L1} replaces that
assumption by $L^1$ in the odd subclass, with the same fixed radial
amplitude. It neither supplies a phase-supremum weak $(1,1)$ estimate
nor removes the angular assumption for a general even component.
The inherited sharpness statement in Section~\ref{intro:sharpness}
concerns the Orlicz scale, not a maximality claim for the Hardy class.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The phase supremum does not preserve the weak endpoint]
\label{mod:no-weak-phase-sup}
For the Hilbert kernel in dimension one and any $d\ge2$, there is no
uniform weak $(1,1)$ estimate for
$\sup_{\deg P\le d}\mathbf J(T^P_t f:t>0)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
We give explicit bounded compactly supported counterexamples using only
linear phases; the argument in fact applies to every $d\ge1$.
Fix an integer $m\ge1$, put $N=4m$, and define
\[
 s_j=\sum_{r=0}^{j-1}8^{-r},\qquad
 c_j=\frac{j+s_j/2}{N},\qquad
 \delta=\frac{1}{100N8^N},\qquad
 \xi_k=2\pi N8^k.
\]
For $0\le j<N$ let
\[
 I_j=[c_j-\delta,c_j+\delta],\qquad
 f_N(y)=\frac{1}{2\delta N}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\mathbf1_{I_j}(y).
\]
Each summand has integral $1/N$, so $\|f_N\|_1\le1$.
Moreover $0\le c_j\le1$ and $\delta\le1$, hence $f_N$ is bounded
and supported in $[-2,2]$. Consider the disjoint output intervals
\[
 J_k=\left[\frac{k+3/4}{N},\frac{k+5/4}{N}\right],\qquad
 m\le k<3m,\qquad E_m=\bigcup_{k=m}^{3m-1}J_k.
\]
Each has length $1/(2N)$, and therefore $|E_m|=1/4$.

The centers encode the signs needed for the Hilbert denominator. Indeed,
$0\le s_j\le8/7$, and direct summation of the geometric progression gives
\[
 \xi_kc_j\equiv
 \begin{cases}
 0,&j\le k,\\
 \pi+\dfrac{\pi}{8}s_{j-k-1},&j>k
 \end{cases}
 \pmod{2\pi}.
\]
The residual angle in the second case belongs to $[0,\pi/7]$.
For $y\in I_j$ and $k<N$,
$|\xi_k(y-c_j)|\le\xi_k\delta\le1/4$.
Using $\cos u\ge1-u^2/2$ and $\pi/7+1/4<1$, we obtain
\[
 \cos(\xi_ky)\ge\tfrac12\quad(j\le k),\qquad
 \cos(\xi_ky)\le-\tfrac12\quad(j>k).
\]
On the other hand,
\[
 j\le Nc_j\le j+\tfrac47,\qquad
 Nc_j\ge j+\tfrac12\ \ (j>0),\qquad N\delta\le\tfrac1{100}.
\]
Consequently, for $x\in J_k$ and $y\in I_j$,
\[
 \frac1{16N}<|x-y|<3,\qquad
 x-y>0\ \ (j\le k),\qquad x-y<0\ \ (j>k).
\]
Thus every packet contributes a nonnegative real part to
\[
 R_k(x):=\int_{\R}\frac{e^{i\xi_ky}}{x-y}f_N(y)\,dy.
\]
This is an absolutely convergent integral on $J_k$, since that interval
is separated from every packet support. For each $0\le r<m$ the left
packet $j=k-r$ is present, and on that packet
\[
 0<x-y\le\frac{r+2}{N},\qquad
 \frac{\cos(\xi_ky)}{x-y}\ge\frac{N}{2(r+2)}.
\]
Its real contribution is therefore at least $1/(2(r+2))$. Summing these
$m$ contributions and retaining the nonnegative contributions of all
remaining packets yields
\[
 |R_k(x)|\ge\operatorname{Re}R_k(x)\ge H_m,
 \qquad H_m:=\frac12\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}\frac1{r+2}
 \longrightarrow\infty.
\]

For the Hilbert kernel, take the original bivariate phase
$P_k(x,y)=\xi_ky$, of degree at most one, and the constant radial amplitude
$b=1$. The support distances just proved give the exact sharp-tail identities
\[
 T^{P_k}_{1/(16N)}f_N(x)=R_k(x),\qquad
 T^{P_k}_{3}f_N(x)=0\qquad(x\in J_k).
\]
For every $0<\lambda<H_m$ these two radii form a strict one-pair
$\lambda$-jump. Taking the supremum in $\lambda$ and then in the phase,
with both suprema still pointwise before the spatial norm, gives
\[
 G_N(x):=\sup_{\deg P\le d}\mathbf J(T^P_t f_N:t>0)(x)
 \ge H_m\qquad(x\in E_m).
\]
In particular, testing the weak norm at the strict level $H_m/2$ gives
\[
 \|G_N\|_{L^{1,\infty}}
 \ge\frac{H_m}{2}|E_m|=\frac{H_m}{8}\longrightarrow\infty,
 \qquad \|f_N\|_1\le1.
\]
This contradicts any uniform weak $(1,1)$ bound, already on bounded
compactly supported inputs. No principal-value limit or external
counterexample theorem is needed. Thus the fixed-phase weak theorem in
Part~\ref{part:fixed} is complementary to Theorem~\ref{mod:main}; it is
not a weak-type consequence of the phase-supremum estimate.
\end{proof}



\begin{proposition}[Failure of the spatial strong Hardy endpoint]
\label{mod:spatial-hardy-obstruction}
The angular Hardy conclusion of Theorem~\ref{mod:main} does not imply
a strong estimate from $H^1(\mathbb R)$ to $L^1(\mathbb R)$.
Such an estimate fails even for the Hilbert kernel, the constant radial
amplitude $b=1$, and one fixed linear phase.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Set
\[
 a(y)=\tfrac12\bigl(\mathbf1_{(0,1)}(y)-\mathbf1_{(-1,0)}(y)\bigr).
\]
This is a real $H^1(\mathbb R)$ atom: it is supported in $[-1,1]$,
has integral zero, and has $L^\infty$ norm $1/2$. Use the phase
$P(x,y)=\pi y$. For $x>2$ the truncation at $t=1$ is
\[
 T^{P,1}_1a(x)=\int_{-1}^1\frac{e^{i\pi y}a(y)}{x-y}\,dy
             =\frac{2i}{\pi x}+E(x),
 \qquad |E(x)|\le\frac1{x(x-1)}.
\]
Indeed $\int_{-1}^1e^{i\pi y}a(y)\,dy=2i/\pi$ and
$|(x-y)^{-1}-x^{-1}|\le |y|/[x(x-1)]$.
For $x\ge5$ this gives $|T^{P,1}_1a(x)|\ge1/(\pi x)$.
The trajectory is zero when $t>x+1$, so one-pair tests and the strict
jump definition give
$\mathbf J(T^{P,1}_t a:t>0)\ge |T^{P,1}_1a|$.
Its integral over $[5,\infty)$ is infinite. Thus even this fixed-phase
critical-jump operator has no strong spatial $H^1\to L^1$ bound.
\end{proof}

\clearpage
\part{Fixed-phase endpoints, radial amplitudes, and sharpness}\label{part:fixed}
This part develops the fixed-phase weak endpoint and radial-amplitude
theory independently of Theorem~\ref{mod:main}. The rational oscillatory
and equal-shell correlation estimates used in Part~\ref{part:modulation}
are proved here without invoking the phase-supremum conclusion. The
independent strong estimates provide the local analytic input for the
weak endpoint, the radial extension, and the completion of the
corresponding trajectories.

Section~\ref{sec:nonosc-strong} establishes the unphased strong input.
Sections~\ref{sec:flag}--\ref{sec:strong-far} develop the global phase flag,
annular decay, and the independent fixed-phase strong theorem.
Sections~\ref{sec:terminal-packets}--\ref{sec:degree-packets} prove the
terminal and phase-adapted packet estimates and the concentration-stable
correlations. The weak endpoint is obtained in
Sections~\ref{sec:unphased-weak}--\ref{sec:weak-local}.
Section~\ref{sec:limits} completes the trajectories and proves the direct
bounded-variation transfer; Section~\ref{sec:finite-radial} treats every
finite radial variation exponent and the shellwise Hilbert refinement.
Section~\ref{sec:sharpness-obstructions} contains the sharpness results.

\section{Abstract jump inequalities and the unphased strong endpoint}
\label{sec:nonosc-strong}

This section assembles the jump calculus and the one-dimensional
estimates used throughout the paper, then combines them with a
Riesz-transform factorization to prove the unphased strong critical-jump
theorem.

\begin{lemma}[Gauge normalization]\label{lem:gauge-normalization}
For every $\Omega\in L\log L(\Sph^{n-1})$, the quantity $\mathfrak L$
in \eqref{eq:gauge} is equivalent, with
dimensional constants, to the homogeneous Luxemburg norm associated with
$\Phi_0(t)=(1+t)\log(1+t)-t$.  It also satisfies
\eqref{eq:gauge-parity}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The assertion is immediate for $\Omega=0$, so assume
$\mathfrak L(\Omega)>0$.
Put $A=\|\Omega\|_1$ and $M=\mathfrak L(\Omega)$.  The elementary bound
\[
 \Phi_0(t)\le C\bigl(t+t\log^+t\bigr),\qquad t\ge0,
\]
and $M\ge A$ give, for a sufficiently large absolute $C_0$,
\begin{align*}
 \int_{\Sph^{n-1}}\Phi_0\!\left(\frac{|\Omega|}{C_0M}\right)d\sigma
 &\le \frac C{C_0M}\left(A+
   \int_{\Sph^{n-1}}|\Omega|
      \log^+\!\left(\frac{|\Omega|}{M}\right)d\sigma\right)\\
 &\le \frac C{C_0M}\left(A+
   \int_{\Sph^{n-1}}|\Omega|
      \log^+\!\left(\frac{|\Omega|}{A}\right)d\sigma\right)
 \le1.
\end{align*}
This proves one direction of the Luxemburg comparison.

Conversely, suppose that
$\int\Phi_0(|\Omega|/L)d\sigma\le1$, and put
$m_n=\sigma(\Sph^{n-1})$.  Jensen's inequality yields
\[
 \Phi_0\!\left(\frac{A}{m_nL}\right)
 \le\frac1{m_n}\int\Phi_0(|\Omega|/L)d\sigma
 \le\frac1{m_n},
\]
and hence $A\le C_nL$.  Since
\[
 \log^+\!\left(\frac{|\Omega|}{A}\right)
 \le \log\!\left(1+\frac{|\Omega|}{L}\right)
      +\log^+\!\left(\frac L A\right),
\]
while $t\log(1+t)\le\Phi_0(t)+t$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \int|\Omega|\log^+(|\Omega|/A)d\sigma
 &\le L\int\frac{|\Omega|}{L}
          \log\!\left(1+\frac{|\Omega|}{L}\right)d\sigma
       +A\log^+(L/A)\\
 &\le L\int\Phi_0(|\Omega|/L)d\sigma+A+L/e
 \le C_nL.
\end{align*}
Thus the two quantities are equivalent, with dimensional constants.

If $R\Omega(\theta)=\Omega(-\theta)$, then reflection is an isometry of
the Luxemburg space.  Convexity gives
\[
 \|\Omega_o\|_{L\log L,\mathrm{Lux}}
 +\|\Omega_e\|_{L\log L,\mathrm{Lux}}
 \le2\|\Omega\|_{L\log L,\mathrm{Lux}}.
\]
Homogeneity and the equivalence just proved give
\eqref{eq:gauge-parity}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Jump calculus and one-dimensional inputs}

For a scalar path indexed by positive radii, write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dyadic-jump-definition}
 N^{\rm dyad}_\lambda(F):=
 N_\lambda(F_{2^j}:j\in\mathbb Z),
 \qquad
 \mathbf J^{\rm dyad}(F)
 :=\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda
 N^{\rm dyad}_\lambda(F)^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
For a finite ordered parameter set $I$, $\mathbf J_I(F)$ denotes the same
pointwise functional with the path restricted to $I$.

\begin{lemma}[Pointwise jump and variation calculus]\label{lem:jumpcalc}
Let $E$ be a linearly ordered set.  For scalar $E$-paths $F,G$ and
$\lambda>0$,
\begin{align}
 N_\lambda(F+G)&\le N_{\lambda/2}(F)+N_{\lambda/2}(G),
 \label{eq:jumpadd}\\
 \lambda N_\lambda(F)^{1/2}&\le V^2(F),
 \label{eq:jumpvar}\\
 V^2(F+G)&\le V^2(F)+V^2(G).
 \label{eq:varadd}
\end{align}
Consequently,
\begin{align}
 \mathbf J(F+G)&\le2\mathbf J(F)+2\mathbf J(G),
 \label{eq:pointwise-jump-quasitriangle}\\
 \mathbf J(F)&\le V^2(F).
 \label{eq:pointwise-jump-by-V2}
\end{align}
If $F_t\to0$ at the upper endpoint of its ordered parameter interval
(with value $0$ there when that endpoint belongs to the parameter set), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:anchor-controls-maximal}
 \sup_t|F_t|\le\mathbf J(F).
\end{equation}
For every $r>2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pointwise-jump-to-variation}
 V^r(F)\le\kappa_r\mathbf J(F),
 \qquad
 \kappa_r=\left(\sum_{k=1}^\infty k^{-r/2}\right)^{1/r}.
\end{equation}
Finally, if $F=(F_t)_{t>0}$ is a positive-radius path decomposed into
dyadic parameter blocks, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:longshort}
 \lambda N_\lambda(F)^{1/2}
 \le C\left[\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}
 V^2(F_\varepsilon:2^j\le\varepsilon\le2^{j+1})^2\right)^{1/2}
 +\lambda N^{\rm dyad}_{\lambda/3}(F)^{1/2}\right],
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pointwise-longshort}
 \mathbf J(F)
 \le C\left[\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}
 V^2(F_\varepsilon:2^j\le\varepsilon\le2^{j+1})^2\right)^{1/2}
 +\mathbf J^{\rm dyad}(F)\right].
\end{equation}
Moreover $\lambda N^{\rm dyad}_\lambda(F)^{1/2}\le
V^2(F_{2^j}:j\in\mathbb Z)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For each strict $\lambda$-jump of $F+G$, one of the two corresponding
increments has modulus $>\lambda/2$.  Assign the pair to such a summand;
each assigned subcollection remains ordered and paired, proving
\eqref{eq:jumpadd}.  Inserting the endpoints of a paired collection into a
variation partition gives \eqref{eq:jumpvar}; Minkowski in the finite
$\ell^2$ sum gives \eqref{eq:varadd}.  Taking the supremum in $\lambda$ in
\eqref{eq:jumpadd} proves \eqref{eq:pointwise-jump-quasitriangle}, and
\eqref{eq:pointwise-jump-by-V2} is \eqref{eq:jumpvar} followed by the same
supremum.

For \eqref{eq:anchor-controls-maximal}, first let $t$ lie strictly below the
upper endpoint and let $s>t$ tend to that endpoint.  For every
$0<\lambda<|F_t-F_s|$ there is one strict $\lambda$-jump, so
$\mathbf J(F)\ge|F_t-F_s|$; now let $s$ tend to the endpoint.  If the
upper endpoint belongs to the parameter set, its value is zero by
hypothesis, so the assertion at that endpoint is immediate.

To prove \eqref{eq:pointwise-jump-to-variation}, fix a finite variation
partition and let $a_1^*\ge a_2^*\ge\cdots$ be the decreasing rearrangement
of the moduli of its increments.  For every $0<\lambda<a_k^*$, at least
$k$ partition increments are strict $\lambda$-jumps.  Retain any $k$ of
them and restore their original temporal order.  They are a subfamily of
the consecutive partition intervals, so their interiors are disjoint and
the right endpoint of each retained interval is no later than the left
endpoint of the next.  Hence they form an admissible paired family.  Thus
$\lambda\sqrt{k}\le\mathbf J(F)$, and letting
$\lambda\uparrow a_k^*$ gives $a_k^*\le k^{-1/2}\mathbf J(F)$.  Hence
\[
 \left(\sum_i a_i^r\right)^{1/r}
 \le\mathbf J(F)\left(\sum_{k=1}^\infty k^{-r/2}\right)^{1/r}.
\]
Taking the supremum over partitions proves the claim.  The numerical bound
in \eqref{eq:kappa-r} follows from the integral test.

For \eqref{eq:longshort}, let $j(t)$ be the integer determined by
$2^{j(t)}\le t<2^{j(t)+1}$.  Fix an ordered paired family $(s_i,t_i)$ of
strict $\lambda$-jumps, and denote its finite cardinality by $N$.  If
$j(s_i)=j(t_i)$, assign the pair to that
dyadic block.  Otherwise put
\[
 \widehat s_i=2^{j(s_i)+1},\qquad
 \widehat t_i=2^{j(t_i)}.
\]
Then $s_i\le\widehat s_i\le\widehat t_i\le t_i$, and
\[
 F_{t_i}-F_{s_i}
 =(F_{t_i}-F_{\widehat t_i})
 +(F_{\widehat t_i}-F_{\widehat s_i})
 +(F_{\widehat s_i}-F_{s_i}).
\]
One of the three terms has modulus $>\lambda/3$.  Choose exactly one such
term, using any fixed tie-breaking rule.  The selected middle terms form
an ordered paired family on the dyadic sequence.  The within-block terms
and the selected initial and terminal terms assigned to any fixed dyadic
block have pairwise disjoint interiors, because the original pairs are
ordered.  Insert all their endpoints, together with the two endpoints of
that dyadic block, into a single increasing partition and delete repeated
points.  Every selected short segment is then one increment of this
partition: two such segments may share an endpoint but cannot overlap in
their interiors.  Therefore the sum of their squared increments is bounded
by the $V^2$ square of the path on that block.  Consequently, if
$N_{\rm sh}$ and $N_{\rm long}$ denote the
numbers assigned to short and middle terms, respectively, then
\[
 N=N_{\rm sh}+N_{\rm long},
 \qquad
 \frac{\lambda^2}{9}N_{\rm sh}
 \le \sum_j V^2(F_\varepsilon:2^j\le\varepsilon\le2^{j+1})^2,
 \qquad
 N_{\rm long}\le N^{\rm dyad}_{\lambda/3}(F).
\]
It follows that
\[
 \lambda\sqrt N
 \le3\left(\sum_j
 V^2(F_\varepsilon:2^j\le\varepsilon\le2^{j+1})^2\right)^{1/2}
 +3\frac\lambda3\,N^{\rm dyad}_{\lambda/3}(F)^{1/2}.
\]
Taking the supremum over all finite strict $\lambda$-jump witnesses proves
\eqref{eq:longshort}, including the case in which the jump count is
infinite.  Taking the supremum in $\lambda$ then gives
\eqref{eq:pointwise-longshort}.  The last assertion follows from
\eqref{eq:jumpvar} on the dyadic sequence.
\end{proof}

Normability at the critical jump exponent is essential for the continuum
angular factorizations below.  On a linearly ordered set $E$, define
\[
 N^{\rm ch}_{\lambda,E}(F):=\sup\Bigl(\{0\}\cup
 \bigl\{J\in\mathbb N:\ \exists\ t_0<\cdots<t_J,\quad t_j\in E,\quad
 |F_{t_j}-F_{t_{j-1}}|>\lambda\ (1\le j\le J)\bigr\}\Bigr)
 \in\mathbb N_0\cup\{\infty\}.
\]
As before, the index-set subscript is omitted when it is displayed or
unambiguous.  Then, for every ordered $E$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chain-pair}
 N^{\rm ch}_{\lambda,E}(F)\le2N_{\lambda,E}(F),
 \qquad N_{\lambda,E}(F)\le N^{\rm ch}_{\lambda/2,E}(F).
\end{equation}
The first inequality follows by taking alternating edges of a chain.  For
the second, start from $r_0=s_1$ and $r_1=t_1$ in an admissible family of
pairs.  Inductively, after $r_{i-1}$ has been selected from the preceding
pair, at least one of $s_i,t_i$ is more than $\lambda/2$ away from
$F_{r_{i-1}}$; otherwise
\[
 |F_{t_i}-F_{s_i}|
 \le |F_{t_i}-F_{r_{i-1}}|+|F_{s_i}-F_{r_{i-1}}|
 \le\lambda.
\]
Choose such an endpoint as $r_i$.  The temporal ordering of the pairs,
together with the strict increment, gives $r_{i-1}<r_i$.  The resulting
chain has one initial $\lambda$-increment and then strict
$\lambda/2$-increments, hence has at least as many strict
$\lambda/2$-increments as the original paired family has pairs.  For a
linearly ordered set $E$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chain-jump-seminorm-definition}
 \mathbf J_E^{\rm ch}(F)
 :=\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda
 N^{\rm ch}_{\lambda,E}(F)^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
We omit $E$ when it is the full parameter set.  For a finite ordered set
$I$ and a spatially measurable $I$-path, also put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-chain-jump-spatial-norm}
 \mathcal J^{\rm ch}_{2,p}(F_t:t\in I)
 :=\norm{\mathbf J_I^{\rm ch}(F)}_p.
\end{equation}
Thus \eqref{eq:chain-pair} gives, pointwise and uniformly in every ordered
set $E$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chain-pair-functional}
 \mathbf J_E^{\rm ch}(F)\le\sqrt2\,\mathbf J_E(F),
 \qquad
 \mathbf J_E(F)\le2\mathbf J_E^{\rm ch}(F).
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Pathwise critical-jump normability and integration]
\label{lem:jumpnorm}
For every nonempty finite ordered set $I$, the scalar functional
$\mathbf J_I^{\rm ch}$ is equivalent, with absolute constants uniform in
$I$, to a subadditive seminorm on scalar $I$-paths modulo constant paths.
Consequently, if $\mu$ is a complex measure on a measure space $Z$ and
$F^z=(F_t^z)_{t\in I}$ is coordinatewise $|\mu|$-integrable, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pathwise-jump-minkowski}
 \mathbf J_I^{\rm ch}\!\left(\int_ZF^z\,d\mu(z)\right)
 \le C\int_Z\mathbf J_I^{\rm ch}(F^z)\,d|\mu|(z).
\end{equation}
If the paths also depend on $x$, assume that, for every $t\in I$, the map
$(z,x)\mapsto F_t^z(x)$ is jointly measurable and that, for almost every
$x$,
\[
 \int_Z|F_t^z(x)|\,d|\mu|(z)<\infty,
 \qquad t\in I.
\]
Then \eqref{eq:pathwise-jump-minkowski} holds for almost every $x$.  In
particular, for $1\le p<\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:jump-minkowski}
 \mathcal J^{\rm ch}_{2,p}\!\left(\int_ZF^z\,d\mu(z)\right)
 \le C\int_Z\mathcal J^{\rm ch}_{2,p}(F^z)\,d|\mu|(z),
\end{equation}
whenever the right-hand side is finite.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Use the explicit paired-path norm $\NN_I$ from Lemma~\ref{mod:pathlemma}.
Together with \eqref{eq:chain-pair-functional}, its equivalence to
$\mathbf J_I$ gives
\[
 \mathbf J_I^{\rm ch}(F)\le\sqrt2\,\NN_I(F),\qquad
 \NN_I(F)\le4\mathbf J_I^{\rm ch}(F).
\]
Apply \eqref{mod:pathintegral} and these two comparisons to obtain
\eqref{eq:pathwise-jump-minkowski}. On a finite parameter set the path
functionals are continuous functions of their coordinates, so the stated
joint measurability and sectionwise integrability hypotheses permit the
same argument almost everywhere in the spatial variable. Minkowski's
inequality in $L^p$, $1\le p<\infty$, proves \eqref{eq:jump-minkowski}.
\end{proof}

We next record the pointwise critical-jump estimates for the
one-dimensional model paths used in the factorization.  If
$u\in\Sph^{n-1}$, put
\[
 H_{\varepsilon,u}f(x)=\int_{|s|>\varepsilon}f(x-su)\,\frac{ds}{s},
 \qquad
 A^+_{t,u}f(x)=\frac1t\int_0^t f(x-su)\,ds.
\]

\begin{lemma}[One-dimensional pointwise critical jumps]
\label{lem:one-dimensional-inside-jump}
For every $1<p<\infty$, every $f\in L^p(\R^n)$, and every
$u\in\Sph^{n-1}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:one-dimensional-inside-jump}
 \norm{\mathbf J(H_{\varepsilon,u}f:\varepsilon>0)}_p
 +\norm{\mathbf J(A^+_{t,u}f:t>0)}_p
 \le C_p\norm f_p,
\end{equation}
with a constant independent of $u$. The same estimate holds with the
chain functional $\mathbf J^{\rm ch}$ in place of $\mathbf J$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We first work on $\R$ and keep every jump-amplitude supremum inside
the spatial norm.  The Hilbert term is the proved scalar unphased
specialization of Proposition~\ref{mod:unphased}, with $A=I$,
$b\equiv1$ and angular values $1,-1$ on $\Sph^0$.  Counting measure
on $\Sph^0$ gives the literal, unnormalized operator
\[
 H_\varepsilon f(x)=\int_{|s|>\varepsilon}f(x-s)\,\frac{ds}{s}.
\]
That specialization uses the independent smooth-approximation and
shell estimates, not the present averaging assertion or a jump-amplitude
supremum outside $L^p$.

Here is the sharp-average argument.  Write
$A_tf(x)=t^{-1}\int_0^tf(x-s)\,ds$, let $E_m$ be expectation on the
dyadic intervals of length $2^m$, and set
$D_mf=E_{m-1}f-E_mf$.  For $k,m\in\mathbb Z$ define
\[
 C_{k,m}f=(A_{2^{m+k}}-E_{m+k})D_mf.
\]
We first establish the uniform comparison-square estimate
\begin{equation}\label{eq:one-sided-average-square-error}
 \left\|\left(\sum_j|A_{2^j}f-E_jf|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_p
 \le C_p\|f\|_p.
\end{equation}
The dyadic almost-orthogonality estimate is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:one-sided-average-error-L2}
 \|C_{k,m}f\|_2\le C2^{-|k|/2}\|D_mf\|_2.
\end{equation}
For $k<0$, $E_{m+k}D_mf=D_mf$.  Translation of a function constant
on intervals of length $2^{m-1}$ changes it only across cell boundaries,
giving
\[
 \|D_mf(\cdot-h)-D_mf\|_2
 \le C\min\{1,(|h|/2^m)^{1/2}\}\|D_mf\|_2.
\]
Integration against the actual one-sided box kernel gives the required
$2^{k/2}$ gain; only its finite half moment is used, not smoothness.
For $k\ge0$, the expectation vanishes.  Subtract the box kernel at the
left endpoint of each length-$2^m$ cell, on which $D_mf$ has integral
zero.  The resulting boundary kernel has row and column integrals at
most $C2^m/2^{m+k}$.  Schur's inequality gives $C2^{-k}$, which is
stronger than \eqref{eq:one-sided-average-error-L2}.
All these convolutions are genuine almost-everywhere integrals.

Orthogonality of the $D_m$'s now gives, uniformly over finite scale
sets, the $L^2(\ell^2)$ bound $C2^{-|k|/2}\|f\|_2$.
For every $1<q<\infty$, the vector maximal theorem and the dyadic
square-function theorem give the crude bound
\[
 \left\|\{C_{k,m}f\}_m\right\|_{L^q(\ell^2)}\le C_q\|f\|_q.
\]
Indeed both the sharp average and the dyadic expectation are dominated
by one-dimensional positive maximal operators on the inputs $D_mf$.
Interpolate the actual finite-vector linear operators between this
bound and $L^2$.  For each target $p$ the result is
$C_p2^{-\epsilon_p|k|}\|f\|_p$, where $\epsilon_p>0$ is independent
of the finite scale set.  Increase the scale sets and then sum in $k$.
For each $j$, the corresponding finite sums of $D_mf$ telescope, and
$E_af\to f$ as $a\to-\infty$, $E_bf\to0$ as $b\to+\infty$ in
$L^p$.  Boundedness of $A_{2^j}-E_j$ identifies the coordinate limit
as $A_{2^j}f-E_jf$.  Countable-vector Minkowski proves
\eqref{eq:one-sided-average-square-error} for the original arbitrary
$L^p$ input.

The elementary paired-path inequality
$\NN(v_j:j\in\mathbb Z)\le2(\sum_j|v_j|^2)^{1/2}$ and the
all-integer dyadic martingale jump estimate therefore give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:one-sided-average-long-jumps}
 \|\NN(A_{2^j}f:j\in\mathbb Z)\|_p\le C_p\|f\|_p.
\end{equation}
This is a pointwise path-norm estimate, not a family of estimates for
fixed jump amplitudes.

\smallskip\noindent
\emph{The continuum short-variation square function.}
For $g=D_mf$ and $R=2^{m+k}$ anchor the path by
$F_{k,m}(x,u)=A_{Ru}g(x)-A_{2R}g(x)$, $1\le u\le2$.
It vanishes at $u=2$.  The preceding half-moment and boundary-cancellation
arguments, uniformly for $1\le u\le2$, give
\[
 \|F_{k,m}\|_{L^2(dx\,du)}\le C2^{-|k|/2}\|D_mf\|_2.
\]
The primitive defining $A_{Ru}$ is absolutely continuous, and its
actual almost-everywhere derivative is
\[
 \partial_uA_{Ru}g(x)=\frac{g(x-Ru)-A_{Ru}g(x)}u.
\]
Translations and the averaging contraction show that this derivative
has joint $L^2(dx\,du)$ norm at most $C\|D_mf\|_2$.
Fubini gives the scalar absolutely continuous paths and their
Bochner-integrable derivatives on one conull spatial set.
For an anchored absolutely continuous scalar path,
\[
 V^2(F)^2\le2\sup|F|\int_1^2|F'|,
 \qquad \sup|F|^2\le2\int_1^2|F F'|.
\]
H\"older, first in $u$ and then in $x$, consequently proves
\begin{equation}\label{eq:one-sided-average-short-L2}
 \|V^2(A_{2^{m+k}u}D_mf:1\le u\le2)\|_2
 \le C2^{-|k|/8}\|D_mf\|_2.
\end{equation}
Squaring and summing in $m$ uses the same dyadic orthogonality.
For the crude all-$q$ estimate, integrate the displayed derivative:
\[
 V^2(A_{Ru}g:1\le u\le2)
 \le\int_1^2|\partial_uA_{Ru}g|\,du\le C M|g|.
\]
The vector maximal and dyadic square-function bounds are uniform in
$k$.  Apply genuine sublinear interpolation to the finite-scale square
of variations, first using a countable dense set of parameters.
Subadditivity follows from the variation triangle inequality and
Minkowski in $\ell^2$; all $L^q$ inputs are in the domain by the
integrability just proved.  This yields a summable decay
$C_p2^{-\eta_p|k|}$, $\eta_p>0$, with constants independent of all
finite truncations.  Continuity of the averaging paths identifies
the dense-parameter variation with the full variation.

To reconstruct, retain finitely many scales and dense parameters,
sum the $D_m$ telescopes in $L^p$, and identify each averaged
coordinate by boundedness of its box convolution.  The summable
offset bounds give an $L^p$ majorant for the variation-square tails;
finite-partition lower semicontinuity and Fatou then remove the cutoffs.
Exhausting the countable dense parameters and using path continuity
proves the literal all-integer continuum estimate
\begin{equation}\label{eq:one-sided-average-short-square}
 \left\|\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}
 V^2(A_tf:2^j\le t\le2^{j+1})^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_p
 \le C_p\|f\|_p.
\end{equation}
The pointwise long--short inequality \eqref{eq:pointwise-longshort},
combined with \eqref{eq:one-sided-average-long-jumps}, now bounds
$\|\NN(A_tf:t>0)\|_p$ by $C_p\|f\|_p$.

\smallskip\noindent
\emph{All radii and original representatives on each direction.}
For a fixed unit vector $u$, the map
$(r,x_\perp)\mapsto x_\perp+ru$ preserves Lebesgue measure.
Almost every complete fiber has input $g\in L^p(\R)$.
On each such fiber and at every point, local integrability defines
all averages and all bounded Hilbert annuli, while
\[
 \int_{|s|>1}\frac{|g(r-s)|}{|s|}\,ds
 \le\|g\|_p\bigl\||s|^{-1}\mathbf1_{\{|s|>1\}}\bigr\|_{p'}<\infty.
\]
Both positive-parameter paths are locally absolutely continuous.
Equality of ambient input representatives almost everywhere gives,
by Fubini on complete rays, equality of these integrals for every
positive radius on one common conull set.  No uncountable intersection
of exceptional sets is taken.  Rational positive parameters detect
all strict jump witnesses, making the pointwise functionals measurable.
Integrating the real-line bounds over the fibers preserves the same
constants, independently of $n$ and $u$.

Finally $\mathbf J\le\NN$ and
\eqref{eq:chain-pair-functional} bound the paired and chain critical
jumps by the same absolute multiple of the two path norms.
Add the Hilbert and average constants and choose that constant before
$n,u,f$.  This proves the stated sum for both conventions, for every
positive radius and every original $L^p$ representative.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[Radial profiles]\label{lem:profiles}
Let $1<p<\infty$, let $f\in L^p(\R^n)$, let
$u\in\Sph^{n-1}$, and let $\varphi$ be represented
by a right-continuous function that is locally of bounded variation on
$(0,\infty)$,
$\varphi(t)\to0$ as $t\to\infty$, and
\[
 B(\varphi)=\int_{(0,\infty)}t\,d|D\varphi|(t)<\infty.
\]
For $P^{\varphi}_{\varepsilon,u}f(x)=
\int_0^\infty\varphi(s)f(x-\varepsilon su)\,ds$,
the pointwise path is represented by the compatible Stieltjes
representative constructed in the proof, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:profile-jump}
 \norm{\mathbf J(P^{\varphi}_{\varepsilon,u}f:\varepsilon>0)}_p
 \le C_pB(\varphi)\norm f_p.
\end{equation}
Suppose, more generally, that $(\varphi_u)_{u\in\Sph^{n-1}}$ is a family
satisfying the preceding hypotheses, with right-continuous representatives
chosen so that $(u,s)\mapsto\varphi_u(s)$ is jointly measurable on
$\Sph^{n-1}\times(0,\infty)$, and suppose that
$\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}B(\varphi_u)\,d\sigma(u)<\infty$.  Then, for every
$\varepsilon>0$, the map $u\mapsto P^{\varphi_u}_{\varepsilon,u}f$ is
strongly measurable in $L^p$, its Bochner integral is well defined, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:profile-integrated-jump}
 \left\|\mathbf J\left(
  \int_{\Sph^{n-1}}P^{\varphi_u}_{\varepsilon,u}f\,d\sigma(u):
  \varepsilon>0\right)\right\|_p
 \le C_p\left(\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}B(\varphi_u)d\sigma(u)\right)\norm f_p.
\end{equation}
Both conclusions remain valid with the chain functional.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We apply the pointwise finite-pair norm $\NN$ before taking the
$L^p$ norm. First take a Borel representative of $f$; the transfer back
to an arbitrary $L^p$ representative is justified below.

The right-continuous convention and the zero limit at infinity give
\[
 D\varphi((s,R])=\varphi(R)-\varphi(s),\qquad
 \varphi(s)=-D\varphi((s,\infty)),\qquad s>0.
\]
Indeed, $|D\varphi|((s,\infty))\le s^{-1}B(\varphi)$, so the tail
limit is legitimate.  Tonelli's theorem gives
$\int_0^\infty|\varphi(s)|\,ds\le B(\varphi)$.  Thus the literal
profile integral is a Bochner integral in $L^p$, and Bochner--Fubini
proves
\begin{equation}\label{eq:profile-stieltjes-representative}
 P^\varphi_{\varepsilon,u}f
 =-\int_{(0,\infty)}tA^+_{\varepsilon t,u}f\,d\varphi(t)
 \qquad\text{in }L^p.
\end{equation}
Let $\eta_\varphi$ be the polar density of $D\varphi$ with respect
to $|D\varphi|$. In logarithmic coordinates define
\[
 \mu_\varphi=(\log)_*\bigl(t\,d|D\varphi|(t)\bigr),\qquad
 h_\varphi(r)=\eta_\varphi(e^r).
\]
Then $\mu_\varphi(\mathbb R)=B(\varphi)$ and
$|h_\varphi|=1$ $\mu_\varphi$-almost everywhere.
The compatible pointwise representative is
\[
 L^u_\varepsilon(x)
 =-\int_\R h_\varphi(r)A^+_{\varepsilon e^r,u}f(x)
       \,d\mu_\varphi(r).
\]

On almost every line parallel to $u$, all positive-radius averages
are continuous.  The one-sided Hardy--Littlewood theorem and Fubini give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:directional-one-sided-maximal}
 \mathcal M_u^+f(x)=\sup_{r\in\mathbb Q_+}|A^+_{r,u}f(x)|,
 \qquad \|\mathcal M_u^+f\|_p\le C_p\|f\|_p.
\end{equation}
By continuity, this rational supremum bounds every positive-radius
average on the same conull set.  It dominates the Stieltjes integrand,
so $L^u_\varepsilon$ is defined for all $\varepsilon>0$ there, and
dominated convergence makes its entire path continuous.

For a finite ordered pair family, the sum of the absolute increments
of an integral is at most the integral of the corresponding sums.
Taking the supremum over the pair families proves the path-norm
integral inequality.  Since $\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon e^r$
preserves order, it follows pointwise that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:profile-finite-parameter-bound}
 \NN(L^u_\varepsilon:\varepsilon>0)
 \le B(\varphi)\NN(A^+_{t,u}f:t>0).
\end{equation}
The full-radius path-norm bound proved in
Lemma~\ref{lem:one-dimensional-inside-jump} now gives
$\|\NN(L^u)\|_p\le C_pB(\varphi)\|f\|_p$.  The comparison with
both critical-jump conventions proves the fixed-direction clause.
A fixed countable dense radius set detects the path norm and all
strict jump witnesses of these continuous paths, proving their
measurability as well.

For the spherical clause we use the given jointly measurable
profiles directly.
For each fixed positive $\varepsilon$, joint measurability of
$(u,s)\mapsto\varphi_u(s)$, translation of the Borel input, and
parameter integration give a jointly measurable representative of
\[
 H_\varepsilon(u,x)
   =\int_0^\infty\varphi_u(s)f(x-\varepsilon su)\,ds.
\]
The translation action is strongly continuous in $L^p$; separability
and simple-function approximation therefore give strong measurability
of $u\mapsto H_\varepsilon(u,\cdot)$ in $L^p$.  Moreover,
\[
 \|H_\varepsilon(u,\cdot)\|_p
 \le B(\varphi_u)\|f\|_p,
\]
so the angular Bochner integral exists.  Local spatial exhaustion by
finite-measure sets and Fubini identify this Bochner integral with
the literal scalar angular integral almost everywhere for every fixed
$\varepsilon$.

Let $D=\mathbb Q_+$.  On $D$ the path norm is a countable supremum of
measurable finite-pair tests.  The fixed-direction result and the
coordinatewise Stieltjes identities, intersected over $D$, give
\[
 \NN(H_q(u,x):q\in D)
 \le3B(\varphi_u)\NN(A^+_{q,u}f(x):q\in D)
\]
for almost every $x$ for each direction $u$.  The budget is measurable by rational partitions, and the average norm
by countable pair tests.  Both sides thus have jointly measurable
versions, so Fubini reverses these almost-everywhere quantifiers.  The path-norm integral inequality and weighted
Minkowski, followed by the uniform directional estimate, give
\begin{align}\label{eq:integrated-profile-finite-parameter}
 &\left\|\NN\left(\int H_q(u,\cdot)\,d\sigma(u):q\in D\right)\right\|_p
 \notag\\
 &\quad\le3\int B(\varphi_u)
     \|\NN(A^+_{q,u}f:q\in D)\|_p\,d\sigma(u)
 \le C_p\left(\int B(\varphi_u)\,d\sigma(u)\right)\|f\|_p.
\end{align}
All suprema here remain inside the spatial norm.

To recover the continuum, the one-sided maximal theorem and Minkowski give
\[
 G(x)=\int B(\varphi_u)\mathcal M_u^+f(x)\,d\sigma(u),
 \qquad
 \|G\|_p\le C_p\left(\int B(\varphi_u)\,d\sigma(u)\right)\|f\|_p.
\]
On one conull spatial set, $G$ is finite and the individual Stieltjes
paths are continuous for almost every direction.  Their absolute
values are bounded by $3B(\varphi_u)\mathcal M_u^+f(x)$.
Dominated convergence extends the angular integral on $D$ to a
continuous path $L_\varepsilon(x)$ on all positive radii.  At each
fixed radius it agrees almost everywhere with both the literal
angular integral and its Bochner $L^p$ representative.  The dense-set
finite-witness principle transfers
\eqref{eq:integrated-profile-finite-parameter} to this entire path;
comparison with $\mathbf J$ and $\mathbf J^{\rm ch}$ proves both
claimed estimates with a constant depending only on $p$.

Finally, if the given $f$ is merely an arbitrary measurable $L^p$
representative, choose a Borel version and a measurable null cover of
the set where they differ.  Translation invariance and Fubini show
that their directional averages agree on one conull set at every
radius, hence so do their compatible Stieltjes paths.  The same null-cover
argument on the angular--radial product shows that the literal
spherical profile integrals agree almost everywhere at each fixed
radius.  Thus the continuous representatives above represent the
original input, not an additional regularity assumption on it.
\end{proof}

We repeatedly use the following elementary compactness principle.

\begin{lemma}[Finite witnesses and dense parameter sets]
\label{lem:finite-witness}
Let $I$ be a finite ordered set, and suppose that
$F^{(m)}_t\to F_t$ for every $t\in I$.  Then, for every $\lambda>0$ and
$r\ge1$,
\[
 N_\lambda(F_t:t\in I)
 \le\liminf_{m\to\infty}N_\lambda(F^{(m)}_t:t\in I),
 \qquad
 N^{\rm ch}_\lambda(F_t:t\in I)
 \le\liminf_{m\to\infty}N^{\rm ch}_\lambda(F^{(m)}_t:t\in I),
\]
\[
 V^r(F_t:t\in I)
 \le\liminf_{m\to\infty}V^r(F^{(m)}_t:t\in I),
\]
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-J-lsc}
 \mathbf J_I(F)\le\liminf_{m\to\infty}\mathbf J_I(F^{(m)}).
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-chain-J-lsc}
 \mathbf J_I^{\rm ch}(F)
 \le\liminf_{m\to\infty}\mathbf J_I^{\rm ch}(F^{(m)}).
\end{equation}
If a path $F$ is continuous on compact subintervals of an interval $J$
and $D\subset J$ is dense, then its paired and chain strict jump counts,
its paired and chain pointwise critical-jump functionals, and its
$r$-variation on $D$ agree with those on $J$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Any finite family of strict $\lambda$-jumps of $F$ has a positive minimum
excess over $\lambda$; all of its witnessing increments therefore persist
for sufficiently large $m$.  This applies verbatim to both paired and
chain witnesses and proves the two jump-count inequalities.  Applying
the same argument to a fixed finite partition and then taking the
supremum proves the variation inequality.  If $a<\mathbf J_I(F)$, choose a jump amplitude
$\lambda$ and a finite strict witness with
$\lambda N_\lambda(F)^{1/2}>a$; that witness persists for all sufficiently
large $m$, proving \eqref{eq:finite-J-lsc}.  The identical persistence
argument for a finite chain witness proves
\eqref{eq:finite-chain-J-lsc}.  Finally, continuity permits the finitely
many points in any paired witness, chain witness, or variation partition
to be perturbed into $D$ while preserving their strict order and changing
every increment by an arbitrarily small amount.  This proves all dense-set
assertions.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Homogeneous tail counting]\label{lem:tailcount}
Let $\Omega\in L\log L(\Sph^{n-1})$ and put $A=\norm\Omega_1$.
For every $\delta>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tailcount}
 \sum_{s\ge0}\norm{\Omega\mathbf1_{\{|\Omega|>2^{\delta s}A\}}}_1
 \le C_\delta\mathfrak L(\Omega).
\end{equation}
If $\int\Omega=0$, put $m_n=\sigma(\Sph^{n-1})$ and
$E_s=\{|\Omega|>2^{\delta s}A\}$,
$c_s=m_n^{-1}\int_{E_s}\Omega\,d\sigma$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:meanzerosplit}
 \Omega_s^\sharp=\Omega\mathbf1_{E_s}-c_s,
 \qquad \omega_s=\Omega\mathbf1_{E_s^c}+c_s
\end{equation}
have mean zero and satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:splitbounds}
 \norm{\omega_s}_\infty\le C_n2^{\delta s}A,
 \quad \norm{\omega_s}_1\le2A,
 \quad \norm{\Omega_s^\sharp}_1\le2\norm{\Omega\mathbf1_{E_s}}_1.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $A=0$, all assertions are immediate.  Hence assume $A>0$.
Tonelli gives
\[
\sum_s\norm{\Omega\mathbf1_{E_s}}_1
 =\int|\Omega(\theta)|\#\{s:2^{\delta s}A<|\Omega(\theta)|\}\,d\sigma(\theta),
\]
and the counting function is bounded by
$C_\delta(1+\log^+(|\Omega|/A))$.  This proves \eqref{eq:tailcount}.
The identities and bounds in \eqref{eq:meanzerosplit}--\eqref{eq:splitbounds}
follow from $\int_{E_s^c}\Omega=-m_nc_s$ and
$m_n|c_s|\le\norm{\Omega\mathbf1_{E_s}}_1\le A$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Compact $L\log L$ data in $H^1$]\label{lem:LlogL-H1}
Suppose that $F$ is supported in a fixed ball and $\int F=0$.  Define
$\mathcal E(0)=0$, while for $F\ne0$ put
\[
 \mathcal E(F)=\norm F_1+
 \int |F(x)|\log\!\left(e+\frac{|F(x)|}{\norm F_1}\right)dx.
\]
If $\mathcal E(F)<\infty$, then
$\norm F_{H^1(\R^n)}\le C\mathcal E(F)$, with a constant depending only
on the dimension and the supporting ball.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $F=0$ there is nothing to prove.  Put $A=\norm F_1$.
By translation invariance we may suppose that
$\supp F\subset B(0,R)$, where $R$ is fixed.
Let $\phi\in C_c^\infty(B(0,1))$ have integral one and let
$\mathcal M_\phi F=\sup_{t>0}|F*\phi_t|$.  On a fixed enlargement of the
support, $\mathcal M_\phi F\le C MF$.  The refined weak estimate
\[
 |\{MF>2s\}|\le \frac C s\int_{\{|F|>s\}}|F|
\]
follows by decomposing
$F=F\mathbf1_{\{|F|\le s\}}+F\mathbf1_{\{|F|>s\}}$: the maximal
function of the first summand is at most $s$, and the weak $(1,1)$ maximal
theorem applies to the second.  If $B_R$ is a fixed enlargement of
$B(0,R)$, layer cake, with threshold $s_0=A$, gives
\begin{align*}
 \int_{B_R}MF
 &=\int_0^\infty|B_R\cap\{MF>s\}|\,ds\\
 &\le C A+C\int_A^\infty\frac1s
       \int_{\{|F|>s/2\}}|F(x)|\,dx\,ds\\
 &\le C A+C\int|F(x)|
       \log^+\!\left(\frac{2|F(x)|}{A}\right)dx
 \le C\mathcal E(F),
\end{align*}
where Tonelli justifies the interchange.  Outside that ball, cancellation
gives
\[
 |F*\phi_t(x)|
 \le\int|F(y)|\,|\phi_t(x-y)-\phi_t(x)|dy
 \le C_{\phi,R}|x|^{-n-1}\norm F_1.
\]
Indeed, whenever the integrand is nonzero, $t\ge c_R|x|$, and the
mean-value theorem gives
\[
 |\phi_t(x-y)-\phi_t(x)|
 \le |y|\|\nabla\phi_t\|_\infty
 \le C_\phi R t^{-n-1}
 \le C_{\phi,R}|x|^{-n-1}.
\]
The last majorant is integrable off $B_R$.  Thus
$\norm{\mathcal M_\phi F}_1\le C\mathcal E(F)$.  The radial
maximal characterization of real $H^1$ is
\cite[Theorem~11 and the following Remark~2]{FS72}; this proves the
assertion.  The Riesz-transform characterization
$R_j:H^1\to L^1$ is recorded in
\cite[Section~II.2, p.~144]{FS72}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Unphased strong critical-jump inequality]
\label{prop:nonosc-strong}
If $\int\Omega=0$ and $\Omega\in L\log L(\Sph^{n-1})$, then, for every
$1<p<\infty$ and every $f\in L^p(\R^n)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nonosc-strong}
 \Jop{p}(T^0_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:\varepsilon>0)
 \le C_{n,p}\mathfrak L(\Omega)\norm f_p.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Fix $1<p<\infty$.  We first take $f\in\mathcal S(\R^n)$, restrict the
truncation parameter to a finite ordered set $I\subset(0,\infty)$, and
use the chain convention.  All constants below are independent of $I$.

We record the measurability convention for the angular integrations.  For
$M>\varepsilon$, put
\[
 H^M_{\varepsilon,u}f
 =\int_{\varepsilon<|s|<M}f(\,\cdot-su)\,\frac{ds}{s}.
\]
Strong continuity of translations makes
$u\mapsto H^M_{\varepsilon,u}f$ continuous as an $L^p$-valued map.  The
one-dimensional fixed-truncation theorem, followed by slicing along lines
parallel to $u$, gives
\[
 \sup_{u\in\Sph^{n-1}}\norm{H_{\varepsilon,u}f}_p
 \le C_p\norm f_p,
\]
and $H^M_{\varepsilon,u}f\to H_{\varepsilon,u}f$ in $L^p$ for every
$u$.  Hence $u\mapsto H_{\varepsilon,u}f$ is strongly measurable and
Bochner integrable against every $L^1$ angular density.  Likewise, for
every $a\in L^1(0,\infty)$, the map
\[
 u\longmapsto\int_0^\infty
 a(s)f(\,\cdot-\varepsilon su)\,ds
\]
is strongly continuous in $L^p$, by dominated convergence.  Thus every
application of \eqref{eq:jump-minkowski} below satisfies its strong
measurability and Bochner-integrability hypotheses.

By density, both conclusions extend to every $h\in L^p(\R^n)$.  Indeed,
the fixed-truncation estimate for $H_{\varepsilon,u}$ is uniform in $u$,
while
\[
 \left\|\int_0^\infty
 a(s)h(\,\cdot-\varepsilon su)\,ds\right\|_p
 \le\norm a_1\norm h_p
\]
uniformly in $u$.  Approximating $h$ in $L^p$ by Schwartz functions
therefore makes each corresponding $L^p$-valued map a uniform $L^p$
limit, in $u$, of strongly measurable maps.  In particular these
conclusions apply to the functions $g_j=-R_jf$ introduced below.

Decompose $\Omega=\Omega_o+\Omega_e$ into its odd and even parts.  The
gauge is stable under this decomposition: this is
\eqref{eq:gauge-parity}.

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{The odd component.}

For odd $\Omega$, polar coordinates give the exact rotation identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:odd-rotation}
 T^0_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f
 =\frac12\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}\Omega(u)H_{\varepsilon,u}f\,d\sigma(u).
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{lem:jumpnorm} and Lemma~\ref{lem:one-dimensional-inside-jump} yield
\begin{equation}\label{eq:odd-jump}
 \mathcal J^{\rm ch}_{2,p}
 (T^0_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:\varepsilon\in I)
 \le C_p\norm\Omega_1\norm f_p.
\end{equation}

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{The even component and the Riesz-transform factorization.}

Assume now that $\Omega$ is even; when $n=1$ its mean-zero condition makes
it identically zero.  Let $K$ be the homogeneous principal-value
distribution
\[
 K(x)=\Omega(x/|x|)|x|^{-n}.
\]
Its action on a Schwartz function is obtained by integrating over
$\{\delta<|x|<R\}$ and passing to $\delta\downarrow0$,
$R\uparrow\infty$.  More explicitly, angular cancellation gives the
absolutely convergent formula
\[
 \langle K,\phi\rangle
 =\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}\Omega(u)\int_0^\infty
 \bigl[\phi(ru)-\phi(0)\mathbf1_{(0,1)}(r)\bigr]
 \frac{dr}{r}\,d\sigma(u),
 \qquad \phi\in\mathcal S(\R^n).
\]
Near zero the bracket is $O(r)$, while at infinity Schwartz decay applies;
thus this formula also proves that the principal-value limit exists and is
tempered.

Choose a smooth radial function $\Phi$ with $\Phi=0$ on $[0,1/4]$ and
$\Phi=1$ on $[3/4,\infty)$, and put $\psi=1-\Phi$.  We now fix the
distributional normalization of every Riesz transform used below.  Write
\[
 \mathfrak r_j(\xi)=-i\frac{\xi_j}{|\xi|},\qquad \xi\ne0,
\]
so that $\sum_j\mathfrak r_j(\xi)^2=-1$.

The following rough-multiplier estimate fixes all low-frequency choices:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rough-multiplier-normalization}
 \norm{\widehat K}_{L^\infty(\R^n\setminus\{0\})}
 +\norm{\widehat{K\psi}}_{L^\infty(\R^n)}
 \le C_n\mathfrak L(\Omega).
\end{equation}
For completeness, if $e=\xi/|\xi|$, the polar-coordinate formula for the
even kernel gives, up to a harmless Fourier-normalization constant,
\[
 \widehat K(\xi)
 =c_n\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}\Omega(u)\log|e\cdot u|\,d\sigma(u).
\]
The term containing $\log|\xi|$ is annihilated by $\int\Omega=0$, and
the sine term vanishes because $\Omega$ is even.  Also
\[
 \widehat{K\psi}(\xi)
 =\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}\Omega(u)a_\psi(\xi\cdot u)\,d\sigma(u),
 \qquad
 a_\psi(t)=\int_0^\infty\psi(r)(\cos(rt)-1)\,\frac{dr}{r}.
\]
One has $|a_\psi(t)+\log^+|t||\le C_\psi$ and, uniformly for $R>0$
and $|\tau|\le1$,
\[
 |a_\psi(R\tau)+\log^+R|
 \le C_\psi\bigl(1+|\log|\tau||\bigr).
\]
The functions $u\mapsto1+|\log|e\cdot u||$ have uniformly bounded
exponential-Orlicz norm.  The $L\log L$--exponential-Orlicz H\"older
inequality first gives
\[
 \left|\int\Omega(u)\log|e\cdot u|\,d\sigma(u)\right|
 \le C_n\mathfrak L(\Omega).
\]
Writing $\xi=Re$ and using $\int\Omega=0$ also gives the exact
cancellation
\[
 \widehat{K\psi}(Re)
 =\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}\Omega(u)
