 =\{x:\mathbf J_I(T^{P,b}_{\Omega,\varepsilon}g)>\alpha\}
\]
has measure at most $C_{n,d,\rho}\alpha^{-1}\|f\|_1$.  Use the
mean-zero angular split \eqref{eq:meanzerosplit} with the value
$\delta_a$ in \eqref{eq:allq-polynomial-angular-choice}. The angular
tails have the same summable $V^1L^1$ bound as in the unweighted proof.

For the bounded angular part, Proposition~\ref{prop:allq-direct-packet}
gives the short square function with squared $L^2$ decay
$2^{-[c_{P,\rho}-2\delta_a]s}$ and the packet error with $L^1$ decay
$2^{-c_{E,\rho}s}$. Split the dyadic shells by
\[
 1\le R^m\le2^{\kappa_0s}
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad
 R^m>2^{\kappa_0s}.
\]
There are $O(1+s)$ low shells. Cauchy--Schwarz and the endpoint
differences supplied by Proposition~\ref{prop:allq-direct-packet} give a
low-shell squared $L^2$ bound
\[
 C(1+s)2^{-[c_{P,\rho}-2\delta_a]s}\alpha\Sigma,
\]
plus a summable $V^1L^1$ error.

On the high shells use the same output localization, fixed adjacent-grid
classes, exceptional sets, and rank suffixes as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:farweak}. The normalized sharp-annulus profiles are
products of $b(R\,\cdot)$ with fixed step cutoffs and hence have uniformly
bounded $L^\infty+V^\rho$ norm. Corollary~\ref{cor:allq-high-shell-stability}, followed by Lemma~\ref{lem:RM},
gives
\[
 Cs^2 2^{-[c_{H,\rho}-2\delta_a]s}\alpha\Sigma
\]
for the high dyadic tails. By
\eqref{eq:allq-polynomial-angular-choice}, every displayed exponent is
strictly positive and all polynomial factors in $s$ are summable.

Here is the precise comparison with the majorants in
Proposition~\ref{prop:farweak}.  Write
$G^b_{j,s}+E^b_{j,s}$ for the weighted one-shell decomposition supplied
by Proposition~\ref{prop:allq-direct-packet}, and
$F^{G,b}_{j,s}+F^{E,b}_{j,s}$ for its endpoint difference.  Define
\[
 \mathcal S_s^b=\left(\sum_jV^2(G^b_{j,s})^2\right)^{1/2},\qquad
 \mathcal L_s^b=2\sum_{j\ {\rm low}}|F^{G,b}_{j,s}|,
\]
\[
 \mathcal H_s^b=\mathbf1_{X_s^c}
 V^2(\text{weighted high dyadic tails}),\qquad
 \mathcal E_s^{*,b}=\sum_jV^1(E^b_{j,s}).
\]
Let $\mathcal W$ be the total $V^1$ majorant of the weighted angular-tail
paths plus $C\sum_s\mathcal E_s^{*,b}$, including the low-shell endpoint
errors, and set
\[
 \mathcal G=\sum_s(\mathcal S_s^b+\mathcal L_s^b+\mathcal H_s^b),
 \qquad X=\bigcup_sX_s.
\]
These are exactly the four objects in
\eqref{eq:far-shell-error-majorant}--\eqref{eq:far-global-GW}, with the
corresponding weighted direct-packet and collective-high estimates; multiplication
by $|b|\le1$ leaves the angular-tail comparison unchanged.  Hence the
resulting nonnegative majorants satisfy
\[
 \|\mathcal G\|_2^2\le C_{n,d,\rho}\alpha\Sigma,\qquad
 \|\mathcal W\|_1\le C_{n,d,\rho}\Sigma,\qquad
 |X|\le C\alpha^{-1}\|f\|_1,
\]
and, off the fixed complement,
\[
 \mathbf J(\mathcal F^{P,b}b_{\rm CZ})
 \le C_{n,d,\rho}(\mathcal G+\mathcal W).
\]
The signs are fixed packetwise before the output point is evaluated, and
none of these objects depends on the jump threshold.

For the local path apply
Proposition~\ref{prop:degree-local-reduction}. Its error kernel is simply
multiplied by $|b(|x-y|)|\le1$, while the residual phase has nonpure
degree at most $m-1$ and carries the same radial function. On each unit
output cube the induction hypothesis applies to the translated,
purely modulated input; the fixed input enlargements have bounded
overlap. The piecewise error satisfies
\[
 \|V^1(E)\|_1
 \le C_{n,d}\|\Omega\|_1\|f\|_1.
\]
Combining the far path, local residual, and error with the pointwise jump
quasi-triangle inequality now gives a literal threshold argument.  Namely,
choose a fixed $A_{n,d,\rho}$ larger than all quasi-triangle constants.
Outside $E_{\rm good}^P$, the enlarged-CZ set, and $X$, a strict
$A_{n,d,\rho}\alpha$ witness for the full path forces either
$\mathcal G>c_{n,d,\rho}\alpha$,
$\mathcal W>c_{n,d,\rho}\alpha$, a strict
$c_{n,d,\rho}\alpha$ witness for the lower-degree residual, or
$V^1(E)>c_{n,d,\rho}\alpha$.  Chebyshev, the two displayed majorant
bounds, the induction hypothesis, and the $V^1(E)$ estimate therefore give
\[
 |\{\mathbf J_I(T^{P,b}_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:
                    \varepsilon\in I)>
       A_{n,d,\rho}\alpha\}|
 \le C_{n,d,\rho}\alpha^{-1}\|f\|_1.
\]
Putting $\alpha=\tau/A_{n,d,\rho}$ proves the required distribution
estimate for every strict threshold $\tau>0$, uniformly at the
finite-cutoff stage and for the normalized top block.

All estimates above were first proved with finite cube, shell, and
rational-parameter cutoffs.  To remove them, fix $\tau>0$ and a finite
rational parameter set $I$.  Finite-annulus coordinate convergence gives,
after a subsequence, $F_N\to F$ pointwise on $I$.  A strict witness for
$\mathbf J_I(F)>\tau$ has positive excess and survives for all sufficiently
large $N$, so
\[
 \mathbf1_{\{\mathbf J_I(F)>\tau\}}
 \le\liminf_N\mathbf1_{\{\mathbf J_I(F_N)>\tau\}}.
\]
Fatou preserves the finite-parameter distribution estimate without
changing its threshold.  Increasing finite sets exhaust
$\mathbb Q_+$, and local absolute continuity recovers all positive
parameters, exactly as in the final part of
Proposition~\ref{prop:farweak}. Finally the input $L^1$ norm and weak output quasi-norm acquire
the same factor under the dilation $r_0$, so the constant is independent
of $C_m$. This closes the finite degree induction under the normalization
$\mathfrak L(\Omega)=1$; angular homogeneity restores the factor
$\mathfrak L(\Omega)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Quantitative closure of the finite-radial proof]
\label{rem:allq-parameter-ledger}
For clarity, the order of every small-parameter choice in this section is
as follows.  Once $n,d,\rho$ are fixed, the polynomial sublevel constants
are structural.  Equation~\eqref{eq:allq-vdc-explicit-exponent} first fixes
$c_{\mathrm{vdc},\rho}>0$, then
\eqref{eq:allq-rational-operator-exponent} fixes
$c_{\mathrm{rat},\rho}>0$.  These determine
$c_{\mathrm{eq},\rho}$ and $c_{\mathrm{uneq},\rho}$ in
\eqref{eq:allq-equal-final-exponent} and
\eqref{eq:allq-unequal-final-exponent}, hence
$c_{\mathrm{flat},\rho}$ and $c_{\kappa,\rho}$ in
\eqref{eq:allq-flat-explicit-choice} and
\eqref{eq:allq-collective-explicit-choice}.  Independently,
\eqref{eq:allq-terminal-explicit-decay} fixes
$\delta_{T,\rho}>0$.  Only after these exponents are known are
$B_{*,\rho}$ and $\kappa_{*,\rho}$ selected in
\eqref{eq:allq-packet-ratio}--\eqref{eq:allq-packet-terminal-budget};
the three inequalities
\eqref{eq:allq-packet-hierarchy-a}--\eqref{eq:allq-packet-hierarchy-c}
then fix $c_{P,\rho},c_{E,\rho}>0$.  Finally the angular splitting
parameters are chosen in \eqref{eq:allq-weak-parameters} and
\eqref{eq:allq-polynomial-angular-choice}.  Thus no decay exponent is
defined using a parameter that is chosen later, and every geometric
series in the strong and weak arguments has a strictly positive exponent
for each fixed finite $\rho$.  The resulting constants may deteriorate
as $\rho\to\infty$, as they must in view of
Proposition~\ref{prop:radial-infinity-counterexample}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Completion of the proof}

We give the completion because it also explains why point jumps of $b$
do not create jumps in the truncation trajectory.  Assume first
\eqref{eq:allq-normalize-b}.  For bounded compactly supported $f$ set
\[
 \mathcal A_x(r)
 :=\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}e^{iP(x,x-r\theta)}
       \Omega(\theta)f(x-r\theta)\,d\sigma(\theta).
\]
For every $0<a<c<\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:allq-completion-annulus-kernel}
 \|b(|\cdot|)K_\Omega(\cdot)
       \mathbf1_{\{a<|\cdot|\le c\}}\|_1
 \le \|b\|_\infty\|\Omega\|_1\log(c/a).
\end{equation}
Fubini, first on the countable annuli
$\{N^{-1}<|z|<N\}$, gives one full-measure set on which
\begin{equation}\label{eq:allq-weighted-radial-density}
 \int_a^c|b(r)\mathcal A_x(r)|\,\frac{dr}{r}<\infty,
 \qquad
 T^{P,b}_{\Omega,a}f(x)-T^{P,b}_{\Omega,c}f(x)
 =\int_a^c b(r)\mathcal A_x(r)\,\frac{dr}{r}
\end{equation}
for every $0<a<c<\infty$.  Thus the core path is locally absolutely
continuous.  In particular, a jump of the regulated function $b$ at one
radius changes neither the Lebesgue integral nor the truncation path: no
derivative of $b$ occurs in \eqref{eq:allq-weighted-radial-density}.
For core data the path is also zero for all sufficiently large
truncations at each fixed $x$.

We next construct all coordinates for general data.  In the strong case
choose bounded compactly supported $f_m\to f$ in $L^p$ so that
\[
 \sum_m\|f_{m+1}-f_m\|_p<\infty.
\]
For $g_m=f_{m+1}-f_m$, the zero anchor for core data and
\eqref{eq:anchor-controls-maximal}, followed by
\eqref{eq:allq-strong-core}, give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:allq-strong-uniform-completion}
 \left\|\sup_{q\in\mathbb Q_+}
       |T^{P,b}_{\Omega,q}g_m|\right\|_p
 \le \|\mathbf J(T^{P,b}_{\Omega,\varepsilon}g_m)\|_p
 \le C_{n,d,p,\rho}\mathfrak L(\Omega)\|g_m\|_p.
\end{equation}
Hence the sum of the rational suprema is finite almost everywhere, and
$T^{P,b}_{\Omega,q}f_m$ is uniformly Cauchy in
$q\in\mathbb Q_+$ outside one null set.

In the weak case choose instead bounded compactly supported $f_m\to f$
in $L^1$ with $\|g_m\|_1\le2^{-3m}$.  The core weak estimate and the
same anchor imply
\begin{equation}\label{eq:allq-weak-borel-cantelli}
 \sum_m\left|\left\{x:
   \sup_{q\in\mathbb Q_+}|T^{P,b}_{\Omega,q}g_m(x)|>2^{-m}
 \right\}\right|
 \le C_{n,d,\rho}\mathfrak L(\Omega)
       \sum_m2^m\|g_m\|_1<\infty.
\end{equation}
Borel--Cantelli again gives almost-everywhere uniform Cauchy convergence
on $\mathbb Q_+$.  In either case we define every rational coordinate by
this uniform limit.  Moreover, if
\[
 \mathcal R_m(q,x)
 :=\sum_{k\ge m}T^{P,b}_{\Omega,q}g_k(x),
\]
then $\sup_{q\in\mathbb Q_+}|\mathcal R_m(q,x)|\to0$ almost everywhere.
For fixed $m$ the core path of $f_m$ vanishes for all sufficiently large
$q$; consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:allq-uniform-rational-anchor}
 \lim_{R\to\infty}
 \sup_{\substack{q\in\mathbb Q_+\\q\ge R}}
 |T^{P,b}_{\Omega,q}f(x)|=0
\end{equation}
almost everywhere in both completions.

It remains to identify these coordinates with the desired finite-shell
increments.  By \eqref{eq:allq-completion-annulus-kernel}, for every
rational $0<a<c<\infty$ the core finite-annulus expressions converge to
the corresponding expression for $f$ in $L^p$ in the strong case and in
$L^1$ in the weak case.  A diagonal subsequence over the rational pairs
therefore gives, without changing the preceding uniform rational limit,
the identity
\[
 T^{P,b}_{\Omega,a}f(x)-T^{P,b}_{\Omega,c}f(x)
 =\int_a^c b(r)\mathcal A_x(r)\,\frac{dr}{r}
\]
on one full-measure set.  Fubini on the same countable annuli shows that
the right-hand density is locally integrable for general $f$ as well
(Young's inequality applies in the strong case).  Define the real
coordinates from the base point $1$ by these finite-annulus integrals.
The resulting path is locally absolutely continuous, agrees with the
rational coordinates, and \eqref{eq:allq-uniform-rational-anchor}, by
continuity and density of $\mathbb Q_+$, yields its zero-at-infinity
anchor for all real radii.

For every finite rational parameter set, coordinatewise convergence and
\eqref{eq:finite-J-lsc} transfer the strong estimate by Fatou.  For the
weak estimate, fix its threshold $\tau>0$ before taking the limit: if the
limiting finite path has $\mathbf J>\tau$, one of its finite strict
witnesses has positive excess and therefore persists along the
coordinatewise convergent subsequence.  The corresponding level-set
indicator is bounded by the liminf of the approximating indicators, so
Fatou transfers the distribution estimate at the same $\tau$.
Increasing through finite sets exhausting $\mathbb Q_+$ and then using
local continuity and Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-witness} recovers the full
positive-parameter functional.  This also settles measurability: all
coordinates are measurable, and the completed jump functional is a
countable supremum over finite rational witnesses.  Two locally
continuous paths with the same finite-annulus increments differ by a
constant in the parameter, and the zero anchor makes the completion
unique.

We have proved \eqref{eq:finite-radial-strong} and
\eqref{eq:finite-radial-weak} under \eqref{eq:allq-normalize-b}.  If
$\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}=0$, then $b=0$ almost everywhere and the theorem
is immediate.  Otherwise apply the normalized result to
$b/\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}$; linearity of every coordinate and
homogeneity of $\mathbf J$ restore exactly one factor
$\|b\|_{\mathcal V^\rho}$.  Finally the pointwise weak-$2$
jump-to-$r$-variation inequality from Lemma~\ref{lem:jumpcalc} gives every
$r>2$ consequence, while the zero anchor gives the maximal assertion.
This completes the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-radial}.


\begin{remark}[Dependence on the radial exponent]
\label{rem:finite-radial-dependence}
The proof uses the positive exponents $1/\rho$ in both the translation
modulus and the first-derivative oscillatory estimate.  These exponents tend
to zero as $\rho\to\infty$, and no constant uniform in $\rho$ is asserted.
Proposition~\ref{prop:radial-infinity-counterexample} shows that a merely
bounded radial endpoint is false even for the one-dimensional Hilbert
kernel.
\end{remark}


\subsection{A shellwise quadratic radial-variation refinement for the Hilbert transform}

The abstract transfer in Proposition~\ref{prop:young-radial-transfer} stops
at $\rho=2$.  In the one-dimensional Hilbert model, Fourier orthogonality
allows a sharper shellwise statement.

For bounded $b:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb C$, put
\[
 H^b_\varepsilon f(x)=\int_{|y|>\varepsilon}b(|y|)f(x-y)\,\frac{dy}{y},
\]
where, under the finiteness condition below, the integral is absolutely
convergent at every $x$ for every $L^2$ input and agrees with the
compatible strong-$L^2$ limit of finite upper cutoffs, as proved below,
and define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hilbert-shell-r2}
 q_j=V^2(b;(2^j,2^{j+1})),\qquad
 R_2(b)=\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}q_j^2\right)^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
Concatenating disjoint shell partitions gives $R_2(b)\le V^2(b)$ whenever
the latter is finite.

\begin{proposition}[Shellwise radial $V^2$ Hilbert estimate]
\label{prop:hilbert-radial-v2}
For every bounded $b$ with $R_2(b)<\infty$ and every $f\in L^2(\R)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hilbert-radial-v2}
 \|\mathbf J(H^b_\varepsilon f:\varepsilon>0)\|_2
 \le C\bigl(\|b\|_\infty+R_2(b)\bigr)\|f\|_2.
\end{equation}
In particular, the right-hand side may be replaced by
$C(\|b\|_\infty+V^2(b))\|f\|_2$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $B=\|b\|_\infty$ for the essential supremum and $R=R_2(b)$.
We use the paired-path norm $\NN$ from Lemma~\ref{mod:pathlemma}; in
particular, $\mathbf J\le\NN$, and $\NN$ is subadditive with constant
one. All path norms below are taken pointwise before the spatial norm.

\emph{An open-shell decomposition.}
Put $I_j=(2^j,2^{j+1})$, $r_j=3\cdot2^j/2$, and $c_j=b(r_j)$.
For any $s,t\in I_j$, $|b(s)-b(t)|\le q_j$. Since $I_j$ has positive
measure, it contains a point $t$ with $|b(t)|\le B$; hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hilbert-shell-decomposition}
 |c_j|\le B+q_j\le B+R.
\end{equation}
This pays for possible isolated spikes at the chosen midpoint using the
original open-shell variation, rather than assuming a pointwise bound
by the essential supremum. Define, on the whole real radial line,
\[
 e_j(r)=\mathbf1_{I_j}(r)\bigl(b(r)-c_j\bigr),\qquad
 e(r)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}e_j(r),\qquad
 a(r)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}c_j\mathbf1_{(2^j,2^{j+1}]}(r),
\]
where each summand is zero outside its indicated positive shell.
Each sum has at most one nonzero term at every radius. In particular,
$e$ is zero at every dyadic radius and on $(-\infty,0]$, while
$b=a+e$ at every positive nondyadic radius. No endpoint limits of $b$
are used.

We record explicitly the global variation bound for the residual.
Zero extension of the anchored open-interval error gives
\[
 V^2(e_j;\R)\le40q_j.
\]
Indeed, on any finite variation partition having interior shell points,
let $l,r$ be the first and last such points and clamp the radial variable
to $[l,r]\subset I_j$. The clamped path $b-c_j$ has supremum at most
$q_j$ and variation at most $q_j$. Multiplication by
$\mathbf1_{[l,r]}$ reproduces $e_j$ at every point of the partition.
The product-variation inequality and
$V^2(\mathbf1_{[l,r]};\R)\le39$ give the displayed bound. A partition
with no interior shell point contributes zero.
For disjoint-support paths the quadratic-variation estimate is
\[
 V^2\!\left(\sum_j e_j;\R\right)^2
 \le2\sum_j V^2(e_j;\R)^2.
\]
To see that this also holds for the countable sum, fix a finite
partition: only finitely many supports meet its nonzero values, and
the finite-family estimate applies. That estimate follows by splitting
each increment into its supported components and using
$|z-w|^2\le2|z|^2+2|w|^2$ when its two endpoints lie in different
supports. Taking the supremum over partitions therefore gives, with
$K=\sqrt{3200}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hilbert-residual-budget}
 V^2(e;\R)\le KR,\qquad
 \sup_r|e(r)|\le KR,\qquad
 \sup_{r>0}|e(r)|+V^2(e;(0,\infty))\le2KR.
\end{equation}
The second bound follows by comparing each value with $e(0)=0$.
Finite variation makes $e$ measurable; $a$ is a measurable step
function, bounded pointwise by $B+R$.

\emph{Arbitrary bounded dyadic step coefficients.}
We next prove, independently of any variation of the coefficient
sequence, that for
\[
 a_d(r)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}d_j\mathbf1_{(2^j,2^{j+1}]}(r),
 \qquad |d_j|\le M,
\]
one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hilbert-step-continuum}
 \|\NN(H^{a_d}_\varepsilon f:\varepsilon>0)\|_2
 \le C M\|f\|_2.
\end{equation}
First take $M=1$ and a finite shell set $S\subset\mathbb Z$. Write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hilbert-shell-kernel}
 A_{j,u}f(x)=\int_{2^ju<|y|\le2^{j+1}}f(x-y)\,\frac{dy}{y},
 \quad 1\le u\le2,\qquad
 \nu_j(y)=\frac{\mathbf1_{\{2^j<|y|\le2^{j+1}\}}}{y}.
\end{equation}
Thus $A_{j,1}f=\nu_j*f$ and $A_{j,2}f=0$. Choose the smooth dyadic
frequency pieces $P_k$ at frequency $|\xi|\simeq2^{-k}$, with their
exact reproducing dual pieces, and set
\[
 U_j^{(m)}=d_j A_{j,1}P_{j-m}f,\qquad
 S_mf=\left(\sum_j
 V^2(A_{j,u}P_{j-m}f:1\le u\le2)^2\right)^{1/2}.
\]
The unweighted localized short-square estimates already proved in
Lemma~\ref{lem:allq-terminal-short}, specifically
\eqref{eq:allq-short-L2} and the summation following it, give
\[
 \sum_{m\in\mathbb Z}\|S_mf\|_2\le C\|f\|_2.
\]
Here we use only the constant radial amplitude $1$ and the Hilbert
angular kernel. Scalar multiplication by $d_j$ preserves exact
frequency reproduction. The frequency-ordered tail estimate
\eqref{mod:tree:eq:ordered-tail} therefore applies for each offset $m$:
\[
 \left\|\NN\left(\sum_{\substack{j\in S\\j\ge J}}
 U_j^{(m)}:J\in\mathbb Z\right)\right\|_2
 \le C\left\|\left(\sum_{j\in S}|U_j^{(m)}|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_2
 \le C\|S_mf\|_2.
\]
The last pointwise square domination uses $|d_j|\le1$ and the zero
anchor $A_{j,2}=0$. The actual shell reconstruction
$A_{j,1}f=\sum_m A_{j,1}P_{j-m}f$ holds simultaneously for all integer
$j$ on a common full-measure set: the summed short squares give
absolute coordinate convergence there, and the Littlewood--Paley
identity identifies the limit in $L^2$. Applying the triangle
inequality for entire trajectories and then Minkowski gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hilbert-dyadic-jump}
 \left\|\NN\left(\sum_{\substack{j\in S\\j\ge J}}
 d_j\nu_j*f:J\in\mathbb Z\right)\right\|_2
 \le C\|f\|_2.
\end{equation}
There is no factor depending on $|S|$ or the number of frequency
offsets: finite sums use the constant-one triangle inequality for
$\NN$, and coordinate lower semicontinuity removes the offset cutoff.

For the short part, the same unweighted theorem gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hilbert-short-bound}
 \left\|\left(\sum_{j\in S}
 V^2(d_j A_{j,u}f:1\le u\le2)^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_2
 \le\left\|\left(\sum_j
 V^2(A_{j,u}f:1\le u\le2)^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_2
 \le C\|f\|_2.
\end{equation}
Concatenate the partial shells, using zero at the outer endpoint of
each shell. The pointwise long--short inequality
\eqref{eq:pointwise-longshort}, together with
\eqref{mod:pathequiv}, bounds the full finite-shell path
\[
 F_S(\varepsilon,x)=\sum_{j\in S}d_j
 \int_{\max\{\varepsilon,2^j\}<|y|\le2^{j+1}}
 f(x-y)\,\frac{dy}{y}
\]
by a fixed multiple of the short square in
\eqref{eq:hilbert-short-bound} plus the dyadic path norm in
\eqref{eq:hilbert-dyadic-jump}. This is a measurable majorant with
$L^2$ norm at most $C\|f\|_2$, uniformly in $S$.

For every original $L^2$ representative, every $x\in\R$, and every
$\varepsilon>0$, Cauchy--Schwarz gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hilbert-absolute-tail}
 \int_{|y|>\varepsilon}\frac{|f(x-y)|}{|y|}\,dy
 \le\left(\frac2\varepsilon\right)^{1/2}\|f\|_2<\infty.
\end{equation}
Consequently the finite-shell paths with $S=[-N,N]\cap\mathbb Z$
converge to the literal integral $H^{a_d}_\varepsilon f(x)$ at
\emph{every} $x$ and \emph{every} positive radius. For each finite
paired witness, its value converges; hence the limiting full path norm
is bounded by the liminf of the finite-shell majorants. Fatou proves
\eqref{eq:hilbert-step-continuum} for $M=1$. Scaling gives the result
for $M>0$, and $M=0$ is immediate. In particular, arbitrary jumps
between the coefficients are allowed; no global-variation theorem
has been applied to $a_d$.

\emph{Identification with the sharp truncations.}
Apply \eqref{eq:hilbert-step-continuum} to $d_j=c_j$ with $M=B+R$.
For the residual, Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-radial}, in its
one-dimensional zero-phase Hilbert specialization at $\rho=p=2$,
and \eqref{mod:pathequiv} give
\[
 \|\NN(H^e_\varepsilon f:\varepsilon>0)\|_2
 \le C\bigl(\sup_{r>0}|e(r)|+V^2(e;(0,\infty))\bigr)\|f\|_2
 \le C R\|f\|_2.
\]
The specialization agrees with the literal integral: by
\eqref{eq:hilbert-absolute-tail} its tails are absolutely convergent,
its finite-annulus increments are the stated integrals, and its
zero-at-infinity anchor identifies it with the completed path.

The set $\{y:|y|=2^j\text{ for some }j\in\mathbb Z\}$ is countable
and null. Since $b=a+e$ away from these radii and $a,e$ are measurable
and bounded, the original integrand is absolutely integrable and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hilbert-actual-decomposition}
 H^b_\varepsilon f(x)=H^a_\varepsilon f(x)+H^e_\varepsilon f(x)
 \qquad(x\in\R,\ \varepsilon>0).
\end{equation}
This is an identity of actual integrals, not only of selected $L^2$
representatives. For each of $a,e$, the integral is measurable in $x$
and continuous in the positive cutoff at every $x$, by dominated
convergence using the absolute tail at half the cutoff. Thus its
full path norm equals its countable-dense-radius version and is
measurable. The same facts hold for $b$ by
\eqref{eq:hilbert-actual-decomposition}; all positive radii and the
jump-amplitude supremum remain inside the spatial norm.
Finally, subadditivity of $\NN$, Minkowski, and $\mathbf J\le\NN$
yield
\[
 \|\mathbf J(H^b_\varepsilon f:\varepsilon>0)\|_2
 \le\|\NN(H^a_\varepsilon f:\varepsilon>0)\|_2
     +\|\NN(H^e_\varepsilon f:\varepsilon>0)\|_2
 \le C(B+R)\|f\|_2,
\]
as required. The argument also bounds $\|\NN(H^b_\varepsilon f)\|_2$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:hilbert-absolute-tail}, applied to $a+e$, gives
$H^b_Tf(x)\to0$ as $T\to\infty$ at every $x$. The zero anchor implies
$|H^b_Tf(x)|\le\NN(H^b_\varepsilon f(x):\varepsilon>0)$, so dominated
convergence gives $H^b_Tf\to0$ strongly in $L^2$. Hence, for each
fixed $\varepsilon>0$ and $T\ge\varepsilon$,
\[
 \int_{\varepsilon<|y|\le T}b(|y|)f(x-y)\,\frac{dy}{y}
 =H^b_\varepsilon f(x)-H^b_Tf(x)
 \longrightarrow H^b_\varepsilon f
 \quad\hbox{strongly in }L^2.
\]
Thus the literal coordinates also retain the compatible strong-$L^2$
interpretation.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:hilbert-shellwise-sharper}
The condition $R_2(b)<\infty$ is strictly weaker than global finite
$V^2(b)$.  A bounded function may be constant on each half-open dyadic
shell, so that $R_2(b)=0$, while its jumps from shell to shell have infinite
global $2$-variation.  Thus even this basic model identifies a radial class
strictly larger than the global $V^2$ class of Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-radial}.
\end{remark}


\section{Obstructions and sharpness}
\label{sec:sharpness-obstructions}

This section records four complementary negative results.  They distinguish
the pointwise critical-jump functional from the weaker mixed quasi-seminorm,
show why the soft radial transfer cannot reach exponent two, prove that the
finite-radial theorem has no $\rho=\infty$ endpoint, and show that the output
critical jump cannot be strengthened to true $2$-variation.

\subsection{The mixed-norm exchange is false}

\begin{proposition}[Mixed-norm counterexamples for finite jump paths]
\label{prop:mixed-norm-counterexample}
For every $1\le p<\infty$ and every integer $M\ge2$ there exist a finite
measure space $X_M$ and a two-point scalar path $F$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mixed-outer-bounded}
 \sup_{\lambda>0}\|\lambda N_\lambda(F)^{1/2}\|_{L^p(X_M)}\le C_p,
\end{equation}
whereas
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mixed-inner-growth}
 \left\|\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda N_\lambda(F)^{1/2}\right\|_{L^p(X_M)}
 \simeq(\log M)^{1/p}.
\end{equation}
In addition, for every $M\ge2$ there is a scalar path $G$ on one common
finite ordered parameter set such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mixed-weak-outer-bounded}
 \sup_{\lambda>0}
 \|\lambda N_\lambda(G)^{1/2}\|_{L^{1,\infty}(X_M)}\le\sqrt2,
\end{equation}
but
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mixed-weak-inner-growth}
 \left\|\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda N_\lambda(G)^{1/2}
 \right\|_{L^{1,\infty}(X_M)}=\sqrt2\,M.
\end{equation}
Thus neither the strong nor the weak spatial norm can in general be
interchanged with the supremum in the jump amplitude.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For the strong example, take $X_M=\{1,\ldots,M\}$ with counting measure
and set $a_k=k^{-1/p}$.
On the ordered parameter set $\{0,1\}$ define $F_0(k)=0$ and $F_1(k)=a_k$.
Then $N_\lambda(F(k))=1$ for $0<\lambda<a_k$ and vanishes for
$\lambda\ge a_k$.  Thus the pointwise supremum equals $a_k$, and its
$p$th power sums to $\sum_{k\le M}k^{-1}\simeq\log M$.  For fixed
$\lambda$,
\[
 \|\lambda N_\lambda(F)^{1/2}\|_p
 =\lambda\#\{k:a_k>\lambda\}^{1/p}\le C_p,
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:mixed-outer-bounded}--
\eqref{eq:mixed-inner-growth}.

For the weak endpoint use the same counting space and put
\[
 I_M=\{0,1,\ldots,2\cdot4^M\},\qquad
 a_k=2^{-k},\qquad L_k=4^k.
\]
For $j\in I_M$ define
\[
 G_j(k)=a_k\,\mathbf1_{\{j<2L_k\}}\mathbf1_{\{j\ \mathrm{odd}\}}.
\]
Thus, at the point $k$, the path alternates
$0,a_k,0,a_k,\ldots,0$ through the first $2L_k+1$ coordinates and is
zero thereafter.  For $0<\lambda<a_k$, its $2L_k$ consecutive changes
are admissible paired strict jumps (successive pairs may share an
endpoint).  Conversely, every strict jump interval must contain one of
these changes, and ordered jump intervals cannot use the same change
twice.  Hence, exactly,
\[
 N_\lambda(G(k))=
 \begin{cases}
  2L_k,&0<\lambda<a_k,\\
  0,&\lambda\ge a_k.
 \end{cases}
\]
It follows that
\[
 \mathbf J(G)(k)=a_k\sqrt{2L_k}=\sqrt2
\]
for every $1\le k\le M$, proving
\eqref{eq:mixed-weak-inner-growth}.

Fix $\lambda>0$.  If the path is nonzero at no spatial point there is
nothing to prove.  Otherwise let $K$ be the largest integer in
$\{1,\ldots,M\}$ for which $\lambda<2^{-K}$.  The nonzero values of
$\lambda N_\lambda(G)^{1/2}$ have decreasing rearrangement
\[
 A,\frac A2,\ldots,\frac{A}{2^{K-1}},
 \qquad A=\sqrt2\,\lambda2^K<\sqrt2.
\]
Consequently
\[
 \|\lambda N_\lambda(G)^{1/2}\|_{\ell^{1,\infty}}
 =\sup_{1\le m\le K}m\frac{A}{2^{m-1}}
 \le A<\sqrt2,
\]
uniformly in $\lambda$ and $M$.  This proves
\eqref{eq:mixed-weak-outer-bounded}.
\end{proof}

The first construction shows that an outer supremum in the jump amplitude
cannot be upgraded to the pointwise functional by an abstract exchange of
strong spatial norms.  The second construction proves the same strict
failure in $L^{1,\infty}$ with a genuine jump trajectory on a common finite
parameter set.  In particular, the pointwise-inside weak endpoint in
Theorems~\ref{thm:main} and~\ref{thm:finite-radial} is genuinely stronger
than the conventional mixed weak quasi-seminorm.

\subsection{Failure of an abstract endpoint radial-variation transfer}

\begin{proposition}[Abstract endpoint transfer obstruction]
\label{prop:abstract-radial-v2-counterexample}
For every $N\ge2$ there exist a finite scalar path $F$, a scalar coefficient
path $\beta$, and the weighted-tail path
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abstract-weighted-tail}
 G_i=\sum_{k=i}^{2N-1}\beta_k(F_k-F_{k+1}),\qquad G_{2N}=0,
\end{equation}
such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abstract-input-bounds}
 \mathbf J(F)\le C,
 \qquad \|\beta\|_\infty+V^2(\beta)\le C,
\end{equation}
but
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abstract-output-growth}
 \mathbf J(G)\ge c\sqrt{\log N}.
\end{equation}
Hence no universal scalar-path estimate of the form
\[
 \mathbf J(G)\le C(\|\beta\|_\infty+V^2(\beta))\mathbf J(F)
\]
can hold for the operation \eqref{eq:abstract-weighted-tail}.
Moreover, with absolute changes in the constants, this discrete example
embeds into the same weighted-tail structural class that appears in
Proposition~\ref{prop:young-radial-transfer}, now at the formal endpoint
$\rho=2$:
there are a locally absolutely continuous path
$\widetilde F:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb C$ with
$\widetilde F_s\to0$ as $s\to\infty$ and a bounded right-continuous
$\widetilde\beta$ of finite $2$-variation such that
\[
 \widetilde G_s=-\int_s^\infty \widetilde\beta(u)\,d\widetilde F_u
\]
has the analogues of \eqref{eq:abstract-input-bounds} and
\eqref{eq:abstract-output-growth}.  Thus local absolute continuity of the
input path does not restore an abstract endpoint transfer at $\rho=2$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Fix $N\ge2$ and put
\[
 H_N=\sum_{j=1}^N\frac1j,\qquad
 a_j=j^{-1/2},\qquad \delta_j=\frac{a_j}{\sqrt{H_N}}
 \quad(1\le j\le N).
\]
Define the positive finite path
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abstract-F-definition}
 F_{2j}=0\quad(0\le j\le N),
 \qquad F_{2j-1}=a_j\quad(1\le j\le N).
\end{equation}
Take $\beta_0=0$ and prescribe
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abstract-beta-increments}
 \beta_{2j-1}-\beta_{2j-2}=\delta_j,
 \qquad \beta_{2j}-\beta_{2j-1}=-\delta_j
 \quad(1\le j\le N).
\end{equation}
Thus $\beta_{2j}=0$ and $\beta_{2j-1}=\delta_j$; in particular the
coefficient returns to zero after each positive pulse.  We have
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^N\delta_j^2=1,\qquad 0<\delta_j\le1.
\]

We construct the continuous paths at the same time.  Let
$r(v)=\min\{1,\max\{0,v\}\}$ and set, for $s\in\mathbb R$,
\[
 T_j(s)=r(s-(2j-1))-r(s-2j),\qquad
 \widetilde F(s)=\sum_{j=1}^Na_jT_j(s),
\]
\[
 \widetilde\beta(s)=\sum_{j=1}^N\delta_j\mathbf1_{[2j,2j+1)}(s).
\]
Here $T_j$ is the unit positive triangular tent, rising on
$(2j-1,2j)$ and falling on $(2j,2j+1)$, with $T_j(2j)=1$ and
$T_j(s)=0$ outside $(2j-1,2j+1)$.
The sets where the tents are nonzero are pairwise disjoint, as are the
coefficient intervals.  The finite sum $\widetilde F$ is globally
Lipschitz, hence locally absolutely continuous, and vanishes for
$s\le1$ and for $s\ge2N+1$.  The half-open convention makes
$\widetilde\beta$ right-continuous, including at the last endpoint,
and $\|\widetilde\beta\|_\infty\le1$.  At every grid point,
\[
 \widetilde F(i+1)=F_i,\qquad
 \widetilde\beta(i+1)=\beta_i\qquad(0\le i\le2N).
\]

We first bound the genuine paired jump functional of the input.
Fix any $m\ge1$ and any ordered family
$s_1<t_1\le s_2<t_2\le\cdots\le s_m<t_m$ in $\mathbb R$.
Let $U$ be the set of tent indices that are nonzero at at least one of
these $2m$ endpoints.  Since at most one tent is nonzero at any point,
$|U|\le2m$.  For a nondecreasing function with values in $[0,1]$,
the sum of its increments over these ordered intervals is at most one:
the intervening gaps have nonnegative increments, so the sum is bounded
by the increment from $s_1$ to $t_m$.  Applying this to the two ramps in
$T_j$, and then using the triangle inequality, gives
\[
 \sum_{\ell=1}^m
 |a_jT_j(t_\ell)-a_jT_j(s_\ell)|\le2a_j.
\]
Only indices in $U$ can contribute to the endpoint differences.
Moreover, any $K$ distinct heights have sum at most
$\sum_{j=1}^Kj^{-1/2}\le\int_0^Kx^{-1/2}\,dx=2\sqrt K$,
with the empty sum interpreted as zero.  Consequently
\[
 \sum_{\ell=1}^m
 |\widetilde F(t_\ell)-\widetilde F(s_\ell)|
 \le2\sum_{j\in U}a_j
 \le4\sqrt{|U|}\le8\sqrt m.
\]
If all $m$ pairs are strict $\lambda$-jumps, their left-hand side
exceeds $m\lambda$, so $\lambda\sqrt m\le8$.
Taking the supremum over all finite paired witnesses and all
$\lambda>0$ proves $\mathbf J(\widetilde F)\le8$.
This argument allows gaps and shared endpoints; it does not merely
estimate the consecutive changes of the path.  Restriction to positive
parameters and then to the grid therefore gives $\mathbf J(F)\le8$
as well.

For the coefficient, write
$p_j(s)=\delta_j\mathbf1_{[2j,2j+1)}(s)$ and take an arbitrary
variation partition $x_0<\cdots<x_L$ in $\mathbb R$.
For each fixed $j$, deleting consecutive repeated values of
$p_j(x_\ell)$ leaves a subsequence of $(0,\delta_j,0)$ and does
not change its squared increment sum.  The elementary bound
$|z-w|^2\le4(|z|^2+|w|^2)$, with each retained state counted at
most twice, thus gives the convenient non-optimal estimate
\[
 \sum_{\ell=1}^L
 |p_j(x_\ell)-p_j(x_{\ell-1})|^2\le8\delta_j^2.
\]
At any pair of endpoints at most two pulses have a nonzero
increment.  Cauchy--Schwarz on those at most two terms yields
\[
 \begin{split}
 \sum_{\ell=1}^L
 |\widetilde\beta(x_\ell)-\widetilde\beta(x_{\ell-1})|^2
 &\le2\sum_{j=1}^N\sum_{\ell=1}^L
 |p_j(x_\ell)-p_j(x_{\ell-1})|^2\\
 &\le16\sum_{j=1}^N\delta_j^2=16.
 \end{split}
\]
Taking the supremum over all partitions proves the true variation
bound $V^2(\widetilde\beta)\le4$, not just a bound on the sum of
squares of its consecutive jumps.  Hence
\[
 \|\widetilde\beta\|_\infty+V^2(\widetilde\beta)\le5,
 \qquad \|\beta\|_\infty+V^2(\beta)\le5,
\]
where the second estimate follows by sampling.  These estimates remain
valid on $(0,\infty)$.

Define $G$ by \eqref{eq:abstract-weighted-tail}.  Its even-edge terms
vanish, and every odd-edge term at $k=2j-1$ equals $\delta_ja_j$.
Thus
\[
 G_0=\sum_{j=1}^N\delta_ja_j=\sqrt{H_N},\qquad G_{2N}=0.
\]
For every $0<\lambda<\sqrt{H_N}$ these two endpoints form one
strict jump.  Taking the supremum in $\lambda$, and using
$H_N\ge\log(N+1)\ge\log N$, gives
\[
 \mathbf J(G)\ge\sqrt{H_N}\ge\sqrt{\log N}.
\]

Finally define the continuous output by the actual derivative integral
\[
 \widetilde G(s)
 =-\int_{(s,\infty)}\widetilde\beta(u)\widetilde F'(u)\,du
 =-\int_s^\infty\widetilde\beta(u)\,d\widetilde F_u.
\]
The second equality uses the absolute continuity of $\widetilde F$.
On each falling open interval $(2j,2j+1)$ its derivative is $-a_j$;
off the falling intervals the coefficient is zero, apart from the
finitely many irrelevant endpoints.  Hence, almost everywhere,
\[
 \widetilde\beta(u)\widetilde F'(u)
 =-\sum_{j=1}^N\delta_ja_j\mathbf1_{[2j,2j+1)}(u).
\]
In particular this product is globally integrable, so the displayed
tail is well defined and is zero for $s\ge2N+1$.
On every half-open unit interval $[k+1,k+2)$ the coefficient equals
$\beta_k$, while absolute continuity gives
$\int_{k+1}^{k+2}\widetilde F'(u)\,du=F_{k+1}-F_k$.
Splitting the tail into these unit intervals therefore proves, for
every $0\le i\le2N$, the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abstract-continuous-embedding}
 \begin{split}
 \widetilde G(i+1)
 &=-\int_{i+1}^\infty\widetilde\beta(s)\,d\widetilde F_s\\
 &=\sum_{k=i}^{2N-1}\beta_k(F_k-F_{k+1})=G_i.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
In particular $\widetilde G(1)=\sqrt{H_N}$ and
$\widetilde G(2N+1)=0$, so the same single-pair argument yields
$\mathbf J(\widetilde G)\ge\sqrt{\log N}$ on $(0,\infty)$.
Together with the input bounds $8$ and $5$, these are all the
asserted discrete and continuous estimates, with the zero far anchor.
A universal transfer constant $C$ in either class would imply
$\sqrt{\log N}\le40C$ for every $N\ge2$, which is impossible.
\end{proof}

This obstruction is the sequence-space failure
$\ell^{2,\infty}\cdot\ell^2\not\subset\ell^1$.  Proposition~\ref{prop:hilbert-radial-v2}
and the general argument of Section~\ref{sec:finite-radial} escape it by
using Fourier and packet orthogonality absent from an arbitrary scalar path.

\subsection{Failure at the bounded-radial endpoint}

For the limiting seminorm put
$V^\infty(b)=\sup_{s<t}|b(t)-b(s)|$.

\begin{proposition}[No uniform merely bounded radial theory]
\label{prop:radial-infinity-counterexample}
There is no uniform $L^2$ bound for the one-dimensional radial Hilbert
operator in terms of $\|b\|_\infty+V^\infty(b)$ alone.  Consequently,
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-radial} cannot be extended to $\rho=\infty$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $N\ge1$ put
\[
 R_N=(N+1)\pi,\qquad
 b_N(r)=\mathbf1_{[\pi,R_N]}(r)\sin r,\qquad
 f_N(x)=\mathbf1_{[-2R_N,2R_N]}(x)e^{ix}.
\]
The amplitude $b_N$ is continuous, since its sine factor vanishes at
both endpoints of its support.  Moreover,
$\|b_N\|_\infty\le1$ and $V^\infty(b_N)\le2$, while
$b_N(|y|)/y$ is integrable.  Set
\[
 T_Nf(x)=\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{b_N(|y|)}y f(x-y)\,dy,
 \qquad A_N=\int_\pi^{R_N}\frac{\sin^2r}{r}\,dr.
\]
For each $k=0,\ldots,N-1$,
\[
 \int_{(k+1)\pi}^{(k+2)\pi}\frac{\sin^2r}{r}\,dr
 \ge\frac1{(k+2)\pi}
       \int_{(k+1)\pi}^{(k+2)\pi}\sin^2r\,dr
 =\frac1{2(k+2)}.
\]
Consequently $A_N\ge\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}[2(k+2)]^{-1}\to\infty$.
If $|x|\le R_N$ and $b_N(|y|)\ne0$, then
$|x-y|\le2R_N$.  Splitting the absolutely convergent integral into
positive and negative $y$ therefore gives the exact identity
\[
 T_Nf_N(x)
 =e^{ix}\int_\pi^{R_N}\frac{\sin r}{r}
       (e^{-ir}-e^{ir})\,dr
 =-2i e^{ix}A_N,\qquad |x|\le R_N.
\]
Thus
\[
 \|f_N\|_2=\sqrt{4R_N},\qquad
 \|T_Nf_N\|_2\ge2A_N\sqrt{2R_N},\qquad
 \frac{\|T_Nf_N\|_2}{\|f_N\|_2}\ge\sqrt2\,A_N\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
This contradicts any uniform bound with radial factor
$\|b_N\|_\infty+V^\infty(b_N)\le3$.

Finally, take $n=1$, $P=0$, and $\Omega(1)=1$, $\Omega(-1)=-1$,
so that $K_\Omega(y)=1/y$.  Write
$T^{b_N}_\varepsilon=T^{0,b_N}_{\Omega,\varepsilon}$.
Since $1<\pi$ and $b_N$ vanishes beyond $R_N$, the original sharp
truncations satisfy
\[
 T^{b_N}_1f_N=T_Nf_N,\qquad T^{b_N}_{R_N+1}f_N=0.
\]
At every $x$, these two parameters witness a strict $\lambda$-jump
whenever $0<\lambda<|T_Nf_N(x)|$.  Taking the supremum over such
$\lambda$ yields
\[
 |T_Nf_N(x)|\le
 \mathbf J(T^{b_N}_\varepsilon f_N(x):\varepsilon>0).
\]
Hence the same diverging $L^2$ norm ratio rules out the
$\rho=\infty$ critical-jump extension of
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-radial}, already at zero phase with this fixed
angular datum.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Failure of quadratic output variation for sharp Hilbert truncations}

Let
\[
 H_\varepsilon f(x)=\int_{|y|>\varepsilon}f(x-y)\,\frac{dy}{y},
 \qquad
 \widehat{P_sf}(\xi)=e^{-s|\xi|}\widehat f(\xi).
\]
For $u,t>0$ define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mellin-definitions}
 \Phi(u)=\int_u^\infty\frac{\sin v}{v}\,dv,
 \qquad
 K(t)=\frac1\pi\operatorname{sech}^2(\log t)
 =\frac{4t^2}{\pi(1+t^2)^2}.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Mellin identity]
\label{lem:mellin-hilbert-poisson}
For every $s>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mellin-identity}
 e^{-s}=\int_0^\infty K(t)\Phi(s/t)\,\frac{dt}{t},
 \qquad
 \int_0^\infty K(t)\,\frac{dt}{t}=\frac2\pi.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $\rho(t)=K(t)/t=4t/[\pi(1+t^2)^2]$.  All sine-tail integrals
in this proof are improper limits, not totalized Lebesgue integrals.
Integration by parts gives, for $u>0$,
\[
 \Phi(u)=\frac{\cos u}{u}-\int_u^\infty\frac{\cos v}{v^2}\,dv,
 \qquad |\Phi(u)|\le\frac2u,
 \qquad \Phi'(u)=-\frac{\sin u}{u}.
\]
The remainder integral is absolutely convergent; the displayed formula
also proves the existence of the original improper sine tail.  The
primitive $-2/[\pi(1+t^2)]$ gives
\[
 \int_a^\infty\rho(t)\,dt=\frac{2}{\pi(1+a^2)}\quad(a\ge0).
\]
In particular the asserted mass is $2/\pi$.  Moreover
$M:=\int_0^\infty t\rho(t)\,dt<\infty$, since
$t^2/(1+t^2)^2\le(1+t^2)^{-1}$.

Let $I(s)=\int_0^\infty\rho(t)\Phi(s/t)\,dt$.  The bound
$|\rho(t)\Phi(s/t)|\le(2/s)t\rho(t)$ proves absolute convergence and
$|I(s)|\le2M/s$, hence $I(s)\to0$ as $s\to\infty$.
For $s$ in a neighborhood of any $s_0>0$, say $s>s_0/2$,
\[
 \left|\partial_s\{\rho(t)\Phi(s/t)\}\right|
 =\frac{\rho(t)}s|\sin(s/t)|\le\frac{2}{s_0}\rho(t).
\]
Differentiation under the integral is therefore justified by an
integrable local majorant.  The substitution $t=1/y$ preserves
$\rho(t)\,dt$, and gives
\[
 I'(s)=-\frac1s\int_0^\infty\rho(y)\sin(sy)\,dy.
\]
For completeness, the required Cauchy cosine integral follows from
Fourier inversion, without a contour-integral assertion.  In cyclic
frequency normalization, elementary half-line exponential integration
gives
\[
 \mathcal F^{-1}(e^{-a|\xi|})(x)
 =\frac1{a-2\pi ix}+\frac1{a+2\pi ix}
 =\frac{2a}{a^2+(2\pi x)^2}\qquad(a>0).
\]
Both sides are integrable.  Fourier inversion at $a=2\pi$, followed
by taking real parts and using evenness, yields
\[
 \int_0^\infty\frac{\cos(sy)}{1+y^2}\,dy=\frac\pi2e^{-s}
 \qquad(s>0).
\]
Now integrate the derivative of $\sin(sy)/(1+y^2)$ on $(0,\infty)$.
Both derivative terms are absolutely integrable, and the primitive
vanishes at both endpoints, so
\[
 0=s\int_0^\infty\frac{\cos(sy)}{1+y^2}\,dy
   -2\int_0^\infty\frac{y\sin(sy)}{(1+y^2)^2}\,dy.
\]
Thus $\int_0^\infty\rho(y)\sin(sy)\,dy=se^{-s}$ and
$I'(s)=-e^{-s}$.  The difference $I(s)-e^{-s}$ is constant on
$(0,\infty)$, and its limit at infinity is zero.  Therefore
$I(s)=e^{-s}$, as required.
\end{proof}

The following pointwise representation will be justified by an
absolutely convergent physical-space Fubini calculation in the proof
below.  In angular frequency normalization, for Schwartz functions
with Fourier support compactly contained in $(0,\infty)$, it reads
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-hilbert-representation}
 P_sf(x)=\frac i2\int_0^\infty K(t)H_{s/t}f(x)\,\frac{dt}{t}
 \qquad(s>0,\ x\in\mathbb R).
\end{equation}
In cyclic frequency normalization the left-hand side is $P_{2\pi s}f(x)$;
this fixed positive reparametrization does not alter the variation
comparison.  The integral is absolutely convergent for every $x$, not
merely a representative of an $L^2$ Bochner integral.

\begin{proposition}[Poisson variation dominated by Hilbert variation on the core]
\label{prop:poisson-by-hilbert}
If $f\in\mathcal S(\mathbb R)$ and
$\operatorname{supp}\widehat f\Subset(0,\infty)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-by-hilbert}
 V^2(P_sf:s>0)
 \le\frac1\pi V^2(H_\varepsilon f:\varepsilon>0)
\end{equation}
pointwise.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We establish an everywhere physical-space identity, so no choice of
$L^2$ representatives or countable exceptional set is needed.  Write
$\rho(t)=K(t)/t$ and define the conjugate-Poisson convolution
\[
 Q_af(x)=\frac i\pi\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{y}{a^2+y^2}f(x-y)\,dy
 \qquad(a>0).
\]
For every $f\in L^1(\mathbb R)$ and every $x\in\mathbb R$, Tonelli gives
\begin{align*}
 &\int_0^\infty\rho(t)\int_{|y|>a/t}\frac{|f(x-y)|}{|y|}\,dy\,dt\\
 &\qquad=\frac2\pi\int_{\mathbb R}
       \frac{|y|}{a^2+y^2}|f(x-y)|\,dy
 \le\frac{\|f\|_1}{\pi a}<\infty.
\end{align*}
Indeed, the inner $t$-integral is the exact tail mass
$\int_{a/|y|}^\infty\rho(t)\,dt
 =2y^2/[\pi(a^2+y^2)]$ for $y\ne0$; the zero point contributes
nothing.  Ordinary absolutely convergent Fubini therefore proves,
for every $a,x$,
\[
 Q_af(x)=\frac i2\int_0^\infty\rho(t)H_{a/t}f(x)\,dt.
\]

Elementary inverse Fourier transformation of the signed exponential
multiplier gives, in cyclic normalization,
\[
 \mathcal F^{-1}\!\left(
 e^{-2\pi a\xi}\mathbf1_{\{\xi>0\}}
 -e^{2\pi a\xi}\mathbf1_{\{\xi\le0\}}\right)(y)
 =\frac{iy}{\pi(a^2+y^2)}.
\]
The multiplier is integrable.  Fubini for its inverse Fourier integral
and the $L^1$ function $f$ proves the convolution--multiplier identity
at every $x$.  Since $\widehat f$ vanishes on nonpositive frequencies,
this signed multiplier times $\widehat f$ is
$e^{-2\pi a|\xi|}\widehat f(\xi)$.  Consequently
$Q_af=P_{2\pi a}f$ in cyclic normalization, equivalently $Q_af=P_af$
in angular normalization.  A fixed positive rescaling of the Poisson
parameter does not increase its variation (and in fact preserves it).

Fix $x$ and a finite increasing partition $0<a_0<\cdots<a_J$.
The everywhere identity above and the triangle inequality for the
Bochner integral with values in $\ell^2_J$ give
\begin{align*}
 \left(\sum_{j=1}^J|Q_{a_j}f(x)-Q_{a_{j-1}}f(x)|^2\right)^{1/2}
 &\le\frac12\int_0^\infty\rho(t)
 \left(\sum_{j=1}^J|H_{a_j/t}f(x)-H_{a_{j-1}/t}f(x)|^2\right)^{1/2}dt\\
 &\le\frac12\left(\int_0^\infty\rho(t)\,dt\right)
       V^2(H_\varepsilon f(x):\varepsilon>0)\\
 &=\frac1\pi V^2(H_\varepsilon f(x):\varepsilon>0).
\end{align*}
Taking the supremum over all finite partitions proves the asserted
inequality at this arbitrary $x$.  The normalization is that of the
kernel $1/y$, not $1/(\pi y)$.
\end{proof}

We record the finite-dimensional form of the restriction principle of
de Leeuw \cite{deLeeuw1965} used below, including its elementary proof.

\begin{lemma}[Finite-dimensional de Leeuw restriction]
\label{lem:finite-dimensional-deleeuw}
Let $E$ be a finite-dimensional Banach space and let $M:\mathbb R\to E$ be
continuous.  If
\[
 \left\|\mathcal F^{-1}(M\widehat f)\right\|_{L^2(\mathbb R;E)}
 \le C\|f\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}
\]
for Schwartz $f$, then for every trigonometric polynomial $g$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:deleeuw-vector-restriction}
 \left\|\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}M(n)\widehat g(n)e^{inx}
 \right\|_{L^2(\mathbb T;E)}
 \le C\|g\|_{L^2(\mathbb T)}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We give the restriction argument with an auxiliary Haar phase, so that
periodic averaging is exact rather than asymptotic.  Write
\[
 \mathscr F f(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb R}e^{-2\pi i x\xi}f(x)\,dx,
 \qquad N(\xi)=M(2\pi\xi).
\]
The angular-frequency inverse has its factor $(2\pi)^{-1}$, and the
change of variables $\xi=2\pi\zeta$ gives the exact identity
\[
 \mathcal F^{-1}(M\widehat f)
 =\mathscr F^{-1}(N\mathscr F f).
\]
Thus the assumed Euclidean bound has the same constant $C$ in the
cyclic-frequency convention.  Let $\nu$ be Haar probability measure on
$\mathbb T=\mathbb R/(2\pi\mathbb Z)$, and write
\[
 g(x)=\sum_{n\in\Lambda}c_ne^{inx},\qquad
 G(x)=\sum_{n\in\Lambda}c_nM(n)e^{inx},\qquad
 \lambda_n=\frac{n}{2\pi},
\]
where $\Lambda\subset\mathbb Z$ is finite.  Orthogonality of the
characters gives $c_n=\widehat g(n)$ on $\Lambda$ and zero Fourier
coefficients outside $\Lambda$.

Choose a linear isomorphism $L:E\to\mathbb C^m$, with the Euclidean norm
on $\mathbb C^m$.  Coordinatewise scalar Plancherel, and commutation of
fixed continuous linear maps with the Fourier integral, give
\[
 \|\mathscr F^{-1}H\|_{L^2(\mathbb R;E)}
 \le K_E\|H\|_{L^2(\mathbb R;E)},\qquad
 K_E=\|L^{-1}\|\,\|L\|,
 \quad H\in L^1(\mathbb R;E)\cap L^2(\mathbb R;E).
\]
No Hilbert-space norm is imposed on $E$; this comparison will be used
only for an error tending to zero.

Fix $\epsilon>0$.  Continuity at the finitely many frequencies
$\lambda_n$ gives $r>0$ such that
\[
 \|N(\lambda_n+\zeta)-N(\lambda_n)\|_E\le\epsilon
 \quad(n\in\Lambda,\ |\zeta|<r).
\]
Choose a nonzero compactly supported scalar Schwartz function $\phi$
that vanishes for $|\zeta|\ge r$, and put
$\eta=\mathscr F^{-1}\phi$.  For each $n\in\Lambda$, set
\[
 h_n(\zeta)=\phi(\zeta)
   [N(\lambda_n+\zeta)-N(\lambda_n)],
 \qquad u_n=\mathscr F^{-1}h_n.
\]
These frequency errors are continuous and compactly supported, hence
belong to $L^1\cap L^2$, and
\[
 \|u_n\|_{L^2(\mathbb R;E)}
 \le K_E\epsilon\|\phi\|_2.
\]
For $a\in\mathbb T$, use the Schwartz test function
\[
 f_a(x)=\eta(x)g(a+x),\qquad
 \mathscr F f_a(\xi)
 =\sum_{n\in\Lambda}c_ne^{ina}\phi(\xi-\lambda_n).
\]
In particular every tested Fourier transform is compactly supported;
no growth assumption on $M$ is needed.  Frequency translation and
linearity of the actual integrals give
\[
 \mathscr F^{-1}(N\mathscr F f_a)(x)
 =\eta(x)G(a+x)+R(a,x),\qquad
 R(a,x)=\sum_{n\in\Lambda}c_ne^{in(a+x)}u_n(x).
\]
The functions in this identity are jointly continuous.  Since
$\nu(\mathbb T)=1$ and each character is unimodular, the finite-sum
triangle inequality yields
\[
 \|R\|_{L^2(\nu\otimes dx;E)}
 \le K_E\epsilon\|\phi\|_2\sum_{n\in\Lambda}|c_n|.
\]
For every continuous $E$-valued periodic function $A$, Tonelli and Haar
translation invariance give the exact factorization
\[
 \|\eta(x)A(a+x)\|_{L^2(\nu\otimes dx;E)}
 =\|\eta\|_2\|A\|_{L^2(\nu;E)}.
\]
Apply the assumed Euclidean inequality to each $f_a$ and integrate its
square with respect to $\nu(a)$.  The preceding identity, applied to
$g$ and $G$, and the error bound imply
\[
 \|\eta\|_2\|G\|_{L^2(\nu;E)}
 \le C\|\eta\|_2\|g\|_{L^2(\nu)}
   +K_E\epsilon\|\phi\|_2\sum_{n\in\Lambda}|c_n|.
\]
Scalar Plancherel gives $\|\eta\|_2=\|\phi\|_2>0$.  Cancel this common
factor and let $\epsilon\downarrow0$.  The result is
$\|G\|_{L^2(\nu;E)}\le C\|g\|_{L^2(\nu)}$.
Passing from $\nu$ to ordinary torus volume multiplies both norms by
$(2\pi)^{1/2}$, so the constant remains exactly $C$.  This proves
\eqref{eq:deleeuw-vector-restriction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Failure of true $V^2$ for sharp Hilbert truncations]
\label{thm:hilbert-v2-failure}
There is no constant $C$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hilbert-v2-false}
 \|V^2(H_\varepsilon f:\varepsilon>0)\|_2\le C\|f\|_2
\end{equation}
for every $f\in L^2(\mathbb R)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Assume \eqref{eq:hilbert-v2-false}, with constant $C_H\ge0$.
We first give an elementary finite Gaussian obstruction.  Let $Z_0$ be a
standard real Gaussian and $Q_0=Z_0^2$.  On the product of two independent
copies of the preceding probability space set
\[
 Z_{n+1}=\frac{Z_n'+Z_n''}{\sqrt2},\qquad
 Q_{n+1}=\max\!\left\{Z_{n+1}^2,\frac{Q_n'+Q_n''}{2}\right\}.
\]
Each $Z_n$ is again standard Gaussian.  Fix $a\ge0$, and recursively put
\[
 U_0=\mathbf1_{\{|Z_0|\le a\}},\qquad
 U_{n+1}=\mathbf1_{\{|Z_{n+1}|\le a\}}
             \frac{U_n'+U_n''}{2}.
\]
Induction gives $0\le U_n\le1$, $U_n=0$ when $|Z_n|>a$, and
$Q_n\ge a^2(1-U_n)$.  If $|Z_{n+1}|\le a$ and $U_n'>0$, then
$|Z_n''|\le(\sqrt2+1)a\le3a$.  The symmetric assertion holds with the
children exchanged.  Consequently, writing
$q_a=\mathbb P\{|Z_0|\le3a\}<1$, independence gives
\[
 \mathbb E U_{n+1}\le q_a\,\mathbb E U_n,\qquad
 \mathbb E Q_n\ge a^2(1-q_a^n).
\]
The strict inequality $q_a<1$ follows directly from the strictly positive
Gaussian density on the complement of the bounded interval.  All these
random variables are integrable (use the recursion and Gaussian second
moments).  Taking first $a$ and then $n$ proves
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-gaussian-dyadic-unbounded}
 \sup_n\mathbb E Q_n=\infty,
 \qquad \mathbb E Z_n^2=1.
\end{equation}

Here is the literal path statistic represented by $Q_n$.  For $d=2^n$
independent standard Gaussians $X_0,\ldots,X_{d-1}$, let
$S_l=\sum_{j<l}X_j$ ($0\le l\le d$).  The largest sum of squared block
increments over all dyadic prunings of $[0,d]$ is exactly $dQ_n$.
Indeed, a pruning either keeps the root interval or prunes both children,
which is precisely the displayed recursion.  In particular, this is a
lower bound for the square of genuine strong quadratic variation.

For each $d$ let $\mathcal W_d$ be the finite set of all ordered families
$([a_j,b_j])_{j=0}^{d-1}$ of intervals with endpoints in
$\{0,\ldots,d\}$, with $a_j\le b_j$ for $j<d$ and $b_j\le a_{j+1}$ for
$j+1<d$; degenerate intervals are allowed.  The ordinary finite-dimensional Banach space
\[
 E_d=\prod_{w\in\mathcal W_d}\ell^2_d(\mathbb C)
\]
is given its maximum norm.  Define the complex linear map
\[
 L_dz=\bigl((z_{b_j}-z_{a_j})_j\bigr)_{w\in\mathcal W_d}.
\]
It kills constant paths and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-D-coordinate-control}
 \|L_dz\|\le V^2(z_l:0\le l\le d).
\end{equation}
Padding each dyadic pruning by degenerate terminal intervals gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gaussian-finite-Banach-lower}
 \mathbb E\|L_d(S_l)_l\|^2\ge d\,\mathbb E Q_n,
 \qquad \mathbb E|S_d|^2=d.
\end{equation}

We transfer the assumed Hilbert estimate to these finite path maps.
The physical-space Mellin identity and integral Minkowski give, for every
integrable $f$, the all-frequency conjugate-Poisson inequality
\begin{equation}\label{eq:positive-core-poisson-v2}
 V^2(Q_sf:s>0)\le\pi^{-1}V^2(H_\varepsilon f:\varepsilon>0),
 \qquad Q_sf=\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}
              \frac{y}{s^2+y^2}f(\,\cdot-y\,)\,dy.
\end{equation}
In the cyclic Fourier convention its multiplier is
$\sigma(\xi)e^{-2\pi s|\xi|}$ almost everywhere, where
$\sigma(\xi)=1$ for $\xi>0$ and $-1$ otherwise.  Fix positive strictly
decreasing times $t_0,\ldots,t_d$.  On Schwartz inputs the assumed bound
therefore, using conjugate-Poisson parameters $t_l/(2\pi)$, yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:positive-finite-D-poisson-v2}
 \|\mathscr F^{-1}(M\mathscr F f)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R;E_d)}
 \le \frac{C_H}{\pi}\|f\|_2,
 \qquad M(\xi)=\sigma(\xi)L_d\bigl((e^{-t_l|\xi|})_l\bigr),
\end{equation}
where $\mathscr F$ denotes the cyclic Fourier transform.
This symbol is continuous, including at zero: subtract the constant
vector inside $L_d$ and use $L_d\mathbf1=0$ and $|\sigma|=1$.
We use the unit circle $\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$, its characters
$e^{2\pi ikx}$, and normalized Haar measure.  The cyclic-frequency form
of Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-dimensional-deleeuw}, obtained by rescaling its
$2\pi$-periodic formulation, applies with the same constant.
On this circle replace every Fourier coefficient $c_k$ by
$\sigma(k)c_k$.  Parseval leaves the scalar $L^2$ norm unchanged, while
$\sigma(k)^2=1$ removes the sign from the output.  Thus, for every
trigonometric polynomial $g$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:full-finite-D-poisson-v2}
 \bigl\|L_d((P_{t_l}g)_l)\bigr\|_{L^2(\mathbb T;E_d)}
 \le \frac{C_H}{\pi}\|g\|_{L^2(\mathbb T)},
 \qquad \widehat{P_tg}(k)=e^{-t|k|}\widehat g(k),
\end{equation}
uniformly in the finite positive time set and in $d$.
No spectral-density passage is needed here.

Let $p$ be any zero-mean trigonometric polynomial.  On the product of $d$
independent circles use phase variables $a=(a_j)_{j<d}$ and, for integers $M\ge1$, set
\[
 N_j=(M+1)^{2j+1},\qquad t_l=(M+1)^{-2l},\qquad
 g_{M,a}(x)=\sum_{j<d}p(a_j+N_jx).
\]
Apply \eqref{eq:full-finite-D-poisson-v2}, square, and
integrate in $a$.  For every $x$, translation
$a_j\mapsto a_j+N_jx$ preserves product Haar measure.  Thus the scalar
right side is exactly $(C_H/\pi)^2\mathbb E|\sum_jp(a_j)|^2$, and the
left side equals the squared product-Haar norm of
\[
 \sum_{j<d}\sum_{k\ne0}\widehat p(k)e^{2\pi ika_j}
 L_d\bigl((e^{-t_l|kN_j|})_l\bigr).
\]
As $M\to\infty$, its multiplier tends to $1_{j<l}$ for every fixed
$j,k,l$ with $k\ne0$.  The sums are finite, so Fatou's lemma gives
\[
 \left\|L_d\left(\left(\sum_{j<l}p(a_j)\right)_l\right)\right\|_2
 \le\frac{C_H}{\pi}\left\|\sum_{j<d}p(a_j)\right\|_2.
\]
Realize one standard Gaussian as a measurable function of one Haar
circle.  It belongs to $L^2$ and has mean zero.  Finite Fourier sums of this function,
with the zero coefficient omitted, approximate it in complex $L^2$.
For $e_j=(\mathbf1_{j<l})_{l=0}^d$, coordinate pullback is an $L^2$
isometry, so the triangle inequality gives, for every scalar $h\in L^2(\mathbb T)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-D-L2-continuity}
 \left\|L_d\left(\left(\sum_{j<l}h(a_j)\right)_l\right)\right\|_2
 \le \left(\sum_{j<d}\|L_de_j\|\right)\|h\|_2,
 \qquad \left\|\sum_{j<d}h(a_j)\right\|_2\le d\|h\|_2.
\end{equation}
Both maps are thus bounded linear maps for each fixed $d$; their
continuity bounds need not be uniform in $d$.  Hence the preceding
inequality passes to the actual Gaussian function.  Product
measure preservation now gives, for independent standard Gaussian leaves,
\[
 \mathbb E\|L_d(S_l)_l\|^2
 \le (C_H/\pi)^2\mathbb E|S_d|^2.
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:gaussian-finite-Banach-lower} this implies
$\mathbb E Q_n\le(C_H/\pi)^2$ for every $n$, contradicting
\eqref{eq:finite-gaussian-dyadic-unbounded}.  Thus no asserted Hilbert
constant exists.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Sharpness of the output fluctuation exponent]
The failure already occurs for $n=1$, $P=0$, $b\equiv1$, and the odd kernel
on $\mathbb S^0$.  Thus the pointwise critical jump $\mathbf J(F)$---the
paired/chain weak-$\ell^2$ increment functional, up to the absolute
comparison constants in \eqref{eq:chain-pair-functional}---cannot be
replaced in the main theorems by the true strong variation $V^2(F)$.
\end{remark}



\section{Remarks on earlier proofs}\label{sec:earlier-proofs}
\begin{remark}[Independence from prior proofs]\label{rem:proof-status}
Our proofs do not use the coefficient-detection and annular-decay
arguments in
\cite{AlSalman,CSS,JiangLu,LuWu03,LuWu04,LuZhang} or the endpoint
fluctuation arguments in \cite{DHL,BhojakShrivastava}.  The observations in
this remark concern the cited proofs as written, not the truth of their
theorem statements.  The discussion below refers to the preprint versions specified in
the references.

In the preprint arXiv:2303.00594v1 corresponding to \cite{CSS},
the relevant coefficient-detection step follows the one-ray reduction of
Lu--Zhang \cite[pp.~210--213]{LuZhang}, which \cite[Lemma~3.2]{CSS}
explicitly adapts.  For the top bidegree block
\[
 P_{m,\ell}(x,y)
 =\sum_{\substack{|\alpha|=m\\|\beta|=\ell}}
   a_{\alpha,\beta}x^\alpha y^\beta,
\]
that reduction detects the coefficient tensor only through its diagonal
restriction
\[
 A_{m,\ell}(\theta)=P_{m,\ell}(\theta,\theta)
 =\sum_{\substack{|\alpha|=m\\|\beta|=\ell}}
   a_{\alpha,\beta}\theta^{\alpha+\beta}.
\]
In the genuinely mixed setting $n\ge2$ and $m,\ell\ge1$, the map
$P_{m,\ell}\mapsto A_{m,\ell}$ is in general not injective.  For example,
the alternating bilinear phase $P(x,y)=x_1y_2-x_2y_1$ has a nonzero top
bidegree block, but $A_{1,1}\equiv0$.  Thus the nonzero top-bidegree
hypothesis in Lemma~3.2 does not supply the nonvanishing needed to apply
Corollary~3.4 in the passage to (4.7).
The related annular-decay reduction is invoked by Lu--Wu
\cite[p.~54, (3.1)]{LuWu03} and \cite[p.~308, Lemma~4]{LuWu04},
both referring to the argument of Lu--Zhang.  The global phase flag used here avoids this
diagonal compression and retains the full mixed coefficient data.

In the preprint version of \cite{DHL}, the squared-multiplier geometric
tail preceding (2.16)
carries a factor comparable to $m$ that is not retained in the displayed
estimate; after the Plancherel square root, the operator-norm estimate
(2.16) is therefore missing a factor comparable to $\sqrt m$.
Independently, Lemma~2.11 is false as stated for $0<\theta<1$: as
$t\downarrow0$, the ratio of its left-hand side to its right-hand side is
comparable to $t^{\theta-1}$.  The lemma is used in precisely this range to
reduce a loss of order $m^{2-\theta}$ to the $m$-weighted angular
summability available at the $L\log L$ endpoint.  Consequently, that
proof does not justify the endpoint summation in its displayed form.

In version~3 of \cite[\S2.2.2]{BhojakShrivastava}, the displayed
fixed-shift families
$\mathcal D_u=\{2^j([0,1)^n+m+u):j\in\mathbb Z,\ m\in\mathbb Z^n\}$
are not nested, although the subsequent stopping argument uses
laminarity. For $n=1$ and $u=1/3$, the intervals $[1/3,4/3)$ and
$[2/3,8/3)$ belong to the stated family and overlap without containment.
A genuinely nested grid or another stopping argument is required.
The distinct output-selector issue in the short-jump argument of
version~2 is no longer an objection to the replacement argument in
version~3. Neither observation is a counterexample to the endpoint
theorem itself.
\end{remark}


\clearpage
